# HYBRIDITY, CONFLICT, AND THE GLOBAL POLITICS OF CYBERSECURITY

**Digital Technologies and Global Politics**

Series Editors:

Andrea Calderaro and Madeline Carr

While other disciplines like law, sociology, and computer science have engaged closely with the Information Age, international relations scholars have yet to bring the full analytic power of their discipline to developing our understanding of what new digital technologies mean for concepts like war, peace, security, cooperation, human rights, equity, and power. This series brings together the latest research from international relations scholars—particularly those working across disciplines—to challenge and extend our understanding of world politics in the Information Age.

*Governing Cyberspace: Behaviour, Power and Diplomacy*

Edited by Dennis Broeders and Bibi van den Berg

*Internet Diplomacy: Shaping the Global Politics of Cyberspace*

Edited by Meryem Marzouki & Andrea Calderaro

*The New Knowledge: Information, Data and the Remaking of Global Power* by Blayne Haggart and Natasha Tusikov

*Hybridity, Conflict, and the Global Politics of Cybersecurity*

Edited by Fabio Cristiano and Bibi van den Berg

*The Emergence of China’s Smart State*

Edited by Rogier Creemers, Straton Papagianneas, and Adam Knight

# HYBRIDITY, CONFLICT, AND THE GLOBAL POLITICS OF CYBERSECURITY

Edited by

Fabio Cristiano and Bibi van den Berg

# [Acknowledgements](../Text/nav.xhtml#link1)

This edited volume originates from the 2020 conference of The Hague Program for Cyber Norms titled ‘Moving Forward: Fragmentation, Polarization, and Hybridity’ which, because of the COVID-19 pandemic, was held online on 10–12 November 2020. We are immensely grateful to the participants and the keynote speakers—Madeline Carr, Milton Mueller, Bruce Schneier and Mariarosaria Taddeo—for engaging and inspiring conversations during the conference. We would also like to thank our fellow colleagues at the Institute of Security and Global Affairs, Leiden University, who have provided invaluable feedback on the conference submissions in the initial phase: Dennis Broeders, Monica Kaminska, Els de Busser, James Shires and Tatiana Tropina. We would also like to thank Maaike Stroeks for the editorial support provided for this volume during her internship with The Hague Program. A special word of thanks goes to our colleague Corianne Oosterbaan for her fantastic work with the organization of the conference despite the difficult times, and for her crucial work throughout the editorial process. Finally, we express our gratitude to the Dutch Ministry of Foreign Affairs for their generous and continuous financial support to The Hague Program.

Fabio Cristiano and Bibi van den Berg  
The Hague, July 2022

 

[**1**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp01)

# [**Hybridity and Conflict in Cyberspace** *Rethinking the Emergent Character of International Cybersecurity*](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp01)

*Fabio Cristiano and Bibi van den Berg*

**C**YBERSECURITY HAS DEVELOPED into a crucial issue for national security and international diplomacy. While being defined by an emergent and intrinsic technical character, cybersecurity also represents an important ‘added variable for power politics’ (Bellanova, Jacobsen and Monsees 2020). States and the international community have come a long way in terms of strategic thinking and meaningful policy-making in cybersecurity, for example through the promotion of normative frameworks of responsible (state) behaviour at the international level (Broeders and van den Berg 2020). The recently renewed mandate for the United Nations Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) on international cybersecurity represents an important development in this regard. Established in 2018 by the UN General Assembly, the OEWG is open to all UN member states and allows, to a limited extent, the participation of non-governmental organizations, industry, and academia. However, normative developments at the UN seem to lag behind the fast-paced technological developments and the ever-changing threat landscape in cyberspace (Broeders et al. 2022). They are also not immune to raising geopolitical competition, with the ongoing proliferation of technonationalisms pushing the UN normative process toward further polarisation and fragmentation (Calderaro 2022). In particular, the normative debate continues to be polarized between the so-called like-minded states on one hand, and countries holding more authoritarian views on the internet and its security on the other. Because of this polarization, the UN process on cyber norms has achieved little regarding the regulation of emerging threats and technologies—such as disinformation, AI-enabled cyber operations, and so on (Cristiano et al. 2023a). When considering the fact that state-sponsored cyber operations are on the rise, it can be argued that—despite normative developments at all governance levels—the present and the future of cyberspace as open, free, and secure are at risk today like never before.

This edited volume navigates the process of cyber norms formation by looking at how the global politics of cybersecurity emerge through socio-technical *hybridity* and *conflict*. It does so by problematizing the role of technology in shaping cybersecurity as a primarily problem-solving policy domain (Dunn Cavelty and Wenger 2020). On the one hand, problem-solving approaches to cybersecurity stem from the somewhat irreconcilable temporalities of computation and policy, which have pushed cybersecurity governance to operate on a securitizing logic of emergence, always trying to ‘catch up’ or anticipate new and future threats (Stevens 2016; Smeets 2018). This can be observed in the highly securitized approaches to cybersecurity emerging at the national level (Dunn Cavelty 2007), as well as in the normative impasse on emerging technologies at the international level (Cristiano et al. 2023b). On the other hand, the constant effort to adapt policy categories to technical ones, and vice versa, tends to further polarize policy debates, as shown for example in the highly politicized and diverse views that states have about the very nature of cyberspace (or the ‘infosphere’, to use the Russian terminology) and their strategic postures therein (Rosenberger 2020). While putting cybersecurity solidly on the political agenda, international normative efforts struggle to bridge the divide between the technical and emergent logic of cybersecurity and its global politics. That is, cybersecurity governance seems to often reproduce the same emergent logic that informs technical cybersecurity, with a focus on problem-solving, thus failing to capture cyber-security as a phenomenon constituted by the *continuously moving* interplay of socio-political, technical, and cultural norms (Hayles 2016).

By bringing together selected contributions to the 2020 annual conference of The Hague Program for Cyber Norms, this edited volume proposes to study the emergent character of cybersecurity not only through the translation of technical logics into ‘normative work’, but also by focusing on its emergence as a truly hybrid socio-technical domain of conflict. The contributions to this volume are organised into three broad parts: (1) emerging normative practices, (2) emerging threats and technologies, and (3) emerging actors. The first part unravels national and international practices of norm-making and includes chapters on infrastructure-driven normative changes in internet governance; international law and state practice in response to cyber operations; and the role of courts as novel spaces of norms formation and contestation. The second part focuses on emerging threats and technologies—as well as on ways to regulate them—and includes chapters on disinformation campaigns in the context of emergency communication; social aspects of information operations; and the AI/military strategy nexus in cyberspace. The third and concluding part looks at the normative role of emerging actors and includes chapters on the European Union as a normative power in cyberspace; the role and perspectives of raising economic powers from the ‘Global South’ on national and international cybersecurity; and civil society and multistakeholderism at the UN OEWG. This introductory chapter outlines each section’s rationale and main contributions and suggests that, to move forward with meaningful cooperation and governance, the international community ought to accept that conflict and hybridity constitute “features” and not “bugs” of cyberspace and its inherent global politics.

## Emerging Normative Practices

Cybersecurity is commonly conceived as a security domain that addresses ever-changing risks and threats in cyberspace by reducing and patching vulnerabilities (Liebetrau and Christensen 2021). This emergent technical logic is often at odds with the speed and logic of normative processes, especially at the international level where consensus-building and fierce geopolitical competition hinder substantial progress on the matter (Grigsby 2017; Broeders and Cristiano 2020). The changing nature of technological arrangements at the national level can have direct consequences at the international level (Jasanoff and Kim 2015). In [chapter 2](../Text/Chapter02.xhtml), Roxana Radu and Giovanni De Gregorio explore how the technical fragmentation of the internet infrastructure also shapes and informs the process of hybridisation in internet governance (Mueller 2010). Through an analysis of China’s proposed upgrade of the internet protocol at the domestic level, this chapter engages with the interplay between the establishment of a control-prone internet infrastructure with the fostering of hybridity between global and local governance levels, as well as between the public and private sector (Hurel and Lobato 2020). This case explicitly shows the deep, and circular, socio-technical entanglement that exists between the emergence of new technical and infrastructural configurations with governance ones (DeNardis 2014). Moreover, this chapter further reminds us about the interconnectedness of the technical and governance layers of the global internet, thus raising concerns about the prospects of technical fragmentation as a trigger for the polarization of the global politics of cybersecurity.

Another aspect related to how norms navigate from the national to the international and back pertains to how norms that are agreed upon at the international level translate into practices by states (Finnemore and Hollis 2016). The increasingly prominent custom of collective attributions of cyberattacks is one of the domains in which states have tried to come together through practice as a response to a normative vacuum. In [chapter 3](../Text/Chapter03.xhtml), François Delerue disentangles the relationship between international law and cybersecurity by looking at how the normative assumption that ‘international law applies to cyberspace and cyber operations’ works in the state practice of public attributions of cyberattacks. As shown in this chapter, the emerging normative practice of attributing cyberattacks—in what has been defined as ‘naming and shaming’ (Finnemore and Hollis 2020)—overlooks international law as an element of this publicity. To date, public attribution statements make either no reference to international law or else make unspecific references to it. Here, a discontinuity emerges: while being particularly vocal about engraining international law into the debate on responsible behaviour in cyberspace at the international level, some states are less vocal when it comes to acting on this. By exploring the motives of this ‘silence’, this chapter ultimately highlights the disconnect between normative frameworks and normative practices and demonstrates the different normative interpretations within the group of like-minded states.

The disconnect between international normative frameworks and state practice points to the need for a reconsideration of the sites where (cyber-security) norms are forged and acted upon (Finnemore and Sikkink 1998). With the international community struggling with the promotion of actionable norms of responsible (state) behaviour in cyberspace, processes of norms formation are moving or emerging elsewhere. Although interstate disputes on cybersecurity have never been addressed in an international tribunal, national and regional courts (mainly at the European Union \[EU\] level) have addressed conflicts and cases that can directly influence the normative process at the international level. In [chapter 4](../Text/Chapter04.xhtml), Jan Lemnitzer shows how a simple complaint submitted to the Irish Data Protection Commissioner by a data privacy activist led to the demise of the entire legal basis for transatlantic data sharing at the European Court of Justice. By showing the interconnectedness and hybridity of the processes of norms formation, the chapter argues that national and regional courts, more than multilateral processes, constitute an important space where conflicts over cyberspace are fought and mediated. As the chapter argues, the turn to courts also raises interrogatives about the multistakeholder character of the normative process at the OEWG about the involvement of private actors: while contributing with unique technical knowledge, they also bring their conflicts, fought elsewhere, to the international negotiation table.

## Emerging Threats and Technologies

In recent years, technological innovations have further diversified the repertoire of threats in cyberspace and questioned the categories we use to make sense of and regulate them. As a result, the lines between cyber and information operations are increasingly blurred (Shires 2020; Broeders, Cristiano and Weggemans 2021). On the one hand, the changing threat landscape in cyberspace can be thus seen as the result of the availability of new technologies—such as artificial intelligence (AI) and the ability to process large volumes of data. On the other hand, states relate to technological innovation to further advance their strategic postures and their different ways to understand information and infrastructure, and their security, in cyberspace (Buchanan 2016). However, regulating information as an element of international cybersecurity constitutes a problematic endeavour as it touches upon questions of freedom of speech and requires cooperation between states and tech companies, but these have interests that are often at odds (Cristiano, Broeders and Weggemans 2020; Broeders, Cristiano and Weggemans 2021).

In [chapter 5](../Text/Chapter05.xhtml), Taylor Grossman explores how the emergence of disinformation as a security threat to states informs norms formation at different governance levels. This chapter engages with the practices of mimicking and co-opting emergency communication channels as a threat of difficult regulation. Here, the emergent character pertains to both the nature of the threat as well as to the character of the governance domain (Backman 2021). Through the analysis of different cases of imposter content, the chapter problematizes the two socio-technical logics that inform the normative debate surrounding cybersecurity: one focusing on outcomes and effects and the other focusing on inputs and means. The chapter argues that the severity of imposter content as a threat is not dependent on the technical and harmful effects it generates, but rather on the context in which such a threat emerges. As shown by the rise of disinformation operations during the recent COVID-19 pandemic, emergency management situations are a context in which imposter content can severely harm public confidence, thus requiring specific ad hoc normative responses.

The COVID-19 pandemic has highlighted our societal reliance on the internet and how the emergent character of systemic crises creates opportunities for new threats to emerge (Gronvall 2020). Crises broaden technical vulnerabilities by allowing malicious actors to capitalize on societal vulnerabilities (Groendaal and Helsloot 2021). In [chapter 6](../Text/Chapter06.xhtml), Peter Pijpers and Paul Ducheine address the psychological dimension of influence operations in the context of crises. They specifically look at what makes national audiences more susceptible to fabricated information in cyberspace through the exploitation of a divisive and polarizing topic such as COVID-19. They do so by looking beyond an understanding of disinformation as a technical threat, thus focusing on discursive frames, scripts, and meanings. While disinformation as a threat precedes the ‘invention’ of the internet, the authors argue that cyberspace provides an unprecedented opportunity to harvest data about the audience through heuristics and thus micro-targeting. For international cybersecurity, this should be relevant as it points to the hybrid character of how vulnerabilities emerge in socio-technical encounters.

The emergence of AI technologies is contributing to increasingly sophisticated threats in cyberspace (Cristiano et al. 2023a). At the same time, automation and autonomy also offer the opportunity to process a large amount of data and thus produce preparedness and highly targeted responses to threats (Stevens 2020). When considered in the geopolitical context of different views about what cyberspace is, AI-enabled technologies also raise issues about their potential malicious use, thus pointing at the hybrid character of offence and defence in cyberspace (Taddeo 2018; Kaminska, Broeders and Cristiano 2021). In this context, the role of the private sector assumes further relevance for thinking about how commercial needs and market trends impact national strategies regarding cybersecurity and the normative processes at the international level. In [chapter 7](../Text/Chapter07.xhtml), Amy Ertan problematises the emerging framing of an ‘AI arms race’ as the driver of supply and demand for military innovation in the United Kingdom’s defence sector. By examining the security dilemma emerging through the entanglement of key innovation challenges, the limitations of the public–private procurement model for emerging technology, and technical testing and evaluation, this chapter tackles the complex debate surrounding AI autonomy in conflict environments. Through this analysis, it also advocates for a new role for intergovernmental actors (such as the North Atlantic Treaty Organization) to mediate conflict and hybridity between public and private, as well as for facilitating normative developments.

## Emerging Actors

The emergence of new normative practices, technologies, and threats constitutes both an opportunity and a challenge for moving past polarization and fragmentation in international cybersecurity. Tensions between Western countries and Russia, and the fierce competition between the United States and China, extend into and are reinforced by the digital domain. These can make or break the diplomatic agreement on norms of responsible behaviour in cyberspace. With international cybersecurity currently configured as a polarizing domain of great power politics, the role of emerging actors can be beneficial to move past socio-technical impasses. In [chapter 8](../Text/Chapter08.xhtml), Cedric Amon investigates the emerging EU discourse on digital sovereignty not only as a strategic response to the ‘quasi-national internets’ of Russia and China (as discussed in [chapter 2](../Text/Chapter02.xhtml)) but also as a contribution to European integration. It does so by looking at the complex and hybrid process of norms formation at the regional level through an examination of sovereign policy discourses in Germany and France, thus problematizing the negotiation of national interests with regional ones. The EU discourse on digital sovereignty has been often criticized for its strategic inconsistencies, for potentially touching upon digital rights in a way akin to authoritarian states, and for being at odds with the traditional EU strategy and image of normative power (Broeders, Cristiano and Kaminska 2023; Calderaro and Blumfelde 2022; Floridi 2020). This chapter argues that the EU’s digital sovereignty venture must be understood as a political response to the growing dependence on non-European technology providers, that is, as a public response to market dynamics rather than geopolitical considerations.

If the EU’s strategic efforts can be seen as proposing a ‘third way’ in cyberspace beyond great power politics and polarization, the growing importance of other actors worldwide points to the possibility of imagining cyberspace and its governance in more hybrid ways (Chenou 2014). This seems inevitable, and with the growing societal reliance on the internet in Asia and Africa, cyberspace’s centre of gravity will probably shift, very much like the ‘delocalization’ of normative practices discussed in [part 1](../Text/Part01.xhtml) of this volume (Deibert and Pauly 2019). In [chapter 9](../Text/Chapter09.xhtml), Irene Poetranto, Josh Gold, and Justin Lau explore the positions and involvement of three so-called ‘middle level’ countries—India, Indonesia, and South Africa. This chapter contributes to the debate on multilateralism and pluralism in the normative process of international cybersecurity by exploring the role of emerging economies as new voices that can potentially unsettle the current polarization. By looking at the history of the participation of emerging economies in cyber norms processes, the major issues that the three countries are concerned with regarding the development and use of information and communications technologies (ICTs), and the overlaps as well as the discrepancies of these positions, this chapter highlights a pathway for breaking away, or at least challenging, the polarization of great power politics. In [chapter 10](../Text/Chapter10.xhtml), Arindrajit Basu and Karthik Nachiappan further unpack India’s role as an emerging actor in international cybersecurity. Through an historical exploration of the country’s position in multilateral processes, this chapter shows that India’s capacity and willingness to engage with multilateral negotiations rest on the domestic interests at play. The chapter attempts to shed light on how India might contribute to the UN normative process on cybersecurity by comparing the country’s past position in other multilateral processes to its current position on the issue of cross-border data flows at digital trade discussions. Through this analysis, the chapter ultimately reveals the importance of thinking about the hybrid character of cybersecurity between domestic contexts and international positions.

The emergent role of middle-level powers shows that the ‘making of’ multilateralism at the international level relies largely on technical and economic developments at the national level. The UN OEWG welcomes this multiplicity of voices through a strong focus on pluralism, in the form of multistakeholderism. Interestingly, multistakeholderism itself has become an issue of polarization at the UN level, with authoritarian states opposing the substantial involvement of civil society in the normative process. In [chapter 11](../Text/Chapter11.xhtml), André Barrinha and Louise Marie Hurel problematize the ‘hybrid place’ that civil society occupies at the OEWG. By looking at the participatory mechanisms, types of engagement, and different states’ perceptions and representation of civil society in the OEWG, this chapter develops a critique of how the UN process constructs civil society as an actor and issue of normative relevance. Focusing on the complex dynamics of representation, this chapter argues that the OEWG contributed to the reification of an idea of civil society that is both diverse and unified; that questions and legitimises states’ decisions in this area. It also advocates for a more plural understanding of civil society through a consideration of its inherent hybridities as an asset, and not a burden, for the process of responsible behaviour in cyberspace.

## Conclusions

Taken together, these contributions ultimately reveal how—against the backdrop of fragmentation and polarization—hybridity and conflict constitute a valuable resource for international cybersecurity and its governance. They point to the necessity of thinking about cybersecurity as the result of the always-shifting relationship between technology, politics, and the making of international society. Due to this essentially contested nature, cyberspace remains difficult to govern. The internet has proven to be an ideal ‘space’ to blur a wide variety of boundaries, for instance through the contested boundaries between the national and the international, and between technology and politics. With the debate moving slowly at the United Nations, geopolitical tensions and power play have become the main ‘source’ of norms in cyberspace, with the emergence of more hybrid forms of norms production. As emerging technologies and threats further challenge international security, multilateral diplomacy struggles with devising shared norms of responsible (state) behaviour in cyberspace. It has become increasingly difficult to address (state-sponsored) cyber and information operations that, occurring at the threshold of blurring categories, further push conflict in cyberspace toward the hybridity of being neither war nor peace. Similarly, the hybrid character of political agency in cyberspace—such as private/public, state/non-state, national/international, civilian/military—enables the emergence of socio-technical conflict. To conclude, the emergence of novel normative practices, threats, technologies and actors points to the fact that hybridity and conflict are intrinsic to cyberspace and are here to stay. Through this understanding, the international community can move forward with devising norms that look at cybersecurity not as a technical problem to solve, but as a truly political phenomenon.

[**I**](../Text/nav.xhtml#prt01)

# [EMERGING NORMATIVE PRACTICES](../Text/nav.xhtml#prt01)

 

[**2**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp02)

## [The New Era of Internet Governance *Technical Fragmentation and Digital Sovereignty Entanglements*](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp02)

*Roxana Radu and Giovanni De Gregorio*

**T**HE RAMPANT EVOLUTION of new technologies, powered by 5G connectivity and artificial intelligence (AI) technologies, alters the current status of the internet infrastructure in unprecedented ways. As a result, the internet, as we know it today, will likely change face in the next decade. This chapter argues that this process is driven by an increasing acceleration towards fragmentation on a technical layer and, ultimately, hybridisation in the governance of the internet. Taking the example of the Chinese proposal for an upgrade to the Internet Protocol to support future uses, we reflect on the ways in which a control-prone architecture advances hybridisation along global and local levels, as well as across public and private sectors. A significant alteration at the level of technical protocols could trigger a much broader paradigm change, affecting global cybersecurity and undermining the protection of fundamental rights via constitutional safeguards.

The internet as we know it today will likely change face in the next decade. The evolution of digital technologies comes with proposals for new models of governance and control of the internet. In a move to upgrade core technical standards, China and its tech giant Huawei have recently advanced a proposal to the International Telecommunications Union (ITU) for a new internet architecture planned for 2030 (Huawei 2020). While suggestions for technical upgrades have been made on many occasions, the Chinese proposal integrated features of centralised control over communications and authentication through instruments such as ‘shutoff protocols’. The details are provided in the Chinese White Paper entitled *Network 2030—A Blueprint of Technologies, Applications and Market Drivers towards the Year 2030 and* *Beyond*. While its scope is subject to controversy, the immediate objective is to set up a new research group to deal with the limitations of current Internet Protocol (IP) standards for anticipated future uses.

Against the background of increased Chinese power and geopolitical influence, this proposal exacerbated fears that technical standards could be leveraged to replace existing decentralised decision-making within standard-setting bodies with more hierarchical modes of governance. It came at a time of heightened politicization of the field and added another layer of complexity to the ongoing normfare, or the assiduous development of norms of differing character advanced by a wide range of actors (Radu et al. 2021). In the new scenario, the internet would be altered at its root, triggering worries that the global infosphere could be turned into multiple spaces of national control and digital sovereignty.

For the digital ecosystem, power and security are two sides of the same coin. The fragmentation of the global internet affects both. When it manifests at the level of underlying infrastructure and basic standards and protocols, important functions such as interoperability, interconnection, and exchanges of data packets are endangered. A structural reconfiguration of the internet as part of a deliberate fragmentation is thus feared at the highest level (Drake, Cerf and Kleinwachter 2016). Technical splintering of the global internet could include network address translation, routing, IPv4 and IPv6 incompatibility, and alternate domain name system (DNS) roots. At the infrastructural level, the 5G debates have shown that security and strategic interests are clearly at stake in the rollout of next-generation wireless networks. As a result, the 5G Chinese equipment manufacturers have been blocked or their access restricted in many countries (Radu and Amon 2021).

The fragmentation underway at the level of safeguards and regulations is currently deepening and moving to infrastructural levels. The new normative battleground leading to polarisation in internet governance builds on the digital sovereignty discourse that both authoritarian and democratic countries have adopted (Claessen 2020). While remaining invisible to the end user, technical network functionalities have revealed an ever-more pronounced rift between western and Chinese positions in multilateral and multistakeholder institutions. Such tensions raise questions related to the security of the global internet and the risk of creating splinters with unequal levels of technical and legal protection. Fragmenting the architecture of the network according to two sets of standards would also affect the social layer of the internet, resulting in a clear separation between ‘bottom-up’ and ‘top-down’ approaches to its governance. Moreover, the decentralized decision-making processes that has prevailed so far at the level of internet protocols is strongly challenged by centralized approaches such as the one proposed by China at the ITU (Hoff-mann, Lazanski and Taylor 2020).

An important consequence of the gradual fragmentation of the internet is the blurring of lines between different levels of governance, particularly international and national, public and private. The governance of the internet is subject to a mix of global and local trends which *de facto* represent a hybrid system of governance of a transnational technological architecture. While, for instance, China and Russia tend to exploit the global dimension of the internet to centralise a model of governance that is closed to national realities and aspirations, the United States and the European Union have been outspoken about how they work towards a global and open governance of the internet. However, across the board, where the distinction between the public and private sector is already weak, hybrid processes emerge, in large part justified by a security and value-driven ethos to protect users and citizens.

We analyse these trends below, scrutinizing the literature and adding new perspectives based on legal and policy research. Our study shows how the new framework of internet governance plays out at the level of technical standards and protocols, affecting not only the internet architecture itself, but also how state actors define their strategies for cyberspace. This new perspective allows us to observe the extent to which the boundaries of internet governance and international cybersecurity are increasingly blurring.

### Fragmentation of the Internet: Technical Concerns in Focus

National segmentation, based on introducing limitations to internet technical functions or flows over the infrastructure, is not a new phenomenon. Countries such as China, Iran and Russia have already established strict national delimitations and controls to separate from the global communication network (Sherman 2019). Expanding this trend and creating more spheres of national control has serious consequences also on the offline world. Seen for a long time as a ‘democratising’ platform built on Western ideals, the internet has not been a neutral environment. Standards and protocols have played a critical role in promoting an open internet. Technologies for rerouting around damage in the case of outages and disruptions (Aben 2015 2018) and circumventing censorship and network restrictions (for example, at the level of the domain name system) were built in early on. They came before a set of legal safeguards from international law, human rights law, as well as regional and national legislation started broadly applying to the internet (Radu 2019).

Discussions around a potential fragmentation of the internet are not new (Drake, Cerf and Kleinwachter 2016; Mueller 2017). The scholarly attention given to domestic policy and jurisdiction has complemented a growing interest in studying technical standards and resources as instruments of national control. Standards such as Internet Protocol (DeNardis 2009) or the Border Gateway Protocol (Mathew 2016) have been deeply entrenched in the architecture of the internet since its early days. Despite the decades-long attention to the topic, technology decoupling is a relatively recent phenomenon, predicated on the ability to isolate and protect networks against emerging threats. While it can foster resilience, such decoupling can also introduce new risks on its own.

Following a number of changes since its first release in 1983, the Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol (TCP/IP) remains the dominant protocol supporting the current internet. It was originally developed as part of the ARPANet programme, funded by the US military and later globalized by US companies implementing it on their services. The most significant changes to the internet protocol included the transition from IP version 4 (IPv4) to IPv6, an increase to 128-bits addressing in order to respond to the growing number of devices connected to the internet. Earlier scholarly work on the ‘turn to infrastructure’ in internet governance explained how governments have deliberately targeted technical controls as a means to leverage power in cyberspace, jeopardizing the security of users (Musiani et al. 2016).

A recent proposal from China—new IP networking for Network 2030—aims to provide an upgrade to the IP in place for the current internet, with a view to supporting future technologies such as remote surgery (Jiang 2019). It is presented as an answer to the insufficiency of IPv6 for scalability in next-generation networks and an adequate solution for new uses in a world of quantum computing. The defining features of the Chinese protocol include intrinsic security, robustness, determining forwarding, global reachability, best efforts and connection of heterogeneous networks (Jiang 2019). It was put forward in September 2019 by a representative of Huawei, the world’s largest smartphone manufacturer and one of the biggest infrastructure providers involved in the 5G rollout. The new IP would connect physical entities (from wearables to vehicles), as well as industrial control devices, while providing ‘specific quality of service and security policies based on user identity, rather than mapping to something instead’ (Hogewoning 2020)**.**

Although the proposal remains vague in terms of technical details, the functional models it discusses were criticized by a number of Western actors. The new IP builds on existing advances, but parts ways with key components of the current architecture when it comes to addressing and forwarding (RIPE NCC 2020, Hoffmann, Lazanski and Taylor 2020). While presented as a decentralized system, the Chinese architecture would move forwarding and access away from the end users to centralized authorities, who would have the capability to block particular data flows if needed. In this scenario, the core of the network would have enhanced control over the end points. Moreover, the proposed plan challenges the ‘open and flexible system that is much more the result of decades of evolution’ (Hogewoning 2020).

Cybersecurity has emerged as a key concern in relation to the splintering of the internet, bringing forward concerns of increased governmental control over internet exchange points and alternation of routing protocols to serve geopolitical interests and weaken technical protections (Hoffmann, Lazanski and Taylor 2020). Alongside a technological decoupling, the fragmentation engendered by social and legal values embedded in architectural design is likely to have a long-lasting impact on the internet, as discussed in the next section.

### The Politics of (New) Standards

Global standardization processes are particularly interesting to explore in this transformation, as they take place beyond national boundaries but are not exempt from state influence. The consolidation of technical protocols that lead to an increased control of the global internet at the national level is of particular concern. In countries where extensive forms of surveillance and control over information are in place, like the Arab states or China, the internet has repeatedly been subject to public restrictions leading to the blocking of certain online services and/or the strict monitoring of data (Zittrain et al. 2017; Warf 2011; Deibert et al. 2008). The case of internet shutdowns is an extreme example of states, mostly authoritarian regimes, relying on network architecture to express their sovereignty over the digital environment (De Gregorio and Stremlau 2020).

The private infrastructure that allowed the internet to evolve into a global, vital network relies on the creation and upgrade of protocols and standards in a voluntary manner (although not necessarily in a disinterested fashion). In venues such as the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) or the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN), network engineers and experts worked in a personal capacity or as representatives of particular constituencies to put forward their ideas as voluntary contributions. The collaborative approach they introduced meant that the basic standards and protocols underpinning the global functioning of the internet could be discussed openly and agreed upon by a community in which governmental presence has always been minimal. The bottom-up mode of standard creation became highly influential and the related practice of ‘Request for Comments (RfC)’ is still dominant (Radu 2019). Yet the motivations driving engagement in standard-making processes and bodies began to be questioned, as most draft proposals come from experts with a strong corporate affiliation. The costs associated with in-person meetings several times per year are high and the participation of diverse voices is low. In the IETF, the standard-making process is currently led by US and Chinese engineers (Winzen and Weyrauch 2019).

The new IP model presented by Huawei representatives in international venues came with a proposal for establishing a new governance structure in the form of a working group within the International Telecommunications Union (ITU). As the United Nations specialized agency on information and communication technologies, the ITU has long struggled with defining the scope of its work on internet governance due to its multilateral functioning. At the same time, it worked towards containing the ‘turf war’ with organically grown bodies such as the IETF and ICANN (Chenou and Radu 2015). While leading a number of global standardization processes, the ITU avoided mandates overlapping with the development of basic standards and protocols at the IETF.

The tradition of voluntary contributions is closely linked to the fact that the internet infrastructure of the early decades (1970s to 1990s) developed in the absence of state constraints. But new patterns have emerged in the last two decades, as the Chinese government has strongly supported the development of new technology standards across various venues (Seaman 2020; Hoffmann, Lazanski and Taylor 2020), not only to secure a first-mover advantage for its companies but also as an ‘instrument of international power competition’ (Kim, Lee and Kwak 2020). In the elaboration of 5G standards, for example, Huawei and China Mobile were clearly leading in terms of contributions to the 3rd Generation Partnership Project (3GPP) framework and owned a majority of patents for implementation (IPLytics 2019). The 2018 Standards Law specifically encouraged international participation in global standardization processes (Hoffmann, Lazanski and Taylor 2020), while authorities such as the Standardization Administration of China helped harmonize standards work nationally and internationally. In recent years, there has been increasing evidence that partner countries are required to adopt Chinese-origin standards (Hillman 2018; Schneider-Petsinger et al. 2019).

The largest infrastructure development project of the century, the Belt and Road Initiative, is an important vehicle for the deployment of fibre optic connectivity and expansion of Chinese-origin technology in more than 150 partner countries across four continents. The technological work is led by Huawei and ZTE and comes accompanied by Chinese technical assistance. This new form of ‘cyber power’ (Broeders, Adamson and Creemers 2019) is predicated on a new vision of a digital pole, gravitating around standardisation and economic influence over infrastructure-building projects. The debates on cyber sovereignty have crystallized around national prerogatives to control data flows within digitally sovereign spaces, with the help of the home-grown tech industry.

At the international level, the internal control paradigm dates back to a 2011 proposal made to the UN Secretary General for an ‘International Code of Conduct for information security’ (66/359) by the representatives of Russia, China, Tajikistan and Uzbekistan (Radu 2013). The document assessed that the signatories of the code:

> endeavor . . . to prevent other States from using their resources, critical infrastructures, core technologies and other advantages to undermine the right of the countries, which accepted this Code of Conduct, to independent control of information and communications technologies or to threaten the political, economic and social security of other countries.

Offering early insights into how the concept of digital sovereignty might be understood, the Code of Conduct showed that information control was a new normative battleground leading to polarisation in internet governance. The two camps profiling in the discussions—the signatories of the Code of Conduct on the one hand, and the United States and European countries on the other hand, strengthened their positions in the last decade. As infrastructure takes centre stage in political struggles, attempts to fragment the internet architecture reveal a widening gap between ‘bottom-up’ and ‘top-down’ approaches to governing the network. The decentralised decision-making for internet standards and protocols that has prevailed so far via Western organisations such as the IETF or ICANN is challenged by profit-driven, exclusively private rule-making implemented through obscure processes by market-leading companies (Radu and Hausding 2020). It is also challenged by the new centralised model proposed by the Chinese (with the backing of Saudi Arabia, Iran and Russia) in a multilateral, state-dominated venue such as the ITU.

### Digital Sovereignty in Context: The Role of Private Networks

Over the years, a much more nuanced discourse has evolved around digital sovereignty and its importance in the information society (Pohle and Thiel 2020). In the late 1990s, UN debates on information security exposed cyber sovereignty as an approach specific to illiberal regimes (Radu 2013), but this is no longer the case. While the 2011 version of the Code of Conduct put forward by the Shanghai Cooperation Organization placed a strong emphasis on the principle of sovereignty as a means of national control, more granular definitions and understandings have surfaced in the last five years. The European Union itself has adopted a strong technological sovereignty and strategic autonomy stance in the work of the Von der Leyen Commission (2019), promoting a vision of digital sovereignty that is focused on the needs of Europeans and the European social model. For the Chinese authorities, cybersecurity has become a ‘new territory for sovereignty’, as stated in the National Cyber Security Strategy. Beijing’s intelligence laws impose obligations on any provider of internet services in China to share data held on the mainland with the Chinese government upon request, showing how sovereign control applies in practice to anyone doing business in the country.

The internet represented a step-change in communication technology, presenting open standards and protocols which allowed different entities to separately design networks and deliver services to their users under a unified architecture. At the end of the last century, the debate reflected the libertarian (Barlow 1996; Johnson and Post 1996) and realist stances (Goldsmith 1998; Lessig 1997; Reidenberg 2017). The decay of national sovereignty and territorial borders was represented by ‘a world in which jurisdictional borders collapse, and in which goods, services, people and information “flow across seamless national borders”’ (Hirschl and Shachar 2019, 1–2). Technological developments led the overwhelming majority of constitutional states to adopt a liberal approach with respect to regulation (De Gregorio 2019). To a large extent, democratic states have chosen to rely on online intermediaries to ensure the enforcement of public policies online as opposed to direct forms of intervention. This was pointed out more than fifteen years ago by scholars such as Birnhack and Elkin-Koren (2003) and Reidenberg (2004), who clarified that states can ensure the enforcement of public policies online by regulating network intermediaries, network engineering and technological instruments.

But the way in which we understand infrastructural power in global communications is likely to change due to the rise of ‘walled gardens’ and of authoritarian controls. First, powerful content providers and technology platforms from the West have consolidated their position in the market by building their own architecture and creating the technical means to operate in a self-sufficient manner (Moore and Tambini 2018). Submarine cables are a case in point. The shares of tech giants such as Google, Apple, Facebook, Amazon, Microsoft (known as GAFAM) in cable building continue to grow exponentially (Miller 2019). In 2018, Google owned 8.5 percent of the world’s undersea cables (BroadbandNow 2018); by 2019, it was the sole owner of three submarine cables and part owner of ten more. Facebook also had part ownership in nine cables and was a major capacity buyer for a tenth, while Microsoft and Amazon were contributing to building four and three cables, respectively.

Second, governments exert more control over infrastructure for security reasons, reasserting the critical important of communications in exercising key state functions. Historically, sovereignty has been closely linked to communication infrastructure as it was part of a state prerogative to exercise control over a territory (Hill 2014). From building the road system of the Roman Empire to connecting the early telegraph networks, exercising ‘infrastructural power’ (Mann 1984) has been a key condition for establishing and affirming sovereignty over a given territory. The communication technologies of the twentieth century (radio, telephone, etc.) brought new challenges to both sovereignty and territoriality, due to their transnational nature and their (almost exclusive) private ownership. The state itself was no longer building and managing the networks. Instead, it entered agreements with private providers as it saw necessary, whether they were based in its national jurisdiction or not. Governments kept their communication online using this private infrastructure and slowly seized the opportunity to define rules and safeguards for the protection of critical elements. In some cases, the core elements of the so-called ‘critical infrastructure’ were state-owned, but in the majority of cases they remained private, enjoying a special protection status.

As the Chinese argue, upgrading basic standards and protocols for faster and better quality is critical for next-generation devices and artificial intelligence developments. But they are also part of a political dominance struggle for China to become the global leader in AI (Radu 2021). This ambition is shared by many other states, but the Chinese have the competitive advantage of the largest user base in the world and a powerful home-grown industry. Their ability to control communications at the local level ranges from the management of data traffic to the screening of content. As Chinese-origin companies start exporting local values, the imposition of unilateral rules, without public oversight, accentuates the fragmentation of the internet along private-interest lines and poses a real danger for the global internet (Huston 2016).

### The Hybrid Phase of Internet Governance

Alongside trends of internet fragmentation, a hybrid governance framework emerges, where the lines are blurred between global and local values and the public and private sectors. In the last twenty years, global trends have underlined various patterns of convergence, where the state-centric model has started to lose its power (Ip 2010). Presently, the complexity of the different powers and gravity of actors at the global and local levels begs the question of how to govern them (Murray 2011). [Figure 2.1](#Chapter02.xhtml#fig2_1) offers a schematic representation of the two main axes on which hybridity unfolds in the internet governance arena.

![FIGURE 2.1 Two axes of internet hybridity.](Images/chpt_fig_001.jpg)

**FIGURE 2.1  
Two axes of internet hybridity.**

In this hybrid framework, global and local dynamics are increasingly intertwined, making the distinction between democratic and authoritarian systems less relevant. Scholars have noted the rise of ‘global law’ (Ziccardi-Capaldo 2008; Prins et al. 2017) to define a meta-legal system where different organisations and entities produce and shape norms with extraterritorial implications. The evolution of different systems leads to the emergence of plural logics which operate according to their internal rationality. As a result, the unitary state (and its law) is slowly replaced by the fragmentation of new institutions imposing their principles and values beyond traditional territorial borders.

The Chinese or Russian attempts to influence internet governance have passed through global and national strategies. Likewise, even if the EU and the United States have developed a strong narrative around protecting an open internet, establishing global values remains a point of contention. The interplay between global and local dimensions across the Atlantic leads to the consolidation of regional systems of governance which, in turn, characterise liberal forms of US and European governance. China has pursued a comprehensive strategy since 2015, exerting influence through the multilateral UN system, being more vocal in technical multistakeholder bodies such as ICANN and the IETF and imposing more regulations at the local level. In a consistent manner, the Chinese follow a clear model of digital sovereignty based on local values.

Looking at the public/private divide, the trend of hybridisation is clear. In authoritarian countries, where the distinction between public and private sectors tends to be weak, the relationship between governments and businesses results in a new model of infrastructural (public) governance. China provides a long-standing example of the hybridisation of internet governance. It has always monitored online activities (Zittrain and Edelman 2003), while building a digital garden wall where national businesses had the time and space to grow outside any form of competition under what is known as the ‘Huawei model’ (Wen 2020). This system has led to the creation of Chinese ‘digital political economy’ (Hong 2017).

It is worth stressing that some of the strategies employed by Chinese companies mirror the Western conception of the internet opening up to new services such as TikTok (Keane and Yu 2019). At the same time, the authorities are maintaining a strong control over businesses and have recently started sanctioning monopoly positions within their own markets (CNN 2021). Baidu, Alibaba and Tencent have become strong competitors of Google, Apple, Facebook, Amazon and Microsoft. In other words, China is only partially opening up to digital globalisation, while maintaining control over the network architecture and business conduct within its borders. Exporting this model abroad via new standards leads to a hybrid global regime in which the boundaries between the work of private intermediaries and political dominance aspirations are increasingly blurred.

In this light, attempts by state actors to gain global control by impacting the architecture of the internet through fragmentation represent powerful ways to regulate the digital environment. While authoritarian countries might benefit from extending their powers online and blocking any expression of legal pluralism from the private sector, democratic governments face a major impasse between accelerating the move towards mass surveillance and technical control and entrusting the private sector to safeguard individual liberties and freedom using regulatory incentives. This framework leads tech giants to enjoy a broad margin of discretion in determining standards of protection. Therefore, alongside the state expression of digital sovereignty, the ability of tech giants to govern infrastructures and digital spaces following their own rules constitutes another form of power that is in urgent need of checks and balances.

While in democratic constitutional states the protection of rights and freedoms is experiencing increased hybridisation due to the overlapping authority of public and private ordering, China focuses on increasing its authority on a technical level and within the technical community. The Chinese model relies on increased surveillance of users through state-connected businesses, which can collect, use and share data while competing with other actors on a global scale. The national-level social credit system provides a glimpse of the vision of control that China wants to pursue at the domestic level in the next few years.

### Conclusion

This chapter explored the characteristics of a new phase of internet governance dominated by attempts to alter the network architecture. As we move to govern digital spaces at the crossroads between democratic and authoritarian values, new forms of technical control meet national practices and political ambitions. Technical internet standards, which have long been considered a neutral foundation for a subsequent normfare (Radu et al. 2021), are no longer out of scope. The case of China constitutes a paradigmatic example of the collusion of interests between the public and the private sector, as well as the many ways in which location values transpire in technical standards. The technical redesign of the TCP/IP stack, proposed by Huawei as a means to overcome the limitations of current IP standards for anticipated future uses, allows centralised control over authentication and includes shutoff protocols. With broad applications for both individual uses and industrial devices, this technical framework is modelled on a set of controls applied by the Chinese at the domestic level.

But attempts to fragment the internet via the integration of control-prone standards do not stop at the technical level. Independent of protocols of interoperability needed to avoid a complete polarisation of the internet out of technical incompatibility, the governance of the internet as we know it today is affected. A first consequence is visible in the selection of discussion venues, with a preference for multilateral organizations such as the ITU. The decentralized decision-making of standard-setting bodies such as the Internet Engineering Task Force is thus challenged.

The proposal of IP fragmentation also follows the polarisation trend, where the imposition of unilateral technical rules would also have real implications for the security and governance of many sectors which rely on the global internet. Alternative standards developed out of local rules and dynamics and exported internationally raise concerns that the use of new technologies for control and surveillance would reshape the digital space and ultimately make the protection of fundamental rights and rule of law via constitutional tools obsolete. This new internet governance framework reflects various degrees of hybridisation across global and national, public and private levels. Multilevel collaboration thus becomes crucial to understanding the exercise of power(s) online.
