[**4**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp04)

## [The Hybrid Role of the Big Tech Companies and the Impact of Courts on the Making of Cyber Norms](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp04)

*Jan Martin Lemnitzer*

**W**HILE THERE HAD LONG BEEN an implicit expectation among scholars researching cyber norms that the future order of cyberspace would be settled by a multilateral treaty, ideally at the United Nations level, it is increasingly becoming clear that such a treaty is highly unlikely, at least for the foreseeable future. The failure of the 2017 UN Group of Governmental Experts (GGE) to agree on a final report made it clear that the great powers disagree fundamentally on key issues such as the interpretation of self-defence or sovereignty in cyberspace. The Open-Ended Working Group, originally set up by China and Russia as a counterweight to the GGE they felt was dominated by Western experts, succeeded in its stated goal of broadening the debate on cyber norms and including many more voices, especially from less developed countries. Yet, after more than a decade of cyber norms debate, we must conclude that while the United Nations provide an excellent forum to broaden the conversation and establish a global consensus on uncontroversial points, it is not suited to achieving progress on the many norms that are deemed contentious by the most powerful states. Yet, this does not mean that the development of cyber norms is likely to come to a standstill. Instead, what we are observing is a beginning ordering of cyberspace through legal contestation. As this chapter will show, this development will elevate the role of courts in the making of cyber norms and shine a harsh light on the hybrid role of the big tech companies in this process.

A prominent role for legal disputes in the norm creation process in cyberspace is fundamentally a natural process because no field of international law has been formed through unanimous consensus—as Christian Tams points out, every single one of them has been shaped by one or more major decisions of the International Court of Justice (Tams 2014). In some sections of international law, the court’s decisions have had a profound effect on legal thinking (for example, regarding state responsibility) while in others the impact was less profound as it coincided with major multilateral treaties (for example, on the Law of the Sea). In any case, the moment the ICJ takes on a new area of law for the first time is always an important milestone for the ‘mainstreaming’ of a field, as could be observed with environmental or human rights law. However, that does not necessarily mean that this first ruling will be a major doctrinal innovation. Rather, it is likely to confirm and elevate some of the principles already developed in the subfield in other documents or courts decisions. Thus, while there is some scholarship on the role of international or even national courts in the making of international norms, this chapter will attempt to draw out what is distinctive about this process regarding the norms of cyberspace (see Roberts 2011).

We are still in the very early stages of this development as no interstate dispute on cyber norms has reached an international tribunal thus far. However, national and EU courts are starting to take on cases that have a direct impact on some of the biggest issues in the cyber norms debate. I have written elsewhere about why insurers and their customers are currently arguing about the definition of cyberwar in US courts (Lemnitzer 2021), but one hugely important case has already been decided. The first section will look at how a complaint to the Irish Data Protection Commissioner launched by a single data privacy activist ended up overthrowing the legal basis for transatlantic data sharing at the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU)—*twice*. Now, the European Union and the United States must find agreement on limits to online mass surveillance by state intelligence agencies to ensure that data sharing between private and public entities can resume.

The second section will show that the most significant legal battle in cyberspace today is the confrontation between the dominant big tech companies and the attempts by states to rein them in through specific laws and regulations. This confrontation will ultimately be decided in and by courts, and it involves genuinely new legal questions raised by the power of online platforms and the way they can transcend national boundaries and make their influence felt anywhere. Secondly, this confrontation puts the leading tech companies in a unique hybrid position in the world of cyber norms: one the one hand, they shall be restrained and regulated to curb the ways in which they have allegedly abused their enormous powers. On the other hand, their unmatched technological and organizational capabilities mean they are key partners in states’ efforts to manage cyberspace and improve cybersecurity.

The third section will outline how the dynamics of these conflicts between big tech companies and states change if they are led by authoritarian regimes rather than liberal democracies. Courts are still an important forum but fulfil a very different function, focusing on an executive rather than norm-setting role—but with important exceptions. The hybrid role of tech companies also manifests itself in different ways: they are still deemed a threat to the political system due to their enormous reach, wealth and influence, but their alter ego is not that of a stabilizing ally. Instead, US tech companies increasingly appear as determined and well-funded fighters for free speech and the right to privacy.

In the conclusion, this chapter will argue that we need an urgent discussion about what this hybrid role and the turn to courts means for the future role of states and private companies in the creation of norms and rules for cyberspace. Given that a distinctive feature of internet governance is its embrace of multistakeholderism, a model that relies on giving private actors a real stake in the governance of cyberspace due to their unique capabilities, can the current setup survive the tensions inherent in their emerging hybrid role?

### Who Sets the Rules for Transatlantic Data Sharing?

This section intends to illustrate what the process of creating cyber norms through contestation looks like, using the one major case that has resulted in binding court decisions as an example. As will be shown, the process is complex and the outcome often messy, creating huge uncertainties for businesses and making states wish they had found a way to address some thorny issues of state espionage much earlier. Nine years ago, Austrian digital privacy activist Max Schrems started a legal battle with Facebook,^([1](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.001)) the EU Commission, the US government and the US National Security Agency (NSA) and won comprehensively, showing how activists can use courts to upset the existing political order even if the subject at hand is as momentous as the framework for transatlantic data sharing.

In June 2013, Schrems filed a complaint to the Irish Data Protection Commissioner asking it to use its statutory powers to ban Facebook from transferring user data from EU customers to the United States for storage and processing.^([2](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.002)) That practice, Schrems argued, exposed his user data to the surveillance activities of US intelligence agencies like the NSA. The Commissioner rejected the complaint as obviously unfounded, but Schrems persisted and challenged the decision before the High Court. Impressed by the Snowden revelations, the High Court concluded that what was really at stake in Schrems’ complaint was the legality of the so-called Safe Harbor regime for transatlantic data sharing agreed on by the United States and the EU Commission in 2000. In particular, the lack of any meaningful way to challenge the use of his data once it was in the United States raised the question whether the deal honoured the requirement under EU law that data can only be shared with jurisdictions that offer adequate data protection roughly equivalent to that guaranteed for EU citizens. Given that important questions of EU law were at stake, the High Court passed the matter on to the ECJ. After the European Commission had admitted during oral hearings that it had no way of guaranteeing that any safeguards for data protection were met after it had been transferred to the United States, the court threw out the Safe Harbor agreement as illegal because it allowed governments to interfere with the agreed-upon protections and gave no meaningful legal remedies to EU citizens who wanted to challenge the way their data was used (*Maximilian Schrems v Data Protection Commissioner* 2015).

While the judgement was technically limited to ‘personal data’, this did little to limit its impact because storing customer and staff personal data on US-based cloud servers has long been standard practice for many companies worldwide. The logic of the judgement meant that the key tool that businesses use to legitimize the transfer of personal data, the so-called Standard Contractual Clauses (SSC), was only valid for transfers to jurisdictions whose privacy protections are equivalent to those of the EU. Two months later, Schrems resubmitted his original complaint to the Irish Data Protection Commissioner, arguing that the SSC relied on by Facebook included exceptions to enable illegal mass surveillance by the US government. Therefore, by the legal logic of the CJEU judgement it (and the many thousand SSCs with similar exceptions) should be illegal as well. The commissioner deemed the complaint well-founded, and via the by now well-trodden route through the High Court, the case was once more presented to the CJEU for clarification.

Meanwhile, the EU Commission and the US government had replaced the Safe Harbor agreement with a new data protection arrangement called Privacy Shield, hoping that this time it would stand in court and allow for a reliable framework for EU–US data sharing. Yet, when the CJEU passed its final judgement on the matter in July 2020 (the so-called *Schrems II* decision), it explicitly referenced the NSA mass surveillance programmes PRISM and UPSTREAM and ruled that Privacy Shield did not provide adequate protections for the rights of EU citizens under the General Directive on Data Protection (GDPR) and the EU Charter of Fundamental Rights (*Data Protection Commissioner v Facebook Ireland Limited and Maximilian Schrems* 2020). Therefore, the new agreement was invalid, the SSCs it meant to reinforce remained illegal, and all data transfers covered by these arrangements had to stop at once. Soon, Facebook was formally told to stop transferring data to the United States by the Irish Data Protection Commissioner or face a potential fine of 4 percent of annual turnover.

The US government complained that the United States had reformed its intelligence and surveillance oversight since the Snowden revelations in 2013, and now had higher standards and better oversight mechanisms than most countries (US Department of Commerce 2020). In practice, EU companies and institutions continued to use the big US-based cloud providers, but now insisted their data should only be stored and processed on servers physically located in the EU. All leading cloud providers offered this service at a slight premium, but the question remains whether this is sufficient to satisfy the legal requirements of the *Schrems II* decision. After all, US cloud providers are bound by the US Cloud Act and must hand over data in their possession to US intelligence agencies if required to do so. Corporate customers in the EU can try to protect themselves by encrypting everything they store in the cloud, but nobody knows how many of them are doing this for all forms of personal data (Propp and Swire 2020).

Since the end of the Trump administration, the US government and the EU Commission have been engaged in serious talks on how to resolve the impasse and create a new EU–US data sharing agreement (European Commission 2021). Thus, the two CJEU judgements have forced the United States and the EU to debate the limits of what is permissible in mass surveillance and cyber espionage although all states involved would have much preferred to avoid this aspect of the cyber norms debate (Meltzer 2020). In February 2021, EU Justice Commissioner Didier Reynders suggested that the easiest way forward would be for the US Congress to pass comprehensive federal data protection legislation that the EU Commission could then formally declare ‘adequate’ (Stupp 2021). If this turns out to be the chosen solution, it would be the most remarkable example yet of the so-called ‘Brussels effect’ where the EU leverages access to its market and consumers to shape rules and legislation far beyond its borders (Bradford 2020). Yet, the talks are still facing significant hurdles and the question of which US surveillance activities are within the scope of the EU judgement are proving thorny (Christakis 2021a).

Moreover, the legal fallout of the *Schrems II* decision is not limited to the United States; other countries with data sharing agreements based on ‘adequacy declarations’ from the EU Commission such as Canada, Japan or Switzerland now know that they are open to legal challenge. The UK, currently seeking to establish a data sharing agreement with the EU following Brexit, just had to endure the humiliation of seeing its bulk online surveillance practices declared illegal by the European Court of Human Rights (Siddique 2021). Thus, with a third transatlantic data sharing agreement still lacking and further disputes likely, it remains unclear whether the *Schrems II* decision will come to be seen as a milestone in raising global standards of consumer data protection or whether it ends up contributing to the fragmentation of the internet by creating limited and exclusive data sharing zones (Hamilton 2020). As is always the case with courts making policy, the judges in Luxembourg are too detached from the implementation of their decision to offer any guarantees that the disruption they have caused will ultimately result in a new agreement setting clear legal limits to mass surveil-lance of global internet traffic by intelligence services. But while it is too early to assess the ultimate impact of the judgment, we can say that as an example of a court acting as a policy-maker, it is broadly familiar: started by an activist and building on existing legal principles of data protection, a court case has forced the hands of powerful states while leaving the bureaucrats to deal with the messy consequences.

### Whose Internet Is It Anyway? The Legal Battle Between Big Tech and the World’s States

This second section will sketch the ways in which courts are being used by both sides in the emerging power struggle between states and the big tech companies. Compared to the first section, the dynamics at play are much more complex, interconnected and, in many ways, genuinely new.

#### New Laws for New Platforms

While courts may like to build on existing legal ground, the reality of the platform models developed by the leading tech companies will force them to deal with genuinely new legal problems (de Reuver, Sørensen and Basole 2018). The digital platforms provided by Facebook, Google or Apple are not just marketplaces of ideas but fully fledged ecosystems in which people spend substantial parts of their working and social lives. Exacerbated by the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic, digital platforms have replaced or are about to replace television news, the office, the supermarket, the café, the high street and the main square. All this valuable data resides with a handful of companies that excel in data processing and individualized advertising. They also retain full control over what happens on their platforms. As a result, they have become extremely rich and powerful. As private companies they are registered in one country (often the United States or China), but their products are designed to transcend geographical boundaries. Both politically and legally, states have yet to adapt to the fundamental philosophical leap that the platform model represents, and the concentration of power that it has brought. As states attempt to gain more control over what happens on these privately owned platforms, the tech companies resist, and many of these disputes will inevitably end up in courts. The legal questions raised are often genuinely new, and the way the courts will answer them will have direct consequences for the future of cyber norms. Their judgements will decide the ways in which democratic elections and free speech will be protected (or not), and who will ultimately control the algorithms which are ruling so many aspects of modern life.

The most innovative approach developed by states so far is a reimagination of competition law for the platform age. The new Digital Markets Act that the EU Commission presented in draft form in December 2020 introduces the new concept of the ‘gatekeeper platform’: once a platform is identified as so large and stable that it has acquired ‘systemic’ importance for its users, it will be assigned special duties and restrictions. Platforms will be required to be ‘interoperable’ with other services and are no longer allowed to give their own products a more prominent position on their platform than those of their competitors. Two proposed rules in particular strike at the heart of the platform business model: first, business users must be allowed to strike deals with their customers outside the gatekeeper’s platform and second, advertising clients must be allowed to carry out their own independent verification of their advertisements hosted by the gatekeeper. In essence, the EU is responding to the challenge of digital platforms by creating an entirely new kind of competition law written especially for them.

The EU is clearly anticipating resistance: at 10 percent of the company’s total global turnover, the maximum fines are twice as high as in GDPR, and in cases of persistent non-compliance, it is openly threating ‘behavioural and structural remedies, e.g., the divestiture of (parts of) a business’.^([3](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.003)) Since Europe has very few of these companies, this is also an open challenge to the US (and to a lesser extent Chinese) tech giants. The Biden administration must decide whether it wishes to defend the commercial interests of American companies against Brussels’ latest ideas or adopt the gatekeeper concept to develop its own regulation for the tech giants. Either way, it is highly likely that one or more of the platform companies will challenge the new rules and restrictions or even the whole concept of the ‘gatekeeper platform’ itself, given that as of today it rests upon a market impact assessment of the EU Commission rather than any established principle of law. It will then be up to the CJEU to decide the fate of this new legislation, and with it how internet platforms will operate in the future.

#### Tech Companies and Courts: Fight, Avoid, Pre-Empt or Build Your Own?

Once the tech companies enter the legal arena they are a formidable force, easily being able to afford large legal teams packed with the best counsel, if necessary sustained for years in multiple jurisdictions at once. States know from experience that losing a case against a tech giant is a very real possibility. Yet, even the largest companies prefer to avoid court battles against powerful states if they can. As we have seen above, issues where existing laws and principles can be logically extended to restrict new digital activities are most susceptible to court interference. It is no coincidence that the areas where the big tech companies have been most keen to showcase their self-regulation efforts and ethics committees fall into this category, in particular the combination of artificial intelligence and facial recognition technology in commercial products. Moreover, the new EU draft regulation on artificial intelligence (AI) and facial recognition technology bears the hallmarks of effective lobbying as it uses tough and principled language, but upon closer inspection creates very few new obligations for the tech industry and largely outsources their enforcement to the private sector.^([4](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.004)) This does not mean that their activities in this field are likely to evade scrutiny by courts though; the link to existing law on data protection makes it highly likely that civil rights activists like Max Schrems will target the implementation of this technology in the coming years.

A particularly interesting aspect of the struggle between the tech companies and states for control over what is happening on online platforms is the increasingly open attempt by the social media giants to establish their own private court system. This raises fundamental legal and political questions, since the cases debated in these ‘courts’ are not minor quibbles about how to interpret the house rules set by the platform providers. Instead, they are often directly related to key debates in the cyber norms process, such as how to protect the integrity of elections and the fight against disinformation or hate speech online. When Facebook and Twitter banned the president of the United States from using their platforms in the aftermath of the storming of the US Capitol on 6 January 2021, this brought the problem into stark relief: are the leading digital platforms still simply private businesses who are free to permanently ban any account if they find it to be in violation of the rules they have written for their customers? Or have we reached a point where the importance of these platforms for public debate means they can no longer just treat them like their private empires?

After questions were raised about the legality and political legitimacy of its ban, Facebook had its decision examined by its new Oversight Board which is clearly modelled to look and act like a real court.^([5](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.005)) The board decided that Facebook correctly applied its own rules and that the ban should remain in place, without engaging with the fundamental question of whether heads of state may have the right to be a on a platform with a global scale like Face-book’s.^([6](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.006)) Predictably, Trump announced that he will challenge Facebook in court (‘Trump sues Twitter, Google and Facebook’ 2021). Besides the obvious political motivations behind Trump’s legal maneuvers, there are serious questions about the role the leading social platforms have acquired in public life that will need to be settled sooner or later.

An overlooked but equally important aspect is that the Oversight Board accepted Facebook’s argument that it had established a threat of violence. In fact, both Facebook and Twitter justified their decision to ban Trump and subsequently many other accounts tied to the QAnon conspiracy theory or right-wing extremism by arguing that their continued ability to post content would pose a threat of inciting violence. In essence, the social media platforms claim to have sophisticated threat analysis teams that can make an informed assessment when a particular individual or group is crossing a threshold beyond which they are likely to incite deadly political violence. This is a capability more commonly associated with a domestic intelligence agency or police force. Yet it is not hubris that has led the platforms towards boasting of such capabilities; rather, states have for years transferred the responsibility for complex political judgement calls like this to the social media platforms. By requiring them to practice and enforce ‘content moderation’ they inadvertently pushed them into a strange hybrid role they never wanted: trying to make money from cat videos going viral while simultaneously upholding the standards of political debate regarding hate speech.

#### Content Monitoring

In a marked change from the original approach taken by laws such as the famous Section 230 in the United States which relieved websites of any responsibility for what their users posted online, most countries have added more and more exceptions in recent years.^([7](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.007)) Yet, Facebook and the other platforms struggle with these laws because states are asking them to achieve the impossible: defining terrorism, disinformation or hate speech in a way that clearly distinguishes these from other forms of robust political activism is a task that has eluded political science for decades, yet the social media platforms must make thousands of such decisions every day, and almost in real time. There is a clear trend towards ever more precise requirements for faster removal of content, prevention of uploads of unwanted material and close cooperation with prosecutors. Civil society groups and tech companies are currently challenging several of the laws in court (‘Google takes legal action’ 2021; Electronic Frontier Foundation 2020).

In practice, the laws require a human being employed by a platform to make a split-second decision on whether content posted in another country (and perhaps a foreign language) is crossing the line towards hate speech or inciting violence, considering the context it was created in and the audience it was written for. Moreover, the social media platforms must simultaneously apply multiple legal definitions of hate speech or similar concepts that differ considerably even among like-minded liberal democracies. These are complex decisions that used to be made in courts of law but are now routinely made by poorly paid ‘content moderators’. They rely on guidelines produced by regional teams within Facebook that in turn must develop coherent political principles and provide guidance and examples on how to apply them in a multitude of languages and national regulations.

Unsurprisingly, Facebook’s main objective when attempting this Herculean task is avoiding getting sued in important jurisdictions. As leaked internal documents show, Facebook adapts less to how rigid local rules are but rather seeks to identify which violations will trigger court or legal enforcement actions and instructs its moderators to focus on removing such content (Wong 2021). This has two effects: first, it focuses the enforcement efforts on some countries at the expense of other, presumably less well-governed ones. Second, within the areas of focus it leads to a bias towards stricter censorship: the risk of being sued for failing to implement legal requirements for controlling hate speech is much higher than that of being successfully sued by an individual account holder appealing their ban or content removal (see Keller 2021).

The link between ‘content monitoring’ and states’ efforts to establish cyber norms is particularly clear when considering election protection: a duty not to interfere in democratic elections was one of the few genuinely new rules introduced in the recent OEWG report, showing how seriously states are taking the threat from digital disinformation campaigns. Yet, except in the small number of states with advanced cyber capabilities, the implementation of this norm is left almost entirely to the tech companies. Eager to prevent a repeat of the US election campaign in 2016 that resulted in uncomfortable congressional hearings about their passivity in the face of Russian interference, Facebook, Twitter and the other platform providers are now putting genuine effort into the protection of elections (see ‘Facebook, Google step up’ 2020).^([8](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.008)) They have specialized teams for this task that closely work with election officials in the various countries with upcoming votes and train parties and politicians to keep their accounts and data safe. A joint report by Microsoft and the Canadian government as part of the Paris Call for Trust in Cyberspace emphasizes the enormous scale and complexity of the multi-stakeholder efforts necessary to protect an election in the digital age (Government of Canada 2021). However, Facebook focuses its efforts to stop illicit campaigns manipulating national politics on those jurisdictions where it fears legal trouble (Wong 2021). As a result, election campaigns in the Global South receive far less and much-delayed protection from interference.

This is just one of the problems emerging from a model whereby states have assigned the hard work of content monitoring to the platform providers while using their court systems to establish the ‘effectiveness’ of the implementation efforts. Platform providers have learned to live with constant allegation of either endangering democracy through their passivity or shutting down free speech through their overzealous interpretation of national laws. Finding the right balance between successful ‘content moderation’ to fight hate speech and political disinformation on the one side and overly broad and sweeping censorship on the other will be a constant struggle that is difficult enough on its own terms.^([9](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.009)) Yet, what makes the challenge of tech regulation so daunting for states is that it is not only important *what* content created by *whom* is featured on the platform, but also *to whom* it is shown and *how*.

#### The Legal Battle for Algorithms

Slight changes in the algorithms behind the platforms can have drastic consequences as they are bestowed with almost magical powers to promote or suppress content (Riemer and Peter 2021). This purely technical process acquired political significance when accusations emerged that Facebook’s algorithm deliberately promotes politically extremist content or conspiracy theories since they are ‘highly engaging’. In other words, one of the most widely used sources of political information stands accused of deliberately distorting the general political debate in favour of fringe positions—even those inciting violence—to increase its ad revenue. Facebook’s new Oversight Board is sufficiently worried by these allegations to announce it will begin asking Facebook’s technical staff to explain to its members (such as former *Guardian* editor Alan Rusbridger or former Danish Prime Minister Helle Thorning-Schmidt) how the algorithm works and what it does (Hern 2021).

While the board completes its technical education, Facebook’s public relations team is already preparing its arguments for an upcoming court dispute. Facebook’s Vice President of Global Affairs and former UK Deputy Prime Minister Nick Clegg recently published an article claiming that political polarization was a feature of modern society and not created or enhanced by Facebook’s algorithm (Clegg 2021). Anybody who says otherwise, Clegg argues, will have to prove that the algorithm has significant negative effects on society. This is a pointer towards one of the key battlelines between states, tech companies and civil society in the coming decades: Clegg is of course right that in any court the burden of proof lies with the accuser, but how can anyone outside the engine room of a tech company prove that certain features of an algorithm are illegal and have damaging consequences?

It is therefore no coincidence that the EU’s proposed Digital Markets Act requires ‘gatekeeper platform’ companies to disclose the inner workings of their algorithms to regulators. Armed with this information, the power balance between the regulator and the regulated will shift decisively. If—as is highly likely—one or more of the tech giants are not pleased with the prospect of direct government interference into the technical core of their businesses and sues against the laws or regulations demanding them to comply, this will be completely uncharted territory for any court. Not only could a dispute over algorithm control unite the competition-law and content-moderation aspects in unprecedented ways, any such dispute will require courts to scale new heights in their ability to combine technical, political, economic and legal argumentation—all in an effort to resolve what is a clash between commercial freedoms, fundamental human rights and the interests of societies in shaping their own public discourse. Ultimately, this question will be laid before both the US Supreme Court and the EU’s Court of Justice, and their answers will have far-reaching consequences for our future digital economy and society.

### Tech Companies and Courts in Authoritarian States

Looking at the future relationship between big tech companies and states, courts emerged as a key forum, and the tech companies are in a hybrid role as both a threat to democracy and the main enforcer of rules meant to protect free speech and fair elections. Yet, we have only looked at Western, liberal states so far. When analyzing the relationship between tech companies and more authoritarian regimes, a rather different picture emerges. Here, the social media platforms are seen as facilitating unwelcome political and social debates, and are in some cases perceived as a genuine threat to regime stability. To try and rein in what they perceive as the malign influence of these companies, these states usually rely on their court systems.

The clearest example of this strategy is Russia, where the internet has traditionally been a relatively free space even though media outlets and other forms of public life have been tightly controlled by the government. Recently, however, the Kremlin has begun a coordinated campaign against social media platforms by using its prosecutors. Russian authorities are suing Twitter for not removing content related to Alex Navalny and have initiated legal proceedings against five social media platforms (Twitter, Google, Facebook, TikTok and Telegram) for allegedly failing to delete posts urging children to take part in illegal protests (Harding 2021). However, the Kremlin soon discovered that not all social media platforms used by Russians to exchange their views have physical offices in Russia, which makes suing them much harder. Consequently, the Duma is preparing a law requiring all foreign information technology companies offering services to Russians to have a physical office space in Russia (Clark 2021). This approach forces the social media platforms to choose between risking the personal freedom of their local staff members, implementing local censorship requirements, or abandoning the market entirely. In this way, the use of courts differs from that in liberal democracies: instead of being the first stage in a lengthy court battle, the opening of legal proceedings in an authoritarian state forces foreign companies to decide immediately whether they wish to leave the market or acquiesce to the changed rules. The exact same dynamic is currently playing out in Hong Kong, where Google, Twitter and Facebook are threatening to leave if new government restrictions are introduced (Davidson 2021). As China is preparing to implement the type of content control it has long practiced on the mainland, the big tech companies will have to make their choices.

Given the size of the Chinese market and the scale of the intervention required by the Chinese government, tech companies have seriously struggled with such decisions in the past. After famously leaving the market in 2010 over its refusal to cooperate with the state censorship system, Google attempted to re-enter the market in 2018 with a censored search engine, only to abandon the plan after an outcry among its employees (Gallagher 2018). Chinese tech companies have long tried to balance compliance with Chinese regulations with their global expansion as vast capitalist businesses.^([10](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.010)) Yet, the recent proceedings against Jack Ma, the CEO of Alibaba, for speaking out against Chinese regulators or the lawsuit for data privacy violations against Didi just after it had floated its stock in the United States are part of a wider plan by the Chinese government to tighten state control of tech companies and curb foreign influence (Hoskins 2021).

A specifically Chinese innovation in the use of courts is to apply an approximation of the Western ‘content moderation’ logic to curb unwanted content *beyond* its borders: when Chinese authorities sued a German academic who had angered Beijing with his statements regarding events in Xinjiang province and accused him of being part of a US-led disinformation campaign, the government-friendly *Global Times* openly explained that this approach was chosen because it looks similar to the Western way of ‘content moderation’ by court: ‘The litigation method based on facts and law is in line with international practice, and more acceptable and easier to get support’ (Zhang and Fan 2021). Using the concept of ‘protective jurisdiction’, a foreigner living outside of China criticizing China on US-owned social media platforms can be prosecuted according to Chinese law if found to have violated Chinese interests. The same logic can be found in the new data security law, which also regulates data activities conducted by organizations and individuals outside of China if they are deemed to either harm China’s national interests or the legal interests of citizens and organizations in China. This provision seems designed to allow the Chinese government to drag selected foreign companies before Chinese courts for activities abroad.^([11](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.011))

While China and Russia are regimes where governments have long expected courts to do their bidding, the most interesting current developments are taking place in India, where the government’s attempts to use prosecutors to shut down certain political debates on social media platforms are meeting a court system that traditionally considered itself as highly professional and fiercely independent. Therefore, unlike in China or Russia, the tech companies have decided to use this court system to fight back. The requirements in India’s new digital media law to store electronic communications and disclose them to the government if required clash fundamentally with the business model of social media platforms like WhatsApp, which is offering encrypted communication to its more than 400 million Indian users (Ellis-Petersen 2021a). Citing a recent Indian Supreme Court decision on the right to privacy, WhatsApp decided to sue the Indian government. In its statement, the company makes it clear that it sees this case as part of a wider struggle, referring to the efforts of ‘some countries’ to make online communications traceable and declaring that ‘WhatsApp is committed to doing all we can to protect the privacy of people’s personal messages, which is why we join others in opposing traceability’ (Ellis-Petersen 2021b). For India’s court system, the stakes are high: they can either drive one of the main communication providers out of the country, or severely embarrass the government, while judiciously maintaining their independence in either scenario. Needless to say, WhatsApp’s parent company Facebook is also engaged in monitoring and protecting elections in India, as in any other country.

The decision to sue the government marks a major escalation in WhatsApp’s dispute with the Indian state: when it had previously accused the Modi government of using spyware to hack the WhatsApp communications of Indian journalists, scholars and activists, it decided to file a suit in the United States against the Israeli spyware firm NSO Group which it suspected of having hacked US-based WhatsApp servers to spy for the Indian government. This, it claimed, was a clear violation of US anti-hacking laws. What happened next further illustrates the link between these court battles and the wider cyber norms debate: first, NSO Group claimed it should be immune from such lawsuits because its clients are foreign governments who were ultimately responsible for deploying the spyware. Since the governments enjoy immunity from such lawsuits, so should NSO Group as it was merely facilitating these state activities (Kirchgaessner 2020). This claim motivated Microsoft, Google, Cisco and others to file a rare joint amicus brief to the appeals court to warn it that awarding immunity to private spyware companies posed grave risks to global cybersecurity. After all, these companies produce not only surveillance software but all kinds of intrusion tools for their hacking teams and customers. Therefore, states must control the activities of such companies or face enormous proliferation risks for potentially devastating cyber weapons.^([12](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.012)) Astonishingly, some of the largest corporations in the world behaved like activists by jumping on a court case to gain attention for their agenda, which happens to be the introduction of a comprehensive arms control regime to cyberspace to prevent authoritarian regimes from abusing them. The recent leak of alleged targets of state-funded NSO operations has made this issue more widely known and further highlighted how interstate disputes can drive norm development: enraged by his inclusion on that list, French president Macron has pressured the Israeli government into taking a much firmer stance on the NSO Group’s export activities (McKernan 2021a, 2021b).

In conclusion, the roles of both the courts and the major tech companies change when comparing democratic and authoritarian regimes. Here, involving the courts often marks the end and not the beginning of a legal battle. Interestingly, while tech companies are also cast into a hybrid role of both partner and threat, Western states fear the tech giants might undermine or corrupt free political discourse, but authoritarian states fear they might enable it.

### Conclusion

The examples presented in this chapter demonstrate that courts and legal disputes will play a crucial role in the upcoming confrontation between private tech companies and the world’s states. Taken together, their decisions will set out what states and private companies can and cannot do in cyberspace. Therefore, stalling negotiations at the United Nations do not mean that the development of norms in cyber space has been frozen or interrupted. Courts are most courageous in developing new rules under two conditions: first, they need to be able to build on existing legal protections and link them in meaningful ways to a new context. From a legal point of view, the Schrems decisions are a natural extension of the jurisprudence on data protection that has developed since the 1980s, coupled with a question about access to a fair legal recourse. Lawsuits against the storage of biometric data to enable facial recognition systems or against potentially biased decisions made by an AI system supporting public administrations will also be able to rely on well-established legal principles and are thus very likely to succeed. Second, courts usually only act decisively when there has been a marked political failure to address or even acknowledge the issue that the claimant is bringing to court. We are currently seeing similar dynamics playing out in the string of legal victories by climate activists who successfully made the case that basic human rights compel governments to take much stronger action than they have considered so far.

However, there are two important caveats: first, what data protection and climate activism have in common is that a court’s recognition that a right exists is completely detached from the implementation and the practical consequences of its judgement. Second, we should not rely upon courts to fulfil this role: as a recent analysis of their role in rights protection in the United States has shown, an excessive reliance on courts leads to a selective and uneven form of rights protection, with some rights emerging very strong (to the point of smothering sensible compromise) because they were established by a powerful precedent, while other equally worthy causes are ignored because nobody successfully sued in their name (Greene 2021). In short, courts can partially but not fully replace the political process of norm creation through public debate, legislation and multilateral treaties.

Both limitations will become ever more apparent once the first cases force courts to decide whether the legal innovations created to control the tech giants are compatible with the wider constitutional framework. We can expect important and controversial decisions on government control of company algorithms, the use of AI (especially in combination with facial recognition technology) and the monitoring or removal of content from social media platforms. These cases will explore the limits between permitted and illegal government interference with tech companies and their platforms and how freedom of speech should be ensured in online discussions, especially before elections. Therefore, these decisions will have a direct impact on some of the most important debates regarding cybernorms and the future of the internet. As has happened in other fields, some of these court decisions might even be cited directly in future cyber norms codification efforts. In this way, even national courts can end up phrasing lasting principles of international law if the community of states agrees that an important point has been comprehensively settled. The challenge for the cyber norms field lies in providing a concise account of how these many different legal disputes add up to a wider battle renegotiating the rules in cyberspace and the relationship between state and private power in the twenty-first century.

The second key insight emerging from this chapter is the hybrid role of big tech: as new norms in cyberspace are being formed in regulatory court battles pitting states against leading tech companies and attempting to wrestle control over their products from them, the same companies are expected to be trusted partners in governing and policing the internet. They are not only required to implement whatever national standards apply regarding the monitoring of online content but are also pillars of the fight against political disinformation. While politicians fight for votes by promising to rein in and tax the big tech companies, they receive cyber security briefings from the election protection teams shuffled around the world by the very same companies. In fact, contemporary cybersecurity is unthinkable without the contribution of these companies: we depend on them for the creation or implementation of new security standards in product development, the swift and reliable patching of vulnerabilities, or the provision of guidance for other companies and organizations’ incident responders when a new piece of malware is emerging. Beyond close cooperation in protecting elections and shutting down disinformation campaigns, this even means joint action with law enforcement agencies in conducting campaigns against malicious hackers. At the strategic level, tech companies are also trusted partners in intelligence work, the creation of military AI and, in some cases, even offensive cyber operations.

In authoritarian states we are also seeing a turn towards the court system and a hybrid role for the big tech companies, but the dynamics are completely different. Here, the launch of legal proceedings is not the beginning of a long-winded process, but a clear signal that the regime has changed the rules. If the tech company is unwilling to comply, the alternatives are leaving the country (if the company is foreign) or being broken up and taken over by interest groups closer to the regime (if the company is domestic). Yet, private tech companies are also making use of courts in their struggles with authoritarian regimes, either by suing the government directly (in states where an independent judiciary still exists), or by dragging the foreign companies that help these regimes with their more nefarious online activities before courts in Western countries. In this way, the tech companies that are so often criticized for undermining public discourse in the West have become some of the most effective campaigners for freedom of speech and against government censorship and surveillance in these countries. Some of them have even begun to actively don the mantle of rights activists: while Facebook complains about new data protection rules preventing it from tracking its customers, its subsidiary WhatsApp is making proud statements about taking on the Indian government as part of the wider struggle for online privacy.

Being both the problem and part of the solution, or the main menace to and the protector of free speech, at the same time but in different contexts, means that the cognitive dissonance in the boardrooms leading these companies must be immense. This has implications for ‘multistakeholderism’ that need to be discussed: what the awkward term describes is that, unlike in purely consultative arrangements focused on influencing governments, private interests are given an active role in the governance of cyberspace and are allowed to make decisions on technical standards and develop policies as if they had governmental authority (Carr 2015; DeNardis 2014; see also Hof-mann 2016).^([13](#Chapter04.xhtml#ch04.ftn.013)) For example, the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN) oversees critical parts of global digital communications infrastructure but is a purely private company after its contract with the US government ended in 2015, and its legitimacy as a multistakeholder organization is fragile (Jongen and Scholte 2021).

This model of governance is in principle well-suited to cyberspace because so many of the relevant assets and so much of the skills and knowledge related to how the internet and IT networks run are in private hands, and it would be foolish to ignore them. However, the hybrid role of tech companies identified in this chapter raises obvious questions since our current model of internet governance is putting enormous trust in the capability of multinational private companies to faithfully separate their roles as adversaries and servants of the state. We are expecting these companies to fulfil functions that are vital to national security, the maintenance of healthy political debates and the future of the internet while governments simultaneously engage in tough talk about ‘reining them in’ through regulation and threatening to break them up for systematic competition law violations. Privately, employees of the large tech companies admit that internal conversations about this increasing tension are already taking place. We should have this discussion in public too, especially since the recent standoff between Facebook and Australia has given us a glimpse of the disruptive potential of disputes between states’ tech giants (see ‘Facebook reverses ban’ 2021). In the financial industry, conflicts of interest between different branches of the same company are resolved through so-called ‘Chinese Walls’ that prohibit the sharing of information between them and limit direct contact between employees. While imperfect and difficult to implement, this practice might be a useful starting point for a conversation on how to address the tensions caused by the hybrid role of tech companies and the upcoming legal battles for their regulation.
