[**II**](../Text/nav.xhtml#prt02)

# [EMERGING THREATS AND TECHNOLOGIES](../Text/nav.xhtml#prt02)

 

[**5**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp05)

## [Disinformation Campaigns and Norms of Emergency Communication in Cyberspace](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp05)

*Taylor Grossman*

**H**OW DO NORMS around deception translate into cyber-enabled disinformation campaigns? Particularly as the lines between peace and war become blurred, traditional interpretations of disinformation become murkier. This chapter investigates norm formation in the realm of disinformation predicated on mimicking or otherwise co-opting emergency communication channels. First, it investigates the literature surrounding the formation of conflict-related norms, specifically the translation of kinetic activity into the cyber realm. It focuses on two distinct logics of normative prohibitions and prescriptions: effects-based logics, which focus on outcomes, and means-based logics, which allow for distinctions to emerge based on the methods used in a specific incident. Next, the chapter outlines the specific case of imposter content, or disinformation that parodies official channels of communication, in emergency management situations. This section identifies several mechanisms through which imposter content threatens public confidence and makes a case for why this specific type of disinformation should be considered through the lens of perfidy. The chapter concludes with several considerations about the future study and normative evaluation of imposter content.

Early in the morning of 11 September 2014, residents of St Mary Parish, Louisiana, began receiving alarming text messages. Originating from a phone number with a 646 area code, these alerts read: ‘Toxic fume hazard warning in this area until 1:30 PM Take Shelter. Check Local Media and columbia [chemical.com](http://chemical.com)’ (Fitzgerald 2014). News of the attack quickly appeared on Twitter, as hundreds of user accounts posted photos and videos of an explosion in the area. A YouTube video also surfaced which showed ISIS (Islamic State of Iraq and Syria) claiming credit for the attack (Chen 2015; Delwiche and Herring 2018). St Mary Parish, an area with a population of a little under 55,000 residents along Louisiana’s Gulf Coast, houses a variety of chemical and natural gas plants (US Census Bureau 2020). In nearby Centerville, an unincorporated community in the parish, a real plant called Columbian Chemicals is known for producing a petroleum substance found in plastics and rubber called carbon black.

And yet, the entire incident was fabricated. As officials from local emergency response units began to investigate, they quickly discovered that no such explosion had occurred. Columbian Chemicals was operating as normal. No plant by the name of ‘Columbia Chemical’, which had been cited in the text message alerts, even existed in the area. Additionally, the 646 area code that had been used to circulate initial warning messages was not linked to any Louisiana-based location. In fact, 646 covers parts of Manhattan and the Bronx in New York City. Duval Arthur, the director of St Mary Parish Office of Emergency Preparedness and Homeland Security, quickly released a statement about the incident, deeming it a hoax. Arthur told reporters, ‘If we can find out who did this, we’re definitely going to bring charges against them for terrorizing’ (Fitzgerald 2014). He and other Louisiana officials mused publicly that the incident was the work of an individual with ‘a real sad, sick sense of humour.’^([1](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.001))

However, the Columbian Chemicals hoax, as it later came to be called, was not a simple prank but the product of a centrally orchestrated disinformation campaign out of the Russian Internet Research Agency. Although its history is difficult to trace, this agency arose out of a broader push by Putin’s government to erode internet credibility and usefulness as an organizing space for Russian opposition. Eventually, the Internet Research Agency turned toward sowing polarization and distrust in American institutions—starting with the St Mary Parish incident (Sanger 2018, 184).

This incident is in many ways a classic example of a well-executed disinformation campaign. Russian trolls were able to stir up confusion and panic by introducing a diverse array of ‘evidence’ of a terrorist attack on a local plant. Later analyses would demonstrate the inorganic quality of the social media deluge, noting in particular the absence of a ‘vocal minority’ that usually is instrumental in drawing public attention to an incident (Kodama 2015). Few local authorities were also alerted on social media, and posts did not appear to be geo-tagged to the St Mary Parish area. Yet, in the immediate wake of a campaign, such signals can be hard to parse; for a few hours at least, the hoax worked in stirring up interest, and was left unchecked by official channels.

This event is also unsettling because of its use—or, rather, manipulation—of traditional channels of emergency communication. Text message alerts have been used in a range of settings to update citizens about dangerous situations and have a long history in the United States in particular. For example, Hawaii’s Emergency Management Agency uses a drop-down menu to issue a variety of warnings to nearby cell phones, including tsunamis, landslides, and amber alerts (Barbash 2018). Many US jurisdictions also rely on Wireless Emergency Alerts (WEA), which can be authorized by federal, state, local, tribal, or territorial authorities and sent to any mobile device within a predetermined geographic area (‘Wireless Emergency Alerts’ n.d.; ‘FEMA Fact Sheet’ n.d.). These systems rely on public trust to be effective; their misuse or parody risks causing undue panic and even violence in the short run (imagine panicked citizens fleeing the area or taking up arms against suspected terrorist attackers at the Centerville plant), and eroding confidence in local emergency response systems in the long run.

How do we evaluate such an incident? The chemical sector is one of sixteen types of critical infrastructure identified by the US government as vital to national security interests (‘Critical infrastructure sectors’ n.d.). An actual, physical attack on the Columbian Chemicals plant would easily be considered an act of aggression, stoking calls for a military response. Of course, Russia didn’t attack Columbian Chemicals—not physically, and not directly. Yet, the Internet Research Agency did manipulate institutionalized warning systems, exploiting the inherent authority of text alert systems to create uncertainty, fear and, eventually, doubt. Other Russian disinformation campaigns have capitalized on trusted networks of public information. Later in 2014, the Internet Research Agency was reportedly responsible for spreading rumours of an Ebola outbreak in Atlanta. Fake news reports on Twitter even included a YouTube video showing medical professionals in protective gear taking an infected individual to the airport (Chen 2015). Once again, the hoax did not result directly in damage or destruction, but the event did capitalize on American trust in the medical community and traditional avenues for public health information dissemination. The ongoing coronavirus pandemic has proven exceptionally ripe for similar campaigns.

Deception has always been a function of human conflict. And yet, cyberspace has reopened many of the classical conundrums of the subject between acceptable deception and impermissible ‘bad faith’ violations of obligations to the enemy. Although disinformation campaigns are generally considered lawful in periods of conflict, certain types of perfidious deception have always been impermissible. Deceptive practices cannot be used to draw an enemy directly into an ambush or attack; similarly, medical distress signals cannot be parodied (Greer 2015). Even attacks that do not result in violence or physical damage may still trespass normative understandings of what constitutes permissible ruse.

### Theoretical Framework: Logics of Normative Prohibitions

New weapons almost always come under legal and moral scrutiny, and yet the vast majority of these innovations are eventually accepted and integrated into appropriate behaviours in times of conflict. Emergence of the rare prohibition is a source of contestation in international relations literature. This section will explore two distinct logics for understanding the emergence of normative prohibitions. The first purports that normative prohibitions are fundamentally effects-based. The second logic considers that normative prohibitions can be based on the means themselves.

#### Normative Prohibitions as Effects-Based Logics

In this first logic, normative prohibitions emerge out of effects-based logics and reflect conventional interpretations of the utility of a particular technology or tactic. At its core, an effects-based logic ‘starts from the claim that states care primarily about the “extent” of damage that is inflicted, and not about the “means” by which this damage was inflicted’ (Farrell and Glaser 2017). Although effects-based logics are usually associated with realism and its focus on interests and power, these logics can also structure rational-choice institutionalism and its model of norm generation (Price 1998).

While cyberspace may open new political questions, the rules governing kinetic attacks provide an important baseline for interpreting weapons and tactics employed in this new domain (Lindsay 2015). However, effects-based logics in cyberspace do not always generate straightforward analogising with kinetic predecessors. Effects-based logics can be used to generate new and distinct normative prescriptions and prohibitions. Yet, what fundamentally ties these theoretical approaches together is their commitment to outcomes: cyber operations are deemed either synonymous with or preferable to kinetic equivalents because of the outcomes with which they are commonly associated. Using this logic, cyber operations are expected to be interpreted based on the effects they produce. In other words, a cyberattack and a kinetic attack that produce the same outcome should be viewed as essentially identical. Any differences in interpretation are rooted in effects-based understandings: a cyberattack may have more second- and third-order effects, for instance; or a cyberattack may be more easily reversed. Minimal theoretical weight is given to the distinct method or means of attack.

#### Normative Prohibitions as Means-Based Logics

This second logic does not negate the importance of effects-based logics in norm development. Norms governing war are almost never completely unaffected by political and interest-based processes, which tend to elevate outcomes in moral analyses. As Ward Thomas points out, ‘norms are products of political processes and therefore cannot be divorced from considerations of power and interest, which often distort—but do not obliterate—moral principles’ (Thomas 2001, 3). However, through this logic, normative prohibitions have their own causal force: norms, interests, and power are mutually constitutive, constructing state identities and shifting priorities accordingly (Thomas 2001, 20). Norm adoption is not always based on self-interest; rather, some norms arise out of logics of appropriateness, or broadly agreed-upon standards of behaviour (March and Olsen 1998; Finnemore and Sikkink 1998; Risse 2000). Human activity ‘involves evoking an identity or role and matching the obligations of that identity or role to a specific situation’ (March and Olsen 1998, 951). Actions are rule based, as individuals make decisions based on how they deem that certain choices will align with a particular identity to which they aspire or ascribe.

Several scholars including Tannenwald (1999), Price (1997), and Watts (2014) have argued that means-based prohibitions are not simply extensions of outcomes-based prohibitions: while outcomes can provide a starting point for these prohibitions, means can come to be judged as unacceptable regardless of the effects rendered (Percy 2007). Outcomes often provide a starting rationale for weapons bans, but they rarely capture the complete picture. Instead, certain means can be judged unacceptable regardless of effects. However, this logic is much less common in the cybersecurity literature. A means-based logic could generate distinct normative prohibitions and prescriptions based on the method of cyberattack. If means-based logics exist, then cyberattacks will be interpreted differently *because* of the type of method used, regardless of whether the outcomes produced are parallel to kinetic or other alternatives.

#### Normative Prohibitions and Disinformation

Norms surrounding disinformation campaigns remain fairly nascent and disjointed. Several frameworks have been developed but have faced trouble gaining widespread adoption (‘Developing norms and standards’ n.d.). Perhaps the most successful and well-known attempt at codification is the European Union Code of Practice on Disinformation, which emerged out of a High Level Expert Group convened on the issue (European Commission 2018b). The Code was adopted in the summer of 2018 and updated in 2022. Several online platforms have been urged to produce implementation reports, demonstrating the progress that they have made to comply with the Code of Practice. However, the Code primarily focuses on the responsibilities of social media companies, with a stated focus on ‘empowering consumers’. When it comes to faked accounts, the Code provides very little in terms of specific normative standards, apart from recognising ‘the importance of intensifying and demonstrating the effectiveness of efforts to close fake accounts’ as well as the importance of establishing ‘clear marking systems and rules for bots to ensure their activities cannot be confused with human interactions’ (European Commission 2018a). There is no specific mention of imposter content or of faked accounts that parody government authorities. Similarly, there is no mention of disinformation surrounding emergency communications.

Indeed, most efforts at norm creation and codification in the realm of disinformation focus on ‘fake news’ and improving journalistic ethics and fact-checking, rather than on imposter content that seeks to mimic authorised, insider sources from governments.^([2](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.002)) As Alicia Wanless and other disinformation researchers have pointed out, the field is still struggling to understand how to measure the effects of influence operations, let alone how to stop their proliferation (Wanless 2021; Shapiro, Thompson and Wanless 2020).

When it comes to studying and measuring effects of influence operations and other forms of disinformation, there is little consensus. Effects-based logics of norm formation are clearly tricky to apply if we cannot generate reliable outcomes. The causal relationships between an incident and an outcome are also often murky or complex in the world of online interactions. As the United States seems intent on relearning with some frequency, the relationship between a written or verbal communication and a violent incident can be difficult to parse; inciting violence is not protected speech, but ‘abstract appeals for illegal actions or even highly offensive expression’ often are (Wagstaff 2021; Goodman, Dugas and Tonckens 2021; Braddock 2021).

### Perfidy and Cyber-Enabled Disinformation

Where outcome measurements fall short, normative prohibitions that focus on means-based logics may provide a helpful lens in understanding norms surrounding disinformation. One such example is the perfidy prohibition. Perfidy is a central component of traditional notions of honour in warfighting, often described as an act of ‘bad faith’ treachery.^([3](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.003)) This prohibition is not, as many often assume, simply a ban on ‘the impersonation of civilians during armed conflict’ (Rowe 2013). A perfidious act could involve other forms of deception or betrayal, including assassination or the use of poison. Classical examples of perfidy include feigning an intent to surrender, affecting sickness or injury, or employing false flags or insignia of protected status (such as emblems of neutral parties or medical services). Importantly, perfidy attempts to codify the distinction between acceptable and unacceptable forms of deception—a distinction that is central to cyberattacks and disinformation.

While the general ban is now a codified element of international law, the specifics of the normative prohibition have been difficult to pin down in practice (Watts 2014, 108). This definitional elusiveness arises in part because perfidy is predicated on *method*, not outcome. Although thus far the perfidy prohibition has related only to actions that achieve a violent outcome, the outcome itself is not all that makes the action objectionable.

There are three distinct elements of what makes perfidy outlawed under traditional understandings of Just War theory:

1.  Perfidious mechanism: a perfidious act involves a treacherous betrayal, predicated on breaking the enemy’s trust.
2.  Outcome/intended outcome: a perfidious act is designed to kill or destroy.
3.  Causal/temporal relationship: a perfidious act involves an immediate or semi-immediate payoff.

All three elements must be present in order to be considered legal perfidy. A soldier who feigns sickness to flee a battle is not considered in violation of the legal perfidy prohibition; although they have broken the enemy’s trust, invoking element (1), they have not done so to harm their opponent. Elements (2) and (3) are thus missing. Similarly, camouflage techniques that are used to disguise weapons factories are generally not deemed perfidious, since the (3) causal and temporal relationship is quite complex and delayed: although these instruments will eventually be used to maim or kill, they are not being put to that purpose in the factory itself. Even cases of munitions factories draped in medical or religious symbology have not risen to the level of legal perfidy (Watts 2014).

Using this definition, nonviolent cyberattacks can be evaluated as part of a continuum of perfidious behaviour. A nonviolent cyberattack will never reach all three criteria; by definition, these attacks do not involve (2) death or destruction. However, nonviolent cyberattacks can include (1) a treacherous betrayal with (3) an immediate or semi-immediate (nonviolent) payoff. This three-pronged definition allows for the isolation of the mechanism itself, apart from its connection to violence.

Similarly, in the case of disinformation campaigns, the perfidy mechanism provides a useful lens for evaluating the co-opting or mimicking of trusted forms of emergency management communication. While such a campaign is unlikely to cause (2) immediate or semi-immediate death and destruction, it is predicated on the misuse of recognisable, trusted signals. There is certainly an element of ‘bad faith’ treachery in mimicking emergency text message communications to spread false public health information. Importantly, this understanding also can isolate the means of a particular campaign, without shifting focus to its effects. The direct results of influence operations can be difficult to measure; however, the misuse of a protected symbol such as an emergency communication system is perhaps problematic in its own right. The perfidy prohibition is both a legal standard and a normative frame, which can be used to isolate and evaluate the method of an incident. In the case of imposter content, this lens is particularly fruitful.

### Imposter Content and Emergency Management

As online communities proliferate, so too do the information disorders that plague them. Here, I define disinformation as predicated on two distinct features: (1) the information is false, and (2) the information is knowingly shared to cause harm.^([4](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.004)) This definition helps distinguish disinformation from other information disorders, including false information that is unknowingly shared or shared without intent to harm (often termed ‘mis-information’, these cases often arise when people reshare false information or rumours on social media in the wake of a significant event) and authentic or genuine information that is shared in order to harm (often termed ‘mal-information’, this can include leaked private correspondences or personal photos).^([5](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.005)) My express focus is on disinformation that aims to produce physical or emotional harms, rather than monetary gains. Many disinformation campaigns are orchestrated primarily to scam unsuspecting users out of their cash; others are intended to produce both harm and profit (Waseem and Chang 2021; Sjouwerman 2020). However, for the purpose of this chapter, I will focus on the harm motivation.

Imposter content is a specific form of disinformation: information that poses as originating from an official source. Imposter content can ostensibly arise at any time and provide any information; a spoofed account could disseminate disinformation related to celebrity gossip or animal facts. Faked news accounts are plentiful online, including altered headlines or parody websites. In this paper, however, I will focus specifically on content that masquerades as from a government authority and claims to be providing information about an ongoing or imminent emergency. Importantly, both context and content are relevant here: the imposter content at issue originates from a faked insider authority and purports to share important updates related to an emergency, either real or imagined. In other words, imposter content could invent an entirely new incident or emerge in the course of an ongoing disaster.

Emergency management and communication is a nettlesome field, and much has been written about what makes an early warning system effective. Measuring the impact of warning remains difficult, as individuals are reacting to a variety of stimuli, including media coverage and information from trusted personal sources such as friends and family. People’s reactions may also be conditioned by their education level, socioeconomic status, and other characteristics (Tierney, Lindell and Perry 2001, 29–30; 36–39). However, in the field of natural disaster warning, many scholars agree that the most effective systems operate through multiple channels to provide reassurance and to reinforce actionable information (Tierney, Lindell and Perry 2001; Smith 2013, 125; Herring 2003).

An ideal emergency management communication system relies on both persistent and diverse mechanisms for engagement.^([6](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.006)) First, a society or community works to build up *institutional commitment and mechanisms* in everyday interactions between authorities and the populace. Common systems include reporting lines for emergencies, such as 911 in the United States and 999 in the United Kingdom. Many localities also have hotlines for individuals to request information in non-emergency settings. These mechanisms allow authorities to respond to individual incidents; they also serve to build public trust in response procedures and information dissemination.

These institutional mechanisms then help to frame and promote public *education and preparedness*, including around emergency procedures. Often, these educational campaigns are specifically tailored to local contexts. In Northern California, for example, earthquakes are common occurrences near the San Andreas fault line; in the counties in the area, schools practice earthquake drills, and public awareness campaigns often centre around best practices in the event of prolonged power outages or blocked roads that can result from a significant quake.

In the event of an actual emergency, local or national authorities mobilise *alerts, warnings and notifications*. In the United States, WEAs are perhaps the most commonly used form of emergency communication. These emergency alerts can be broadcast to any mobile device in a specified area and include recognisable haptic markers and notification sounds so that device users can distinguish the WEA from other buzzes and dings (‘Wireless Emergency Alerts’ n.d.; ‘FEMA Fact Sheet’ n.d.). Some counties use CodeRED, an opt-in text alert system that can send short update messages to local devices. These systems use designated numbers: emergency notifications are generally sent from a caller ID 866-419-5000 so that they can be easily identified. CodeRED alerts are often used to warn a population about an impending incident and then direct those individuals to official websites and social media accounts for more information (CodeRED n.d.).

If the public has been well-educated about emergencies and has also experienced institutional mechanisms and interactions with authorities, then that public will understand how to interpret an alert or warning appropriately. A well-functioning emergency management communication system thus builds and reinforces a resilient *public response*. Northern Californians will know to shelter if indoors during an earthquake, and where to assemble in the aftermath. Residents may have even stockpiled goods in case of a power outage and will know to refrain from flooding emergency hotlines with calls unless there is a serious issue.

This, in turn, helps align the public response to the *institutional response*. First responders can preserve resources, focusing on the areas and populations most affected by a specific incident. By reducing panic and uncertainty in the population, emergency response institutions will be able to do their jobs more effectively. Institutional and public responses thus build off of each other in a feedback loop, where first responders and government authorities are acting in alignment with community needs and concerns. An ideal system then reinforces public trust in everyday institutional mechanisms and reinforces education and preparedness principles in everyday life.

Imposter content, however, threatens to disrupt and weaken the relationship between emergency alerts and the public response. Parodied information intervenes directly with the population, introducing a new or exaggerated threat through a mechanism generally reserved for official notification. In the short term, imposter content can provoke panic or uncertainty, as individuals scramble to react to a fake emergency. The long-term effects, however, could be far worse for emergency management systems and public trust.

The problem of ‘over-warning’ or ‘cry wolf’ syndrome is much debated in political science and risk communication contexts. Most often associated with intelligence theorists such as Cynthia Grabo (2002) and Roberta Wohlstetter (1965), ‘cry wolf’ is the phenomenon of government over-warning to the extent that the public stops heeding future alerts. Many natural disaster scholars have argued that this problem is exaggerated or even nonexistent: indeed, over-warning does not reduce the credibility of government alert systems but can even increase public awareness and lead to improved outcomes in the future. Terrorism scholars have been much more divided, with many arguing that warning systems serve only to tip off adversaries and shift targets or tactics (Betts 1980–1981; Pinker 2007).^([7](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.007))

However, the impact of *faked* official warning systems is even less clear. A trigger-happy government warning system is different from a Russian-designed parodied text alert. Whether imposter content ultimately erodes public trust in official authorities in times of crisis is yet to be seen; what is clear, however, is that governments are responding to the potential for such disinformation campaigns to disrupt populations and discredit official sourcing. For example, the US Department of Homeland Security assembled a Social Media Working Group for Emergency Services and Disaster Management which put together a report in 2018 that focused in large part on the problem of imposter content, including best practices related to technology implementation and emergency management authorities (‘Countering false information’ 2018).

Here, the lens of the perfidy prohibition is useful in distinguishing the mechanisms of imposter content that are most troublesome. These situations of imposter content employ a ‘false flag’ of sorts: they rely on mimicking trusted government authorities with the purpose of producing harm. Here, harms may not be immediate and may not include death or destruction; as such, imposter content will likely never rise to the level of *legal* perfidy that is outlined by the laws of armed conflict. Yet, imposter content does perhaps deserve normative consideration all the same. Means-based logics help separate and distil the mechanism behind this specific disinformation campaign: the co-opting and perversion of trusted markers of communication. Perfidy has long been used to separate and insulate certain symbols of neutrality from the scourge of the battlefield. Emergency communications systems—particularly those that address civilians rather than military servicemembers—seem clearly to fall into this protected class. Even without the intention of direct violence, means-based logics provide a framework for adjudicating between permissible and impermissible deception. The mechanism itself may matter, regardless of the ultimate effect.

### Conclusion: Understanding and Testing Logics of Norm Formation

This paper does not contend that outcomes are irrelevant. On the contrary, scholars should devote more time and energy to understanding the long-term effects of imposter content on public trust and safety. The problem of over-warning remains unsettled; the problem of mimicked warning even less so. Imagine, for instance, that you lived in a coastal region of the United States that was known for the occasional tropical storm or hurricane. An adversary then infiltrated the WEA system in the United States or parodied it effectively and began sending weekly or even daily alerts of a major hurricane encroaching on the area. How quickly would residents of the region begin losing faith in the WEA system? How would one tell between a real alert and a fake alert? What would happen if an actual hurricane hit during this time period? What kind of backup or alternative systems of warning would authorities turn to, and would they work? Such an imposter content campaign could lead to lives lost and property destroyed, and could erode long-term trust in the system to a dangerous degree.

Especially as disinformation continues to plague online spaces, the perfidy prohibition and its related mechanisms can be a useful tool for identifying and evaluating imposter content as a specific case. Fake news and its various incarnations are certainly dangerous; however, imposter content in emergency management communication is a narrow, potentially perfidious manifestation worthy of special consideration. Certain symbols have long been protected in physical space—should they perhaps be afforded similar protections in cyberspace?

In previous research, I have evaluated how nonviolent cyberattacks are being interpreted by both lay and expert policy-making audiences (Grossman 2020). Using the two logics of norm formation outlined earlier, I developed four distinct approaches of means-based prohibition formation. Effects-based logics form the basis of the first two: (1) utility maximisation and (2) institutional protection. The first, utility maximisation, understands means-based prohibitions as a product of materialist effects; even when a norm may appear to regulate or outlaw a specific method, the norm actually reflects the outcomes rendered by that method (Goldsmith and Posner 2005; Jervis 1976; Waltz 1979; Gilpin 1983). This approach predicts continuity across domains: whether an attack is perpetrated through kinetic or cyber means is irrelevant, as the focus is on the ultimate effect of the attack. Disinformation campaigns—even imposter content—are unlikely to result directly (and quickly) in lives lost or property destroyed. Utility maximisation is thus likely to have a permissive interpretation of imposter content.

The second approach, institutional protection, is also effects-based in its logic of normative prohibitions. However, the approach focuses not solely on materialist outcomes. Norms are generated to reinforce existing cooperative agreements and arrangements. Normative prohibitions need not generate similar outcomes across domains; instead, normative prohibitions are created and enforced to protect existing institutions. Here, institutions are deeply rational creations, formed by states to maximise self-interest (Stein 2008; Keohane 1984; Doyle 1997; Ruggie 1998). This approach predicts continuity across domains, but on the basis of shared institutions rather than shared outcomes. Perfidious behaviour is thus unacceptable not because it results in materialist losses, but because it weakens existing protections for neutral symbology. In this approach, imposter content could be viewed restrictively because of potential long-term erosion of public trust in emergency response and management channels.

Means-based logics provide the framework for understanding the second two approaches, (3) identity resonance and (4) essentialisation. The third, identity resonance, adopts a constructivist lens for interpreting norms, whereby they are mutually constitutive of the identities and interests of the actors that produce them (Finnemore 1996; Katzenstein 1996; Checkel 1998). Normative prohibitions reflect sociocultural understandings of appropriate behaviour, dominated by logics of appropriateness. The perfidy prohibition is rooted in states’ self-understandings about civilised actions on the battlefield. This approach predicts potential divergence across domains. Although some scholars have argued for a more restrictive attitude toward cyberspace and cyberattacks, the majority have proposed that cyber operations will be viewed more permissibly than their analogues in kinetic warfare.^([8](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.008)) Complex causal chains can obscure responsibility; similarly, attribution issues can make it more difficult to determine the true perpetrator.^([9](#Chapter05.xhtml#ch05.ftn.009)) Imposter content may skirt normative condemnation in this approach, as the perfidious mechanism behind these parodied accounts is less clear.

The fourth approach, essentialisation, contends that normative prohibitions result from the ingrained characteristics of the method used. Certain weapons are deemed cruel or unusual and are banned because they trespass appropriate behaviour. In this approach, cyberattacks are generally emphasised as elusive and unpredictable; these confusing operations of computer code and veiled attackers can strike anyone at any time. Essentialisation outlines a more restrictive view of cyberattacks specifically because of the method, rather than the effect. Disinformation campaigns are likely to also be viewed as less acceptable because of their seeming intangibility. Imposter content, too, could be evaluated more harshly, since it is predicated on a particularly troubling form of invisible deception.

Which approach is ultimately dominating interpretations of imposter content? Further study is certainly needed to unpack how individuals in St Mary Parish and elsewhere understand the parodied emergency information that they experienced. Does the perfidious mechanism present in these incidents resonate with lay audiences? In other words, do individuals *understand* these incidents to be in some way perfidious? Or do they instead evaluate these events based on the immediate outcomes, where no harm means no foul? Survey experiments and interviews can be helpful in untangling public reactions.

By exploring the normative frameworks being used by populations to identify and evaluate these incidents, we can also take a step toward measuring the full effects of imposter content. If people shrug off imposter content as simply annoying or even as clever, is the disinformation campaign less effective in the long term? What is the relationship between individual interpretations of imposter content, and long-term trust in established emergency communication systems? This paper charts several unanswered questions; as is often the case, more dedicated research is needed to understand the effects and interpretations of imposter content campaigns.
