[**7**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp07)

## [The Challenges Associated with AI’s Military Innovation *Examining Practitioner Perspectives in the United Kingdom*](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp07)

*Amy Ertan*^([1](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07.ftn.001))

**W**HAT ARE THE PRESSING concerns around technology enabled by artificial intelligence (AI) in military applications? To what extent are these concerns being addressed in the current development and deployment of emerging technology in defence contexts? This chapter aims to respond to these questions by drawing on the analysis of interviews held in 2020 with primarily UK-based industry experts in AI military innovation.^([2](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07.ftn.002)) Based on this analysis, the chapter explores how the problematic framing of an ‘AI arms race’ currently drives the supply and demand for military innovation in the UK defence sector. It examines key innovation challenges, including perceived limitations of the public–private procurement model for emerging technology, open questions around testing and evaluating advanced learning algorithms, and the complex debates surrounding AI autonomy in conflict environments. The chapter explores the incentives of organisations and highlights how the status quo results in significant security gaps in which military and defence departments do not have the requisite knowledge to demand security assurance deemed desirable by industry experts. This security gap highlights a role for intergovernmental actors such as NATO to act as facilitators for norms-building activity, to mitigate the range of security challenges highlighted in current innovative practice.

The military interest in AI-enabled technology has been well noted (Scharre and Horowitz 2018; Hoadley and Lucas 2018; NATO Science & Technology Organization 2020). The opportunities afforded by emerging technologies such as artificial intelligence offer a competitive advantage in warfare. Having more information or more accurate capabilities offers an edge that marks technical superiority in conflict. A number of states are investing significantly in military AI capabilities, most prominently the United States, the first country to release a defence-focused AI strategy (US Department of Defense 2019), as well as Russia (Hoadley and Lucas 2018) and China (Kania 2017). NATO has announced the launch of an AI strategy due in the summer of 2021 to help coordinate military innovation and responsible use of AI across the Alliance (Sprenger 2021). This chapter explores the military AI innovation landscape across the United Kingdom in which, at the time of submission, no approach to military AI innovation had yet been codified in an unclassified format. In the absence of formal doctrines or policy outlines, this research project explored the dynamics of current innovative practices and the corresponding security challenges through the perspectives of expert practitioners in the field. By interviewing decision-makers, including senior colleagues running AI research programmes in the commercial defence sector, this chapter benefits from the access and reflections of those most informed on the dynamics of UK innovations in AI.

### The UK Context

The UK national AI strategy, titled the ‘AI Sector Deal’ (HM Government 2019), focuses largely on the industrial strategy in developing AI talent and an AI-focused industry sector. The United Kingdom does have the AI Council which published an ‘AI Road Map’ in January 2021, aimed at guiding the UK’s strategic direction regarding AI. At the time of submission, the UK had not yet published a defence-specific AI strategy, though it announced its intention to do so in March 2021 (Ministry of Defence 2021, 42). Within the UK Ministry of Defence (MoD), the focal point for AI research has been the AI Lab, situated within the Defence Science and Technology Laboratory (Dstl). The AI Lab, created in 2018 and based just outside Salisbury, is intended to ‘enhance and accelerate the UK’s world-class capability in the application of AI-related technologies to defence and security challenges’(Ministry of Defence 2018), and in April 2021 announced the creation of a 15-person Dstl unit within the National Innovation Centre for Data (NICD) in Newcastle, focusing specifically on data analytics and collaboration with the private sector (Defence Science and Technology Laboratory \[Dstl\] 2021a). Dstl has also released and maintains ‘The Biscuit Book’, a public document intended to outline the key concepts of AI and data science for MoD customers (Defence Science and Technology Laboratory 2021b). Within another part of the MoD, the Defence and Security Accelerator (DASA) has committed to allocating £4 million investment into AI defence projects (Ministry of Defence, Defence and Security Accelerator, and Heappey 2020), split across several early-stage enterprises over a two-year period. Such investment may seem negligible compared to the declarations of huge investment sums by US counterparts; the FY2021 US Department of Defense (DoD) budget request includes $800 million to AI, complementing the $927 million dedicated to the DoD Joint Artificial Intelligence Centre in 2020 (Konaev et al. 2020, 6; Office of the Under Secretary of Defense 2020). Nonetheless, the UK’s strengths across other domains have shown that financial investment in real terms is not the only factor determining the success or failure of capability building, with the UK evaluated as world’s third cyber power in National Cyber Power Index 2020, a comparative research study focusing on cyber intent and capabilities indicators (Voo et al. 2020).

In November 2020 the UK government announced the creation of an ‘Defence Centre for Artificial Intelligence’ within the MoD, as part of a £400 million defence spending boost. The *Integrated Review* states the Centre will act as the ‘nucleus to accelerate the development and exploitation of these critical technologies from the battlespace to the back office’, coordinating defence innovation from the development of common AI platforms, toolkits and best practices, implementing data management techniques, and testing and validating emerging capabilities (Ministry of Defence 2021, 42). At the time of this writing, few further details have been released on the scope and structure of the Centre.

### Research Aim and Methodology

The UK’s Defence Technology Framework broadly defines AI as ‘the ability of machines to perform tasks normally requiring human intelligence’ that is ‘expected to enable radical transformation across almost every area of Defence activity’ (Ministry of Defence 2019, 18). While this framework lists a range of potential defence applications from AI-enabled cyber defence to intelligence analysis to streamlining logistics and back-office operations, and states that developments will need to be ‘safe, ethical and interoperable with other nations’ (Ministry of Defence 2019, 19), there are currently few further details on how relevant UK decision-makers are approaching military AI innovation at an operational or strategic level.

Similarly, while there is a growing area of literature focusing on the range of implications of AI in national security or defence, there is limited academic scholarship focusing specifically on the UK approach to military AI innovation.^([3](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07.ftn.003)) Primarily, strategic policy analysis is highly focused on themes including US–China competition (Kania 2017; Imbrie and Kania 2019; Scharre and Horowitz 2018), and the broader strategic lens through which innovation is perceived as a race for dominance. The pace of innovation and perceptions on how adversaries may use AI technologies often invokes the term ‘AI arms race’ dynamics (Hoadley and Lucas 2018), through the phrase has been criticised for over-simplifying a complex competitive landscape (Roff 2019; Imbrie et al. 2021). Particularly as states with more capabilities take the lead in developing military AI capabilities, strategic concerns include a growing capability gap between states that can or cannot utilise AI capabilities, impacting interoperability between alliances such as NATO and weakening collective defence opportunities (Fiott 2017; Gilli et al. 2020). Beyond government documents such as the *Integrated Review*, there was little additional reflection found in academic literature on how the UK perceives itself in the competitive military AI landscape.

Most of the literature detailing states’ approaches to military AI development is similarly weighted to an analysis of US innovation (Konaev et al. 2020; Hoadley and Lucas 2018), which is to be expected given the relatively higher information released by the US government, including the Department of Defence AI strategy. Besides the United States, France is the only state to have released a defence-focused strategic AI document which, in addition to highlighting their strategic perspectives, explicitly makes references to planned research on key innovation concepts such as security, trust, testing and standardisation (Ministère des Armées 2019). While there was no specific equivalent detailed outline of UK AI strategy, Taylor (2019) reflects on the UK defence procurement processes, highlighting how AI procurement is likely to diverge from existing MoD procurement aspects in light of requirements, market competition, testing and verification, and the required speed of decision-making. Reflecting on the broad international innovation landscape, Burton and Soare (2019) reflect that effective military innovation will rely on greater civil-military fusion, highlighting that states such as China enjoy a relative advantage compared with the United States and Europe due to greater control of industry assets, including data.

In light of the relative lack of available analysis focusing specifically on the UK’s evolving approach to military AI capability-building, this chapter focuses on the research question: *What are the underlying drivers for the development of military ‘AI’ technology within the UK?* with the research aiming to explore the dynamics surrounding the development of military AI-enabled systems and the alleged ‘arms race’ that is happening in artificial intelligence. Seventeen interviews were conducted with senior-level defence experts at large defence organisations, all of whom had significant experience with determining the direction of AI innovation within their organisation, and all but two of whom were established suppliers to the UK MoD and UK military. The fact that the majority of Dstl’s activity lies behind a security clearance requirement reflects the challenges of observing covert environments such as defence innovation units (González 2012). Rather than applying potentially ill-fitting existing theories to a dynamic and secretive landscape, these interviews relied on a grounded theory approach, in which a researcher draws out concepts and social patterns from the collected data (Glaser 2002). This chapter will focus on the major themes that emerged through an analysis of the interview data: market dynamics (the supply and demand of AI technology), security challenges, risk management and perspectives on risk mitigation.^([4](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07.ftn.004))

### Market Dynamics (AI Technology Supply and Demand)

> *It’s a very complicated relationship. Because they need us to give them certain things because they can’t—they don’t have in-house capability to manufacture. But at the same time, they are only a small part of our customer base*. (P9, on the relationship between the UK defence sector and the MoD).

Interviewees spoke at length about how the market model has changed when it comes to military technology, especially at the cutting edge. On the one hand, military budgets have fallen in real terms compared with the Cold War era, reducing the power of governments as price-makers in the defence space rather than their former monopsony status. On the other, the rise of nontraditional providers of technology for the defence space has changed the layout of the market. Global technology conglomerates like Google, IBM, Microsoft and Amazon (the four firms most frequently mentioned by respondents, followed by firms like Facebook) now have the *budget* and *data* to invest in AI applications. These firms do not contain governments or defence departments as a core part of their business model; their products may be more lucratively marketed towards a commercial audience. There may instead be several *disincentives* for these commercial entities to engage in military projects. Several respondents referred to Project Maven and the public controversy that resulted in Google refusing to renew their contract to work with the US DoD on an image recognition programme.

> *You’ll get paid if you meet the functional requirements*. (P14)

Interviewees described the procurement and supplier market as largely requirements-based; the product is built according to predefined requirements. Respondents were of the view that the UK MoD found price to be a major factor when awarding a contract between tenders, which encourages suppliers to provide competitive quotes. In turn, suppliers are incentivised to focus on the functional requirements (i.e., key purpose and aspects of the technology to be provided) rather than nonfunctional requirements such as security, which add time and cost to product delivery quotes.

> *Been a huge amount of pressure from MoD over where the defence supply chain represents value for money, and whether they’re being screwed over on contracts, and it’s led to this kind of sort of mindset that says that the people supplying MoD shouldn’t really be profitable. You know, they should have real limits on how much profit they can make, they should take all of the risk and MoD should be left with none of the risk, and—but we still want them to innovate*. (P2)

There was a perceived lack of financial support available to defence firms to dedicate to nonfunctional requirements within a product delivery. This dynamic was explained as an effect of requirements-based development practices which did not allow for emerging technologies to be introduced once the project requirements had been finalised (though multiple respondents did mention internal attempts to employ more flexible methodologies such as Agile and DevOps). Respondents felt that being price competitive meant focusing only on the functional requirements, though this was also compounded by the fact that when payment hinges on the delivery of functional requirements alone, there is no incentive to provide additional security-related services. Tensions between the private sector and the UK MoD also often mentioned the restrictions that come alongside working with the state. Procurement contracts with the MoD typically mean the defence supplier does not control the intellectual property for the developed product, which then limits the commercial resale of the tools. This limitation is to prevent adversaries accessing the technology should it be commercially available; however, this presents a challenge for innovating actors that may view defence provision as a limitation on total sales. One respondent (P16) stated: ‘*If I can only sell ten items to the MoD but I could sell it a hundred times privately—why would I restrict my market?*’

Given that most of the respondents within this project were employed at the ‘defence primes’, large established defence corporations representing long-standing suppliers both to the UK MoD and abroad, it is perhaps expected that they believed the role of defence primes were there to stay. Particularly given the consensus among respondents that the MoD often struggled to onboard small/medium enterprises (which may include specialised AI startups) as part of the procurement process, defence primes believed they offered assurance as a party that may acquire or work with a startup to develop technology in a process that interoperates with the MoD innovation process. When asked about the impact of major technology companies refusing to engage with military innovation, respondents also saw the role of defence primes as preventing a ‘race to the bottom’ of AI standards. The overwhelming justification for this view was that defence primes have already come to terms with the nature of warfare and their role in enhancing the UK’s warfighting capabilities in the name of national security.

### Risk Management

> *They* \[MoD\] *send policy people well, very careful about positioning the PR space, they don’t send the engineers . . . but really we need the engineers to talk to engineers*. (P14)
> 
> *I think that companies are failing on their responsibility here*. (P16)

Risk awareness was a major theme highlighted by respondents. The respondents were asked a series of questions that aimed to identify aspects of residual risk (defined as the total risk minus the impact of security controls). Starting with the understanding that risk is compiled of threat (an actor or capability with the potential to cause harm to a system) and vulnerability (a weakness in an asset that may be exploited), AI was clearly understood by the experts as a technology that could represent a threat (it may conceivably be used to facilitate offensive activity) or a vulnerability (the AI system may be compromised and subverted). There was a consensus view that the demand side—the MoD—was not demanding higher AI verification assurances since decision-makers were not familiar enough with AI technology to set out their requirements in these terms. In particular, the shortage of data scientists and staff who can challenge technical aspects of a product’s design were highlighted as skill sets in short supply.

> *I think there is a general lack of understanding within the MoD about what they are asking for and what some of the consequences of it \[AI technologies\] are. They see the upside, they don’t necessarily focus on the limitations or . . . the delta between what they are asking for—what is possible today–and the other stuff that they are going to have to do to bridge the gap*. (P16)

Respondents frequently used the term ‘skills gap’ to explain the lack of informed decision-making that they perceived happening throughout the procurement process. One respondent described their demand-side clients as ‘amateurs in the neutral sense’: while the respondent had spent decades of their career developing their knowledge of data science and machine learning techniques, their MoD equivalent did not have this background. Risk management through the design process was referenced or alluded to in every interview, despite risk never being raised specifically in the question wording. The current innovation process was highlighted as being incredibly difficult to manage when it comes to accurately monitoring risks specifically related to AI technology. One respondent, working for a defence prime, described the challenge of ‘explainable AI’, which was currently in the early concept testing stages.

### Designing Security into Product Development Processes

> *Accountability, transparency . . . all that kind of stuff . . . it adds some time. . . . But again, if you’re not worried about that sort of thing, or if you’re not as worried about the side effects if you get it wrong, you can lash that kind of thing pretty quickly*. (P2)
> 
> *In true human style, I think we pick and choose what suits our narratives. If you’ve got someone very keen to do AI, they’re going to find all the reasons why AI is brilliant. . . . I have these arguments on a daily basis with one particularly clever AI developer. He lays out what he’s doing, how he’s doing it, and I point out ‘you’re asking for a friendly fire incident’. (P14)*

Several interviewees responded that there were active projects underway within the UK landscape to address the challenges of explainable AI, or effective validation and verification of complex software systems before deployment of complex AI. At the same time, no respondent could point to specific testing and evaluation capabilities that analysed the AI aspect of the software product (beyond the standard software testing life-cycle process). For example, no respondents reported standard oversight mechanisms or retesting schedules for AI-enabled products to check that they continued to operate as intended post-deployment. This ties in with market supply; there was no incentive to go ‘above and beyond’ and perform a more expensive service when it was generally understood that the MoD awarded contracts with price competitiveness as a priority factor. The lack of accountability for private innovation (both within and beyond the defence sector) was cited as a concern by one respondent, who noted:

> *I think the greatest threat with non-state actors and corporations having so much power is that they do not inhabit an organisational space where they feel responsibility for their actions the way a nation usually does . . . there is no International Criminal Court for them. (P13)*.

There was a consensus across the board that AI was too immature to be deployed anywhere but the most banal applications across the military currently—due to the number of unanswered questions and security concerns. That said, several major examples were raised of active AI projects, including Programme Nelson (a major active project promoting enhanced data analytics and AI across the British Royal Navy (‘Digital Marketplace’ 2018). One respondent raised the view that at their organisation, themes such as responsibility and trust were often ‘lumped with human factors’, with nontechnical tasks being othered and understudied compared to the colleagues directly building the product to specification. Perhaps one unexpected point was how often participants mentioned the importance of individual decision-makers (given that the questions were usually strategic with state actors as reference objects). Over half of the respondents mentioned silos, or the proposed benefits that would come with cross-expertise input. The recognition of how different perspectives may help predict and proactively mitigate associated AI deployment challenges was reported to have a recent but growing awareness by several respondents:

> *It is absolutely imperative to have, and more urgent to have, diversity in leadership and in tech. (P7)*
> 
> *If there’s one thing that we need the ability to do, it is the blended diversity of teams with diverse skill sets. We’re able to do reviews of projects and go ‘okay we see the benefits and risks of this project being here’*. (P17)

### Security Challenges

> *The people that we are preparing ourselves to have future battles with, to be frank, do not have the same cultural ethical or moral constraints that we set ourselves or we’ve developed. And we need to find a way of dealing with that*. (P8)
> 
> *We’re not going to have one kind of AI. AI is going to diversify, become heterogeneous, the way it learns will be different. ’Cause it will have been created by a different culture.” (P13)*

The technical challenges to securing AI systems were mentioned across interviews: the importance of basic cyber hygiene to protect the integrity of an AI system, including the algorithm and corresponding training data across the deployment lifecycle; adversarial AI techniques which aim to subvert AI systems (for example, tricking an image recognition model into misidentifying a genuine innocuous object as a weapon, which has already been demonstrated in practice (Athalye et al. 2018); and data bias or inaccuracy in the data. However, the more intractable challenges centred around complex factors including escalating pressure to innovate (with AI arms race dynamics), fragmented innovation practices, and the perceived challenges of AI operating in incredibly unpredictable environments in which the laws of engagement are frequently changing. Several geopolitical factors were an overarching theme to the security challenges discussed. Almost every respondent felt that there was an ‘AI arms race’, and those who were less fixed on their answer instead stressed the fluidity of international competition between military capabilities (‘*An arms race? Maybe not now, but in five minutes? Who knows?*’ \[P4\]). There were many different conceptions of what an ‘AI arms race’ might be: whether a conventional arms race without traditional arms, whether there was enough evidence to prove or disprove the dynamics’ existence, or whether it is occurring simultaneously in many different spheres at once (i.e., healthcare, military, or in investment terms). The phrase represented vastly different phenomena to respondents, despite respondents having broadly similar roles and remits.

> *How might it work to push out technology where the operational constraints are different between countries? I know the US and the UK have different rules of engagement and then of course when you consider non-ally states, I’m not sure what the right phrase is, but states with very different thresholds. (P12)*
> 
> *There are no clear guidelines at the moment. (P13)*

The lack of a best-practice methodology was considered an entrenched characteristic of AI. Artificial Intelligence was described as a product of a particular culture; the AI technology developed in China will have different assumptions, characteristics, and goals compared with related AI applications developed in Russia, the UK, and around the world. This posed a security problem to respondents as it made it difficult to anticipate and allow alignment between systems. The distinction between the technologies represents distinctions at the doctrinal level between states. The UK conducts warfare in a different way to its adversaries. The rules of engagement (RoE) differ between alliances, states, and individual conflict scenarios and are considered ‘*massively contextual. . . . Every operation has a different legal framework . . . RoE is a minefield*’ (P9). NATO has published a set of rules of engagement, MC 362-1, that defines ‘the circumstances, conditions, degree, and manner in which the use of force, or actions which might be construed as provocative, may be applied’ (NATO 2003). However, it has been argued that the lack of clarity of certain concepts leads to ‘ambiguity \[which may\] may be detrimental for people involved and for mission accomplishment’ (Cooper 2019). The respondents did not know how AI systems could be universally bound by international norms or clear mandates within such a fluid operational landscape. As one respondent (P1) remarked on diverting state practice and accepted use of AI, ‘*This is a social policy issue, not a technical policy issue*’.

### Perspectives on Risk Mitigation and Discussion

Having identified the various layers of risk that emerge when examining the military AI innovation landscape, a review of mitigations proposed by respondents is categorised into four broad themes: international norms, industry standards export controls, and regulation.

1.  *International Norms:* International agreements, perhaps facilitated by intergovernmental organisations such as NATO, the Organisation of Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD), the United Nations or UN-adjacent bodies were raised as key institutions that hold the capabilities for the development of consensus and norms building among allies. From the intelligence-sharing trust networks, such as Five Eyes, to economic mechanisms, such as the Group of 7, Group of 20 or World Economic Forum, interviewees saw consensus-building as an activity that could take place in a variety of arenas.
2.  *Industry Standards:* Offering resources to defence companies to help them assess the risk and highlight potential mitigations associated with different applications of AI technology which may include software or risk tools, or attempts to reduce silos and ensure a range of perspectives (including legal and human factors) inputs into AI development processes.
3.  *Export Controls:* Export controls for cryptographic tools and systems shows how export controls may be attempted over software. The exact enforcement mechanisms were not outlined. One respondent (P1) articulated a perceived challenge to effective export controls by comparing AI-enabled technology to liquid helium, stating, ‘*It’s hard to contain, it’s not like an aircraft carrier where you can say “I have one, you don’t have one*”’.
4.  *Regulation:* The 2018 EU General Data Protection Regulation is generally given as an example of how long effective regulation can take to filter through to state legislation; an undesirable characteristic, given the rapid pace of innovation across AI as a whole. Legislation was highlighted as being a too slow and bureaucratic option. Regulation of the AI industry was also deemed undesirable in an economic and trade sense; it is assumed that organisations will move to regions with less regulation, which would quell any hopes of developing a UK hub for AI activity across commercial spheres. It should be noted that three respondents did not offer any optimism on reducing the probabilities of the above security challenges presenting itself. Using the same analogy of Pandora’s box, more than one person argued that they did not see a way to feasibly slow the spread of arms race dynamics. For cyber-AI capabilities, the most one could do was to increase cyber hygiene and hope offensive AI systems would not penetrate traditional defences. Across other domains, the answer was less clear.

These interviews revealed a consensus across several aspects: that generally experts do perceive arms race dynamics in the military space, with a mixture of proactive and reactive action underway. For all the talk of proactive action, the ultimate direction of AI appeared to be guided largely by the perceptions of ‘adversarial states’. The pressure to innovate in the military space kept many of my interview respondents employed, a fact many of them highlighted themselves. Their reflections on the military AI landscape in the UK were pragmatic; no interviewee made grandiose claims about UK superiority, and no interviewee said that the UK was world-leading. As colleagues who were often coordinating AI research (or research on other emerging technologies) at large defence companies, these interviewees were most of the time very technical, and the majority of them were not used to making strategic speeches in a spokesperson-like manner.

Long-term implications of AI arms-building may result in asymmetric capabilities between allies or within alliances such as NATO, raising issues of burden-sharing as the technological capability gap grows. Valášek (2019, 10) argues that ‘the impact on automated warfare on alliance politics, while poorly understood, need not be destabilizing’. Valášek highlights the possible roles for NATO to encourage unity between the alliance and shift innovation responses above ‘the national level’. While NATO within multiple interviews was seen as a body that could assist in mitigating practitioner-perceived challenges, few respondents had any suggestions as to how NATO may be able to achieve mitigation goals. No respondents provided critical reflections on the current national-level responses, whereas this is to be expected from a methodology that focuses on capturing the UK’s innovation landscape. Nonetheless, the absence of suggestions on international cooperation—and burden sharing as mentioned by Valášek (2019)—suggests that states are taking an inward-looking approach to innovation. This is further supported by the fact that no respondents stated that they had specifically looked at the innovating strategies of other states when determining their AI strategy (instead, respondents specifically looked at active research ‘themes’ in AI, such as decision assistance or image processing). Rather, it was perceptions of potential adversarial usage of AI—the fact that another state could use ‘machine speed warfare’ to achieve a competitive advantage—that prompted discussions on how far autonomous AI-enabled systems should be integrated in military operations. Only one respondent claimed familiarity with the US DoD’s AI strategy.

The interviews highlighted several themes of residual risk that exist across the UK military innovation landscape, specifically around AI. Highlighting the range of technical and strategic security challenges in the deployment challenges coupled with the lack of an agreed-upon industry or MoD top-down best practice verification tool for military AI systems, the narrative that emerges from the research interviews is one in which there is a lack of high-level oversight into secure AI development. The immaturity of complex AI systems (as described by respondents) only highlights the opportunity to implement an oversight and monitoring system in time of greater and more frequent deployments. However, the market is currently not structured to incentivise this investment in security; often the MoD does not have the technical skill set to demand a higher threshold of security-centred AI technology.

The landscape of defence innovation has also changed dramatically over the last century, an aspect respondents reflected on frequently throughout the interviews. The rise of ‘disruptive innovation’ from the commercial sector marks a change from the early nineteenth century (Bellais and Fiott 2017). A review of recent academic literature highlighted that a significant number of publications framed AI innovation as an ‘arms race’ to technical superiority, whereas the proportion of articles framing AI as a competition is declining (Imbrie, Kania, and Laskai 2020). This decline does not seem to be replicated across the (small but expert) respondent group, all of whom answered that they believed arms race dynamics were present across the military AI landscape. Whether this race formed part of a wider economic competition between states, or whether interviewees perceived elements of a ‘race to the bottom’ dynamic specifically where militaries felt the need to remain competitive against adversaries in conflict, external factors contributed to the need to develop AI-enabled technology. The shift through which innovation was primarily developed by the private sector, and often with private-sector data, raises significant challenges around accountability and responsibility. Respondent perspectives on the AI arms race, alongside their noted views on under-informed decision-makers, are in line with research that suggests avoiding discussions of ‘AI arms race’ dynamics and instead focusing on clearer language that addresses the nuances of AI capabilities and competitive dynamics (Roff 2019).

Boiling down responses on the overall landscape to their simplest parts: governments are less advantaged than they may once have been. Smaller budgets and the politicisation of defence mean that the government does not have the pick of the technology they’re looking for. This is coupled with twenty-first-century hype around AI, and the geopolitical pressure to maintain a comparative technical advantage, which ultimately leads to a leadership’s push for greater development of AI capabilities at a potentially undetermined cost to several aspects of existing security. This search for technical superiority is not new (though we can view it explicitly through the US Third Offset Strategy \[Fiott 2017\]), but the speed at which technology evolves certainly appears so. Bellais and Fiott (2017) comment on how before World War I, a soldier could have used the same kit over decades and throughout their career; it is hard to imagine today’s recruits using the same technology in thirty years’ time.

The reduction in relative market-maker power by the militaries, in line with the rapid rise in influence by private sector suppliers, represents a significant shift in the power dynamic compared with military innovation dynamics a century ago. Regulations, where not applied globally, are likely to simply encourage private actors to move operations elsewhere. Given the relative economic clout of many larger technology companies, it is in a state’s interest to avoid excessive regulation of companies and to keep the industry’s interest in the local economy. Judging from the respondents’ uncertainty, and amid wider academic and public discussions around military innovation, it appears that while the acknowledgement of security challenges around military AI are often discussed, very little is agreed upon in terms of mitigation options.

### Conclusion and Areas for Future Research

This project’s focus on the UK landscape highlighted numerous concerns raised by expert respondents. It also highlighted areas in which the interviewees did not consider themselves sufficiently informed to advise on the best mitigation approaches or near-medium term strategies towards security management of military AI. When asked about their views on potential solutions, a range of answers were recorded from explicit pessimism, such as statements like ‘*Norms are dead, norms-building will not work in 2020*’ (P11) to caveated postulating: ‘*I don’t really know, it’s like the cybersecurity problem*’ (P17). Similar to the gaps highlighted by participants, the reticence to suggest or reflect on potential solutions highlights not only how intractable and complex security management is in this space, but also how underdeveloped the defence discourse space is at the time of this research. In the absence of any best practice—or even prototype best-practice approaches—it might be wise to examine the activity and potential contributions of other actors in this space. The activity of international actors may contribute to a top-down approach across the UK military AI landscape. For this reason, future research projects should focus on the role of NATO, asking questions like ‘What are the strategic security implications of AI in conflict?’ and ‘What are the capabilities and possible roles for NATO in mitigating potential insecurities created by weaponised AI technology?’

The sentiments revealed through these expert interviews reveal several security gaps and opportunities relating to military AI innovation. In drawing together various forms of residual risks perceived by expert practitioners, we have moved from a series of ‘unknown unknown’ in which security and AI are not considered interlinked at software, economic market, and strategic levels. As we highlighted the security implications of current innovative practice, we also learned about possible methods to mitigate existing risk. Regulation is not wholly dismissed as an option: rather the role for export controls or the development of international norms are seen as potential factors that may nudge state behaviour in this space. Experts in the space candidly state that the current innovation landscape raises more questions than answers when it comes to managing the security implications of military AI-enabled technology, highlighting the need for a deeper exploration of potential risk mitigators for identified challenges.

### Notes

[1](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07r.ftn.001). The contents of this chapter do not represent the official views of the authors current or former employers. All views are the author’s own.

[2](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07r.ftn.002). The interviews for this research took place between February and March 2020, and this article was submitted in May 2021. Since this date the UK has substantially evolved its position on AI in defence, most notably through the release of the UK Defence AI Strategy in June 2022. This article therefore offers a snapshot of expert perspectives in the UK prior to the emergence of a formal strategic direction on AI in military innovation.

[3](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07r.ftn.003). At a policy level, the Center for a New American Security (CNAS) and the Center for Security and Emerging Challenges (CSET) regularly publish research papers regarding artificial intelligence in a military context, with a primarily focus on US priorities. For more information, see [https://www.cnas.org/artificial-intelligence-and-global-security](https://www.cnas.org/artificial-intelligence-and-global-security) and [https://cset.georgetown.edu/publications/](https://cset.georgetown.edu/publications/).

[4](#Chapter07.xhtml#ch07r.ftn.004). These themes emerged through qualitative coding of the interview transcripts through NVivo, using an inductive coding methodology. For more information, please see Saldaña’s summary of descriptive coding in Saldaña (2013).

[**III**](../Text/nav.xhtml#prt03)

# [EMERGING ACTORS](../Text/nav.xhtml#prt03)

 

[**8**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp08)

## [The European Normative Venture for ‘Technological Sovereignty’ in Cyberspace](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp08)

*Cedric Amon*

**I**N RECENT YEARS, many states resorted to techno-nationalist practices and claims of digital sovereignty to impose legislation in the online sphere. A sovereign internet is usually viewed as a threat to the global, free and open internet. This fear is based on attempts to modify the global internet architecture and the creation of quasi-national internets by states such as China and Russia. At first glance, the European Commission’s efforts to achieve ‘technological sovereignty’ may therefore have placed the European Union in unusual company. This chapter offers an analysis of key features that distinguish the European sovereignty model from the authoritarian interpretation of digital sovereignty. It argues that the EU’s sovereignty venture must be understood as a political response to the growing dependence on non-European technology providers. A brief examination of Germany’s and France’s sovereignty approach offers additional insights into the concerns and ambitions linked to European technological sovereignty. Hence, the Commission relies on its normative arsenal to (re-)establish a level playing field in the European Digital Single Market. Moreover, the European sovereignty strategy aims to assert strategic (political) autonomy in online matters by expanding its cyber diplomacy and cybersecurity toolboxes.

The concept of sovereignty in cyberspace has accompanied the development of the internet since the early days of its invention. Accordingly, the question of state sovereignty in the digital sphere has been raised numerous times over the years. On the state level, the notion of sovereignty in cyberspace is often associated with proposals such as Russia’s nationally routed ‘RuNet’ or China’s Great Firewall, a heavily state-monitored gateway into a quasi-national cyberspace. These techno-nationalist ventures are usually justified by peoples’ right to self-determination and states’ responsibilities to guarantee the safety of its subordinates. These interpretations correspond to Milton Mueller’s observation, according to which there is a trend of online jurisdictions being claimed along analogue national borders (Mueller 2017a). Although not expressly carried out under the flag of digital sovereignty, the United States is not immune to techno-nationalist tendencies either. The introduction of the Clarifying Overseas Use of Data Act (CLOUD Act \[H.R. 4943 2018\]) or the bans of Chinese network technologies are just a few examples in that regard.

Lately, the concept of a digitally sovereign Europe became increasingly popular. The European Commission and its member states adopted a series of digitally related norms to “protect the fundamental rights and freedoms of natural persons, in particular their right to privacy with regard to the processing of personal data, and the need to protect critical infrastructure in the EU” (German Federal Foreign Office 2021). The introduction of initiatives and regulations such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) in 2018 (EUR-Lex 2016), the Cyber Diplomacy Toolbox (EU Institute for Security Studies 2019) and the Cybersecurity Act in 2019 (EUR-Lex 2019) reflects the European trend of norm creation in support of its “technological sovereignty” ambitions (European Commission 2020a). The Commission’s digital agenda—‘Shaping Europe’s digital future’—as well as the European priorities 2019–2024—‘A Europe fit for the digital age’—provide additional insights into a fierce competition of visions for the cyberspace of tomorrow.

Shedding light on this issue, the chapter will discuss the concept of digital sovereignty from a state-centric point of view. Hereafter, it will provide an overview of the key enabling features which fed into Europe’s norm-driven strategy. The specificity of the European ‘technological sovereignty’ will be highlighted through case studies of Germany’s and France’s views on the topic. Finally, the chapter will present the ways in which the European Commission is leveraging its “normative power” (Manners 2002) to shape the current governance model with respect to its founding principles. The research is based on qualitative content analysis of policy documents, political statements, and press releases from France’s and Germany’s relevant ministries and their officials as well as from the European Commission from 2013 to 2020. The two countries were selected based on their strong influence on European politics and the author’s ability to interpret documentation in their respective languages. The case studies are not intended to provide an exhaustive overview of the countries’ digital sovereignty argumentations. They rather serve to identify the strongest themes from each of the countries which are relevant for the European policy debates.

### The Elusive Definition of Digital Sovereignty

Claims regarding sovereignty in cyberspace can be traced back to the 1990s and are commonly qualified as data, technological, cyber, or digital sovereignty. These terms are often used interchangeably although they do not necessarily refer to the same issue areas. On the one hand, internet libertarians such as John Perry Barlow and John Gilmore proclaimed the independence of cyberspace in 1996 (Barlow 1996) and tried to fend off (governmental) control over the internet (Couture and Toupin 2019). On the other hand, states such as the Russian Federation pushed for the recognition of sovereignty and territorial integrity in the ‘information sphere’ in its ‘Information Security Doctrine’ in 2000 (Russian Federation 2000). The choice of the term ‘information sphere’ is important because it goes beyond the definition of cyberspace.^([1](#Chapter08.xhtml#ch08.ftn.001)) The latter is in fact ‘a subset of the larger information sphere domain’ according to Patrick Allen and Dennis Gilbert (2009, 137). The question of sovereignty and jurisdiction is being studied by various actors of different cyber-related fields. In general, sovereignty in the digital context refers to the assertion of ‘some form of collective control of digital content and/or infrastructures’ (Couture and Toupin 2019). However, the nature of sovereignty and the ways in which it can or should be enforced are often left unspecified. As Duncan Hollis points out, there seems to be agreement over the fact that cyberspace must be governed but there are many unanswered questions as to who should govern it and in which way (Hollis 2012, 12).

From a cybersecurity point of view, the question of jurisdiction is important to determine appropriate responses to cyberattacks. In this regard, the first *Tallinn Manual*’s commentary concedes that ‘a State may exercise control over cyber infrastructure and activities within its sovereign territory’; nevertheless, the commentary also ‘recognizes states’ inability to control or fence cyberspace as a whole (Mueller 2020, 4). Mueller (2017a) attributes this apparent contradiction to increasing security concerns in cyberspace which are accompanied by states’ attempts to conform the cyber domain to national jurisdictions. From an internet governance point of view, the question of sovereignty is key to determining the responsibility of establishing digital norms and enforcing them. Hence, the different scholars concur that “\[t\]he tension between global cyberspace and the territorial state is the primary factor that drives internet governance and cybersecurity debates” (Mueller 2017b). However, the internet’s unique architecture—which grants it resilience through the rerouting of data in case of blockages—challenges the classic notions of territories (and sovereignty thereof) in cyberspace. As such, sovereignty disputes go beyond the interpretation of virtual territories, as they are also concerned with establishing a predominant regulatory regime. As Fabio Cristiano (2019, 33) illustrates, sovereign authority over the ‘online sphere’ can only be ensured if governments also have (some) infrastructural control.

The identification of competing and incompatible governance models for cyberspace further adds to the complexity of the issue (Jamart 2013, 56). According to this assessment, the free and open internet stands for a cyberspace ruled by democratic values (Hohmann and Brenner 2018) while a ‘sovereign cyberspace . . . is inimical to the liberalized information and communications order’ (Mueller 2020, 780). The actions of states such as Russia and China under the guise of digital sovereignty help to understand the apparent incompatibility of the two models. Russia fears that ‘internet freedom and the US doctrine of the “free flow of information” are merely tools that a hegemonic America uses to subvert other states and penetrate them with its own world-view and values’ (Nocetti 2015). Accordingly, it works towards transferring matters of internet governance to intergovernmental organisations, which operate in respect of the Westphalian notion of sovereignty (Nocetti 2015, 118; Claessen 2020, 23). Domestically, Russia introduced ‘several elements aimed at achieving greater government control over the Internet infrastructure’ (Claessen 2020, 23) such as a nationally routed internet called ‘RuNet’. The Chinese government enforces its vision of digital sovereignty based on a similar interpretation of the ‘right of states to govern their respective information space within the confines of domestic legislation’ (Claessen 2020, 23). Although still connected to the global domain name system (DNS), the Great Firewall creates a *de facto* sovereign space which is subject to the country’s national jurisdiction. Furthermore, China’s proposed overhaul of the DNS model shows its persistent efforts to make the internet architecture better abide by sovereign and territorial boundaries (Murgia and Gross 2020; Gross and Murgia 2020). The following section highlights some of the elements preceding the European Commission’s first official mention of ‘technological sovereignty’.

### Enabling Factors for European Technological Sovereignty

On 19 February 2020 the European Commission declared ‘technological sovereignty’ a strategic objective of the 2019–2024 term (European Commission 2020b). The Commission’s strategy was met with concern and taxed as techno-nationalist, especially by observers from the United States (Satariano and Pronczuk 2020; Propp 2019; Bradford and Csernatoni 2021). While some of the critiques qualified the EU’s approach as a turn away from the global and open internet (Manancourt 2020), others deplored a lack of ‘commitment to openness and cooperation’ (Bradford and Csernatoni 2021) in the European model. The criticism, however, falls short of acknowledging the externalities that led to these political measures. Moreover, it does not consider Europe’s insistence on rights protection as well as political and economic autonomy of the past years. The European sovereignty dynamic is influenced by a number of factors such as the defence against cyberattacks or the fear of election meddling. However, two events exemplify the fear of losing the EU’s strategic autonomy: the National Security Agency (NSA) spying affair in 2013 and the introduction of the US CLOUD Act in 2018.

News about the infiltration of European networks for surveillance purposes meant that decision-makers intensified their efforts to secure their infrastructures and the data of their citizens. The political responses ranged from plans to create nationally routed email systems to the hauling of new undersea cables that would not go through US territory (Maurer et al. 2014). The spying case also shed light on the importance of data security for Europeans and paved the way for a first wave of strict(er) data localization requirements.^([2](#Chapter08.xhtml#ch08.ftn.002)) The disclosures occurred amid the reform negotiations of the 1995 Data Protection Directive. Consequently, it can be inferred that the leaks set the tone for high privacy protection standards to be included in the GDPR which was adopted in 2016.

The adoption of the US CLOUD Act in March 2018—two months before the coming into effect of the GDPR—equipped US authorities with the right to request access to data from US companies, even if the data is stored on servers which are not on US territory (US Department of Justice 2019). As such, this law grants US law enforcement unprecedented extraterritorial powers given that most big tech companies are based in the United States and have dominant market positions in the European market. However, such extraterritorial reach puts it at odds with the privacy safeguards put in place by the GDPR (InterCloud 2018; Delacruz and Artzt 2019; Seipp 2020). Additionally, the recent invalidation of the EU–US Privacy Shield Framework (European Court of Justice 2020) and the prior cancellation of its predecessor—the US–EU Safe Harbor Framework (European Court of Justice 2015)—by the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU) highlight the different levels of data protection. The judges annulled the agreement between the US government and the European Union over the transfer of personal data between the parties due to the insufficient standards of data privacy in the United States. Nevertheless, the question of data protection is key for European decision-makers. As we will see in the following case studies, it is regarded as crucial for the safety of privacy, corporate secrets, and national security alike (BMWi 2016; Gauvain et al. 2019; European Commission 2020b).

The timeline shown in [figure 8.1](#Chapter08.xhtml#fig8_1) lists key policies which contributed to the shaping of the EU’s stance on technological sovereignty today. [Table 8.1](#Chapter08.xhtml#tab8_1) at the end of this chapter provides a more comprehensive overview and includes the publication of reports, strategy papers and laws from both France and Germany. It shows the acceleration of policies and strategies being put out following the NSA surveillance scandal and the US CLOUD Act.

### The German Digital Sovereignty Approach

Marked by its own history, Germany evolved into a fervent defender of privacy protection and individual sovereignty (dotmagazine 2017; Cowan 2019; Devins 2017). Observers contend that the sensitivity to privacy protection issues is deeply rooted in the collective memory due to highly efficient surveillance states under the Third Reich and German Democratic Republic (Cowan 2019; Hintz 2013; ‘Surveillance in German History’ 2016). The historical reappraisal of these experiences paved the way for strong legal protections of privacy, including consent requirements for the processing of personal data (German Government 1977) and the recognition of the ‘Fundamental Right to Confidentiality and Probity in Information Technology Systems’ (Bundesverfassungsgericht 1983).

Consequently, the news over the NSA spying affair in 2013 was met with public outrage and a series of political responses (Sargsyan 2016). On 27 July 2013, protesters marched in Berlin, demonstrating against the NSA spying affair, the perceived laxist response of the German government and the alleged involvement of German intelligence services (Al Jazeera 2013; Deutsche Welle 2013; Pfister et al. 2013). Later that year, the freshly elected government dedicated a section of its coalition agreement to the reappraisal of the spying case (Bundesregierung 2013, 104). Interestingly, the agreement also announced measures to ‘regain technological sovereignty’ and welcomed ‘offerings for national or European routing’ (Bundesregierung 2013, 104) aside from initiating a number of digital security reforms to strengthen its networks against outside interventions. These ranged from the creation of trusted vendor labels to the expansion of its cyber defence capacities (Bundesamt für Sicherheit in der Informationstechnik, BSI \[Bundesregierung 2013\]).

Nevertheless, Germany’s digital sovereignty approach goes beyond the mere protection of privacy and ‘individual sovereignty’ (Pohle 2020, 5). Indeed, the German government ascribes (monetary) value to personal information and data (Kelber 2019). Consequently—just as with intellectual property—every user putting out personal data should be able to benefit from it (Kelber 2019; BMWi 2016). Furthermore, the ‘German Digital Strategy’ underlines the importance of trust in the security of data and personal information for the enjoyment of fair competition in the digital market (BMWi 2016). The creation of trust in digital technologies is particularly relevant for the German economy in order to digitise its highly profitable small and medium enterprises (SMEs) (BMWi 2015, 2016).

Another often-cited argument in favour of German and European digital sovereignty pertains to its dependence on foreign technologies. Following the examples of France and the Netherlands, which introduced open source software for their public administrations (PwC Strategy& 2019, 5), the Ministry of Interior launched an investigation into the way of reducing its dependence from (mostly foreign) software providers (PwC Strategy& 2019). Among other issues, the report relates the risks of dependencies to the limited information security provided by non-European providers and their current concentration of market power (PwC Strategy& 2019, 19). The authors state that the continuous growth of digital ecosystems further increases reliance on their products, especially through the introduction of cloud-based platform systems (PwC Strategy& 2019, 14). Another report published by Bitkom (2019, 10), Germany’s federation of organisations in the digital economy, comes to similar conclusions. It therefore portrays digital sovereignty as the middle ground between dependence and autarchy, whereas sovereignty provides Germany with the ability to act autonomously by having competences in key technologies, platforms and services.

### The French Approach to Digital Sovereignty

Aside from concerns about data privacy, the French discourse on digital sovereignty strongly revolves around the nation’s loss of autonomy on the political and economic level (Longuet 2019). First concerns over the dwindling digital sovereignty entered the public sphere in the late 2000s and were prominently linked to Pierre Béllanger, a French entrepreneur, who deplored the loss of digital sovereignty at the cost of US tech companies (Bellanger 2012, 149). Years apart, French senator Catherine Morin-Desailly and Benoit Thieulin shared these concerns. They warned that Europe was on its way to becoming a ‘colony of the digital world’ (Morin-Desailly 2013; Thieulin 2019) because of its increasing technological dependence and the knowledge gap it creates. The governmental ordering of four committees to investigate the nation’s dependent relationships and loss of strategic autonomy in the last year further illustrates the gravity of the matter (Longuet 2019; Gauvain et al. 2019; Thieulin 2019).

Similar to Germany’s strategy, the French national digital strategy of 2015 states that sovereignty must enable the liberty of innovation as well as European competitiveness in the digital field (Gouvernement 2015a). However, French governmental experts point to the dominant market positions of the GAFAM^([3](#Chapter08.xhtml#ch08.ftn.003)) as barriers to market entry and sources of risk for France’s economy (Ministère de l’Europe et des Affaires Étrangères 2018). According to these experts, the fact that these companies are taxed abroad further aggravates the disadvantages of French businesses. Taxation is therefore viewed as a major tool for fairer wealth distribution and equal participation in the digital ecosystem. Consequently, France became the first European government to introduce a digital tax despite the issue still being negotiated at the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) (Gouvernement 2019; Leigh 2020).

Furthermore, French authorities consider technological dependence a major threat to the country’s national security. They are especially concerned about the strong link between economic and national security due to the digitisation of public administration (Tech.Gouv 2019; Assises de la Souveraineté Numérique 2017). The sovereignty strategy thus aims to regain control of (public) information technology systems, software products and critical infrastructures in order to safeguard the nation’s strategic autonomy (Gouvernement 2015b). One of these measures resulted in the incorporation of the French-developed search engine ‘Qwant’ as the Ministry of Defence’s default software (Vie publique 2019). Moreover, the report published by Raphael Gauvain and his colleagues found that dependence on foreign technologies may lead to a severe loss of geopolitical autonomy (Gauvain et al. 2019). According to the report, (over)reliance on others could lead to scenarios in which a technology-providing state makes the availability of its products and services contingent on the alignment to its political actions. This line of argument featured prominently amid the 5G war, where the United States tried to pressure its NATO allies into banning Chinese network technologies on the grounds of alleged network-embedded surveillance mechanisms. The repercussions of anti-Chinese technology restrictions on the global value chain further exposed the risks of technological dependence. Similarly, the parliamentary investigation preceding the adoption of the ‘Loi Huawei’ (République Française 2019) emphasised the importance of the country’s strategic autonomy to avoid getting drawn into broader geopolitical conflicts (Procaccia 2019).

### The EU’s Assertion of Technological Sovereignty

Both France and Germany agree that the success of their digital sovereignty ambitions must be embedded within a European framework. They recognise the importance of overcoming their deficits in the global tech race in order to safeguard EU prosperity and strategic autonomy (von der Leyen 2019; Merkel 2020). Given that the internet and its derivative technologies will undoubtedly be the main driver of wealth in the years to come, Europe must pool its resources to counter the political and economic weight of their main competitors in the field. In line with Manners’ observations regarding European norms diffusion,^([4](#Chapter08.xhtml#ch08.ftn.004)) the European Commission relies on its normative power to shape the digital playing field according to European fundamental values. The global repercussions of the GDPR are a clear example of that power. Amplified by the “Brussels effect” (Bradford 2012, 68), the regulation quickly set the standard for privacy protection far beyond its physical borders. Unlike China and Russia, whose sovereignty strategies target greater infrastructural and content control, the European digital strategy tries to capitalise on a regulated market to break up the quasi-monopoly of big tech companies (Chee 2020; Toko 2019). Accordingly, the Commission aims to enable its citizens by guaranteeing online rights protections and supporting the growth of the European Digital Single Market through “standard setting, tool development . . . as well as build-out of next-generation infrastructures for data processing” (European Commission 2020b, 16).

The insistence on technological sovereignty is based on the recognition that the internet does not fully escape national jurisdictions (Deibert et al. 2012; Mueller 2017a). Indeed, states do have (legal) authority over companies headquartered in their countries. Moreover, they have control over national infrastructures according to legal opinions such as the *Tallinn Manual*. Accordingly, most of the big tech companies, are subject to US—and increasingly Chinese—jurisdiction (Sargsyan 2016), given that foreign market leaders occupy lion’s shares in the EU Digital Single Market and are also rapidly subverting the infrastructural markets such as the DNS service market (Radu and Hausding 2020).

To address concerns about the competitiveness and fair market access in the digital economy, the European Commission proposed the ‘Digital Service Act (DSA)’ and the ‘Digital Market Act (DMA)’ (European Commission 2020c, 2020d). The former will introduce legal certainties in the digital sector through platform liability mechanisms and offer better safeguards for online user rights while the latter intends to break the market concentration of big companies which own the majority of data (‘gatekeepers’). The intended measures of the DMA include preventing the merging of personal information from other services, countering vendor lock-ins in pre-installed apps, and making performance benchmark tools available for clients using gatekeepers’ platforms. In addition to the regulatory breakup of data monopolies, the Commission supports France’s and Germany’s joint initiative of a European trusted cloud architecture governed by European values and regulations (Bundesministerium für Wirtschaft und Energie 2020). Gaia-X is conceptualised around principles such as the ‘right to be forgotten’ and ‘data portability’,^([5](#Chapter08.xhtml#ch08.ftn.005)) which were first introduced by the GDPR, and aims to provide an alternative to proprietary (and non-EU) cloud infrastructures.

In the absence of a global taxation scheme for tech services, the European Commission announced that it could follow the example of some of its member states and introduce a European Digital Service Tax (DST) (Chrysoloras and Horobin 2021). Although negotiations on how to impose digital services of providers, who are not headquartered in a country’s jurisdiction, have been ongoing at the OECD since 2015, an agreement is yet to be reached (OECD 2021; Khanna 2021). One of the main obstacles is the US refusal of a baseline tax within the domestic tax base erosion and profit shifting (BEPS) dispute. The Commission’s stance on the matter includes the reform of corporate tax rules to register and tax profits where ‘businesses have significant interaction with users through digital channels’; the Commission also supports the idea of an interim tax covering digital activities which escape existing tax schemes (European Commission 2018).

Another key addition to the European sovereignty strategy relates to its proposal for a new EU cybersecurity strategy which aims to ‘strengthen the rules-based global order, promote international security and stability in cyberspace’ (European Commission 2020e). It streamlines existing measures to bolster resilience against cyber threats for European citizens, businesses, and infrastructures. Accordingly, it includes reform proposals for the Union’s ‘Directive on security of network and information systems (NIS)’, the intensification of dialogues with multilateral and multistakeholder partners and the creation of a Joint Cyber Unit equipped with deterring capabilities. Additionally, the strategy will enhance the existing EU Cyber Diplomacy Toolbox which equips the EU with instruments to signal its willingness and capacity to react to threats (Auswärtiges Amt 2020). The use of sanctions, a commonly used tool of EU diplomacy, is particularly viewed as a promising way to ‘influence the behaviour of potential aggressors’ (Moret and Pawlak 2017, 1) in the digital sphere. The predilection for cyber sanctions stems from the observation that EU sanctions are often mirrored by other, non-EU countries such as members of the European Free Trade Association (EFTA) or neighbouring countries (Moret and Pawlak 2017; Manners 2002).

Finally, the European strategy to reduce its technological dependence goes beyond concerns and risks in terms of privacy and competitiveness as well as cyber- and national security. The rapid development of new technologies and the lagging political responses have created important policy gaps which are increasing being filled by private actors (De Gregorio and Radu 2020). Consequently, the EU is not only trying to break free from reliance on foreign technology which could be instrumentalized against it; it also must assert its independence against a set of private actors which are exerting *de facto* political power.

### Conclusion

This chapter presented the specificity of the European technological sovereignty strategy and highlighted the differences from other digital sovereignty models. Rather than seeking to isolate itself and modify the current internet architecture, the European sovereignty model relies on its normative power to shape today’s internet governance and to create equal opportunities for its businesses and citizens and to address national security concerns of its member states. The case studies of Germany and France helped situate some of the Commission’s strategic decisions and explain the overall need for a European sovereignty strategy. These decisions are in part built on past experiences such as collective memory of surveillance states which were reignited by the NSA spying affair. Others are based on the growing suspicion regarding extraterritorial provisions such as the US CLOUD Act and the result of national investigations into the dependence of big tech providers. Accordingly, European ‘technological sovereignty’ is seen as a guarantee to act according to its own priorities and without having its actions being dictated by others (Krempl 2020).

The current European Commission has made technological sovereignty its declared goal and therefore projects for the creation of a European data infrastructure are already underway. Although European policy-makers are trying to secure EU strategic autonomy in cyberspace through regulations which can create favourable conditions for the growth of European (tech) companies, they must be mindful not to create an overburdensome regulatory framework that could hinder innovation. Despite these ambitious goals, it remains to be seen whether these efforts will be enough to counter the laws of the internet such as the strong network effects and scalability which comprise much of the big tech’s success.
