[**9**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp09)

## [Views from the Middle *Analysing the Positions of India, Indonesia, and South Africa on Cybersecurity at the UN OEWG*](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp09)

*Irene Poetranto, Josh Gold, and Justin Lau*

**A**S THE WORLD’S NEXT BILLION internet users come online largely from Asia and Africa, the internet’s centre of gravity is shifting from the West to the East. Given this shift, it has become crucial to understand and integrate the viewpoints of non-Western countries in conversations about information and communications technologies (ICTs). This chapter explores the positions of India, Indonesia, and South Africa, as they have been actively involved in United Nations processes for developing rules of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace. In doing so, this chapter contributes to a growing body of literature on this topic that focuses on emerging economies, particularly those that hold a middle ground position. This chapter analyses the three countries’ positions in the following format: first, we provide a short overview of the participation of emerging economies in cyber norms processes; second, we explore some of the major concerns that the three countries have regarding the development and use of ICTs; third, we analyse commonalities and differences in their positions on select key issues at the 2019–2021 UN Open Ended Working Group (OEWG); and fourth, we offer a brief conclusion.

> *I don’t believe this room is entirely divided between just delegations that believe there is a need for an international treaty and those that do not. Some, like South Africa, do occupy a middle ground*.
> 
> —Moliehi Makumane, speaking on behalf of the  
> Republic of South Africa at the OEWG, February 2020

The internet’s centre of gravity is shifting toward the East (Deibert 2012). Between 2019 and 2020, 128 million people came online for the first time in India, while Africa saw significant growth, as the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) increased the number of its internet users by 9 million or 122 percent (Ang 2020). Despite rapid increases in internet usage, Asian and African countries still lack the cybersecurity capacity needed to defend themselves and are more vulnerable to attacks (Portulans Institute 2020a). Recent examples include a cyberattack in May 2021 on Air India, which led to the data breach of 4.5 million passengers, and in June 2020 on a South African group that operates sixty-six hospitals. Meanwhile, Indonesia was one of the largest targets of ransomware attacks in Southeast Asia in 2020 (Bottomley 2020).

Fears that cyberattacks could cause large-scale harm have led UN member states to establish rules for responsible state behaviour in cyberspace. The UN Group of Governmental Experts (GGE) was created in 2004 to study the threats posed by the use of ICTs in the context of international security.^([1](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.001)) GGE members are nation-states selected on the basis of equitable geographical representation. Six GGEs have been convened thus far, and their work has laid the foundation for the ‘rules of the road’ for cyberspace. The 2013 GGE report concluded that international law applies to state actions in cyberspace, and the 2015 GGE report outlined eleven voluntary, non-binding norms for state behaviour in cyberspace (or ‘cyber norms’ for short). The 2017 GGE, however, failed to reach consensus on how exactly international law applies to cyberspace.

Discord among UN member states can be summed up as a tension between two ‘camps’ (Deibert 2015). China, Russia, and their partners aim to create a binding framework that governs state behaviour, while the United States, Canada, and Western European countries argue that states should focus on building on the existing body of international law and cyber norms and establish capacity- and confidence-building measures instead. In December 2018, two parallel processes emerged at the UN to continue deliberations on the ‘rules of the road’ for cyberspace: the 2019–2021 GGE and the inaugural OEWG for 2019–2021.^([2](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.002)) A consensus report was adopted by the OEWG in March 2021, which endorsed the framework set out by previous GGE reports (Gold 2021). Nonetheless, disagreement on fundamental issues remains, resulting in a consensus report that largely fails to address the fundamental causes of global cyber instability today (Basu, Poetranto and Lau 2021).

Although much has been written about the positions held by the two ‘camps’ of states, the viewpoints of emerging economies in cyber norms deliberations have received far less attention. Yet, with the world’s next billion internet users coming online largely from Asia and Africa, several governments on both continents have become key stakeholders in the debates. India has been a GGE member five times, and Indonesia and South Africa three times each (Tikk and Kerttunen 2017). India, Indonesia, and South Africa delivered statements at every session convened by the 2019–2021 OEWG. In addition, Indonesia spoke on behalf of the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM), of which India and South Africa are members.^([3](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.003))

Understanding the perspectives of non-Western stakeholders is crucial to move the implementation of the ‘rules of the road for cyberspace’ forward. In this chapter, we examine the statements made by India, Indonesia, and South Africa at the 2019–2021 UN OEWG to shed light on their concerns and situate them in the context of the challenges in ICT development and use in the respective countries. Unlike GGE deliberations, OEWG’s proceedings are made public. As a result, we were able to review the verbal interventions and written submissions made by UN member states. We analysed written and oral statements that country representatives made at OEWG sessions or those uploaded to the OEWG website, while recordings of OEWG sessions were used to review countries’ statements.^([4](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.004))

We argue that the interaction of domestic and geopolitical factors results in India, Indonesia, and South Africa maintaining a middle ground position at the UN OEWG. Geopolitical factors include tensions with the major powers and their long-standing ‘non-aligned’ status, while domestic challenges include the digital divide and lack of capacity to tackle cyber insecurity (United Nations 2019a). These circumstances are not unique to the three countries, but contribute to emerging economies holding different priorities, values, and attitudes towards discussions on ICTs that are dominated by highly digitalized countries.

### Emerging Economies, Middle Powers, and the ‘Rules of the Road’ for Cyberspace

Disagreement between the two ‘camps’ dates back to the late 1990s when Russia first proposed at the UN a new global legal instrument for managing international information and telecommunications security (Tikk and Kerttunen 2018). Russia, China, and their partners (e.g., Shanghai Cooperation Organization \[SCO\] member states)^([5](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.005)) continue to call for formal treaties to govern state behaviour in cyberspace, while the United States and its allies (known as the ‘like-minded’ countries) maintain that treaties are difficult to negotiate and implement, and are resistant to global agreements that could legitimize authoritarian control of the internet (McKune 2015; Dolman 1979; Nye 2013; Gady and Austin 2010).

China and Russia in turn criticize the ‘like-minded’ states for defying the very norms they promote with considerable effort and resources, that is, conducting mass surveillance and state-sponsored hacking (Kim and Perlin 2019). Beyond the two ‘camps’ is a third cluster of countries, consisting of emerging economies that have not firmly decided which ‘camp’ they will support, the so-called ‘swing states’ (Maurer and Morgus 2014). Thirty countries have been identified as states that could go either way, including Brazil, India, Indonesia, Kenya and South Africa. Of the thirty swing states, twenty-five are either member states or observer states of the NAM. Also, swing states like India, Indonesia, and South Africa are members of the US-proposed 2019–2021 GGE that also voted in support of the Russian-proposed OEWG.

The term ‘digital deciders’ has similarly been used to describe states that influence global internet governance debates but have yet to commit to either side. Of the fifty digital deciders, forty-six states are either member states or observer states of the NAM (Morgus, Woolbright and Sherman 2018). Given the internet’s shifting centre of gravity, the future of state conduct in cyberspace may depend on decisions made by swing states/digital deciders today (Morgus and Maurer 2014). India and Indonesia are NAM’s founding members, while South Africa joined in 1994. The movement was established in 1961 by countries that did not want to side with either major power during the Cold War, and has since grown to 120 member states and seventeen observer states, representing over half of the world’s population (Nuclear Threat Initiative 2018). Its core principles include mutual respect for sovereignty, noninterference in the internal affairs of states, and sovereign equality among all nations, whether large or small (Government of India 2012). NAM founders strategically declared it as a movement, and not an organization, to avoid the bureaucratic implications of the latter. The NAM seeks to strengthen its members’ weak political and economic position vis-à-vis the more developed nations and prioritize the views of the ‘Third World,’ such as on nuclear disarmament (Mital 2016). The NAM not only creates a sense of belonging and a coherent self-narrative for developing countries (many of which were recently decolonized at the time of the movement’s founding), but also provides a platform to discuss ways to meaningfully contribute to international peace and security (Vieira 2016). Despite the end of the Cold War, the NAM, as ‘the biggest ever, “free,” and “informal” association of countries’, continues to exist and promotes the principles of multilateralism, disarmament, and sovereign equality (Kabi 2002, 1161).

Deliberations on the ‘rules of the road’ for cyberspace have taken place in the UN First Committee, in which NAM members actively participate by, for example, calling for the total elimination of nuclear weapons and encouraging multilateral disarmament efforts (Kumar and Brown 2019; also see United Nations 2019b). Although the NAM has been disparaged as a thing of the past, it still plays a dynamic role in shaping current debates, including on cybersecurity (Strydom 2007). At the sixteenth NAM Summit in 2012, the organisation expressed concern that technologies are used ‘for purposes that are inconsistent with the objectives of maintaining international stability and security’ and in contravention of the UN Charter (see United Nations Institute for Disarmament Research \[UNIDIR\] 2012, 60). Subsequently, at the NAM Summit in 2016, the movement called for safeguarding cyberspace from becoming ‘an arena of conflict’ and that any development of a legal framework should come from within the UN ‘with the active and equal participation of all States’ (UNIDIR 2016, 70). The movement also called for joint action in 2017 to combat cyberattacks and expressed concerns about malicious acts mediated through ICTs (United Nations 2017). Similar sentiments were found in the NAM’s statements at the OEWG, along with additional positions on responsible state behaviour that are based on the ‘principles and objectives of NAM’ (NAM 2020, 2).

India, Indonesia and South Africa have been referred to as middle powers. However, the term ‘middle power’ is defined in various ways and there is no commonly accepted definition or method of defining it. There are also no commonly agreed-upon attributes of middle powers and what they should do (Ping 2017). Middle powers, like other states, are self-interested and motivated by the need to recreate their own state (or idea of a state) in the international arena. This is because middle powers are the result of, but also contribute to, competition with other similarly sized and influential states that have like geographic, population, and economic features. If middle powers find that engagement in certain issues serves their strategic interests in the context of regional or global competition with other middle powers, then they are likely to continue to do so (Ping 2017, 192).

Middle powers cannot be identified by their behaviour alone because behavioural criteria must be able to account for all countries that have been considered as middle powers (past and present), and not just those that fit the desired patterns of Western middle powers like Australia and Canada. Therefore, what are considered as middle powers have varied over context, time, and space. Australia, Canada, Norway, and Sweden were traditionally considered as middle powers (Jordaan 2003). Australia and Canada, for example, self-described as middle powers in the post–World War II period to differentiate themselves from the ‘great’ and ‘minor’ powers at the time (Robertson 2017). But past consensus on what middle powers consist of is being undercut by the more recent inclusion of states like India, Indonesia, Malaysia, Nigeria, South Africa, and Turkey in the literature as ‘emerging’ middle powers (Jordaan 2003, 165).

Different scholars have used different approaches to classify India, Indonesia, and South Africa as middle powers. Ping developed a comprehensive definition of middle powers using statistical indicators such as population size and military expenditure, as well as normative and behavioural considerations, resulting in Indonesia’s classification as a middle power (Ping 2017, 106). Efstathopoulos (2011), meanwhile, argues that the ability to perform certain leadership roles is among the key ‘ideational resources’ used by middle powers to generate influence at the global stage. India is considered a middle power, given its coalition-building efforts among developing countries. Similarly, South Africa is considered by Yanacopulos (2014) as an ‘emerging middle power’ due to its tendency to embrace compromise positions in international disputes. Regardless of the diverse conditions under which they are classified as middle powers, the three countries share a similarity in the context of this chapter, which is that they were among the few emerging economies that were actively engaged in the 2019–2021 OEWG series of convenings.

The active participation of India, Indonesia, and South Africa in UN cyber norms processes shows that, while the major powers still set the tone of the debates, middle powers are growing in influence globally. Nonetheless, the participation of less developed countries remains low and inconsistent compared to the developed economies like France, Germany, the United Kingdom, and the United States, all of which have advanced cybersecurity capabilities and are projecting their influence in global cyber governance discussions. This asymmetric representation means that not as much is known about the viewpoints of emerging economies.

India, Indonesia, and South Africa have a long track record of contributions to the UN. As previously mentioned, all three have been GGE members and their representatives delivered statements at every session convened by the 2019–2021 OEWG. India has served eight times as a non-permanent member of the UN Security Council (UNSC) and is one of the strongest advocates for developing countries, focusing on issues of decolonization, racial discrimination, and UN reform (Roy 2021). India’s growing economic and military power has also led to support from the United States and France for its inclusion as a permanent member. India has contributed significantly to UN peacekeeping missions, with almost 200,000 troops over forty-nine missions, and had the fifth largest active UN peacekeeping force in 2020 (Ganguly 2021).

Indonesia, meanwhile, often speaks at the UN on the NAM’s behalf, a role the country has taken on since Sukarno, Indonesia’s first president, hosted the 1955 Bandung Conference of Asian and African countries in Bandung, Indonesia, which paved the way for the NAM’s establishment in 1961 (Coca 2019). Most recently, Indonesia used its UNSC presidency to address barriers and systemic inequalities within peacekeeping operations (Nuri, Gayatri and True 2020) and hosted an Arria Formula meeting on the vulnerability of critical infrastructure against cyberattacks (Security Council Report 2020). South Africa also played a leading role as part of the ‘New Agenda Coalition’ in bridging the divide between nuclear and non-nuclear weapons states regarding discussions on disarmament and the renewal of the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty in 2000.^([6](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.006)) South Africa has been active in pushing for UN reforms, advocating for African interests, and improving international cooperation between the developed countries, UN, and the African Union (de Carvalho, Mutangadura and Leijenaar 2020). This rich history of active participation has led India, Indonesia, and South Africa to develop a reputation as responsible and credible actors among UN member states.

Emerging economies—whether categorized as NAM members, middle powers, swing states, or digital deciders—represent a significant share of the world’s population and economy. Therefore, even though we often focus on the influence of the major powers in UN cyber norms processes, we can no longer ignore the fact that the decisions that emerging countries make would also have a significant impact on the future of the internet (e.g., the internet governance model they choose to adopt). At the same time, we need to recognize that these countries have conflicting national agendas, values and priorities, and therefore lack a single coherent stance on cybersecurity issues.

### Challenges in ICT Development and Use in India, Indonesia and South Africa

This section briefly describes recent challenges that India, Indonesia and South Africa face in the development and use of ICTs within their jurisdictions (Maurer 2020). State preferences on the internet policy area at the global stage cannot be isolated from domestic political struggles, because most sociocultural and political issues related to ICTs likely developed domestically before forces of globalization and multilateralism made them international issues (Drezner 2004). Governments are also more likely to prefer global regulations to “mirror their own national standards” to reduce adjustment costs to a new standard (Drezner 2004, 482). Thus, domestic institutional arrangements, legal frameworks, and technical capacities (among others) affect countries’ positions in global negotiations (Berglund and Calandro 2020).

#### India

With 1.35 billion people, India has the second largest population in the world after China, and at only 45 percent internet penetration as of 2021, the internet’s growth potential remains vast (Mandavia 2019; Kemp 2021). Prime Minister Narendra Modi launched the Digital India campaign in 2015 to transform India’s economy (see Government of India n.d.). Indians today increasingly use digital services like food delivery apps, and download more apps than any other country except China (Kaka et al. 2019). Internet access, however, remains concentrated in urban areas, even though 66 percent of the population lives in rural areas. Ranked eighty-eighth globally in the 2020 Network Readiness Index, India must boost its ICT development to close the digital divide (Cornelissen 2021; Portulans Institute 2020b).

Technology-mediated threats have increased along with growing connectivity. India faces a high frequency of ransomware attacks, for example (K7 Labs 2019, 14; Pahwa 2019). Research by the cybersecurity firm Sophos in 2021 uncovered that ‘Indian entities were the most likely to pay a ransom of all countries surveyed’ (*Business Standard* 2021). Meanwhile, online hate speech, particularly against Muslims, has been on the rise and affects India’s foreign relations with other democracies like the United States, and strategic partners like members of the Organization of Islamic Cooperation (OIC) (Chowdhury 2021; Times Now 2020). In February 2021, India announced new requirements for internet companies to swiftly remove offending posts and to share details on the authors of these messages. This order was seen as part of India’s broader nationalist agenda to tighten its grip on Western tech companies, particularly after many of them ignored government orders to censor content related to the 2020–2021 Indian farmers’ protests (Phartiyal and Kalra 2021).

India has complex geopolitical relationships with the United States and China. Relations with the United States improved in the post–Cold War period, but it was China’s rapid ascent in the international system that led to closer US–India bilateral ties (Ladwig and Mukherjee 2019). In 2018, the United States eased export restrictions for high-technology product sales to India, which allows India to import a wide range of American high-tech products, including sensitive defence technologies (Economic Times 2018). Yet, India has also sought to decrease its reliance on US-based services and platforms, including by mandating that certain kinds of data are to be stored in servers located physically within India (Basu, Hickok and Chawla 2019, 3). India’s data localization policy is part of a wave of reaction from developing countries against ‘data colonialism,’ or processes of data collection and use that occur in ‘the context of an asymmetrical power relation’ between developed and developing countries and therefore analogous to past colonial practices (Thatcher, O’Sullivan and Mahmoudi 2016). India also passed a regulation that requires all foreign billings for digital services provided in the country to be taxed as of April 1, 2020 (Phartiyal 2020). In response, the United States opened investigations into countries planning digital taxes against its firms, including India and Indonesia (Amaro 2020).

Recurring border disputes have led to fractures in India–China relations (*Times of India* 2018). Both countries stabilized conditions following the 2017 Doklam standoff on the India–China border (Wuthnow, Limaye and Samaranayake 2018), and India has joined the SCO, which counts China and Russia among its members (UN Department of Political and Peacebuilding Affairs n.d.). However, the June 2020 clashes in Eastern Ladakh’s Galwan Valley escalated tensions once again. In response, India banned over 100 Chinese phone apps from its domestic market in September 2020 (Yasir and Kumar 2020), and in January 2021, it retained its ban on video-sharing app TikTok and fifty-eight other Chinese apps (Phartiyal 2021). In May 2021, India excluded Chinese companies Huawei and ZTE from carrying out 5G trials in the country, although it did not explicitly ban them from supplying 5G equipment in the future (Pant 2021). India’s uneasy relationship with the United States and China is likely to influence its middle ground position in UN cyber norms deliberations.

#### Indonesia

According to the Indonesian Internet Service Providers Association (APJII), 73.3 percent of Indonesia’s over 250 million people were already connected to the internet in 2021 (BeritaSatu 2020). The geographical challenges presented by Indonesia’s roughly 17,000 islands, however, has contributed to a digital divide, with internet access being concentrated in urban areas (Das et al. 2016, 8). Indonesia ranked seventy-third globally in the 2020 Network Readiness Index, which indicates that more commitment is necessary to improve connectivity and equitable access (Portulans Institute 2020c). President Joko Widodo (or ‘Jokowi’) announced in August 2020 that his administration has allocated US$2.06 billion in the 2021 state budget for ICT development (Sugianto 2020). This effort builds on the 2018 roadmap of ‘Making Indonesia 4.0’,^([7](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.007)) which seeks to transform the Indonesian economy using new and emerging technologies (Putri and Ginanjar 2018).

Indonesia’s weak cybersecurity legislation and lack of personal data protection makes it an attractive target for cyber criminals (Chairil 2019). Without a comprehensive cybersecurity regulation, responsibilities are split across different ministries and law enforcement, making them ineffective in mitigating threats. In 2019, the newly formed National Cyber and Crypto Agency (BSSN) reported 290 million cases of cyberattacks or a 25 percent increase from the previous year, causing US$34.2 billion in losses (Anjani 2021). At the first OEWG substantive session in 2019, Indonesia said on the NAM’s behalf that it fears the ‘malicious use of new ICTs’ and is in support of the inclusive and consensus-based nature of the OEWG to tackle this issue (Geneva Internet Platform 2019). The representatives of Antigua and Barbuda, Iran, South Africa and Thailand aligned themselves with this statement.

Indonesia is increasingly concerned with the proliferation of online hate speech and misinformation, as well as the use of social media to ‘weaponize’ religious intolerance in this highly diverse country (Paterson 2019). These circumstances have led Indonesian government officials, including the head of the BSSN (IDN Financials 2019), to argue that Indonesia ‘must have cyber sovereignty’ to control information flows (Pinardi 2016). Yet, Indonesia has not firmly sided with a particular ‘camp’ at the OEWG, as its foreign policy is guided by the ‘Free Active’ (*Bebas Aktif*) approach, which emphasizes non-alignment and national and regional resilience (Anwar 2018). This approach underpinned first president Sukarno’s decision to host the Bandung Conference, which led to the NAM’s creation (Choiruzzad 2020). Continuing the Free Active approach and advancing Indonesia’s role as a middle power at the UN have been the cornerstones of Jokowi’s foreign policy (Parameswaran 2014, 157). Indonesia is also a co-founder of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN), which endorsed in principle the GGE norms in 2018 (Noor 2018).

Indonesia has developed cybersecurity cooperation with both SCO and like-minded states (Anwar 2018). Despite previously tense relations with China, Indonesia is looking for Chinese support in its infrastructure development, and in January 2021 China and Indonesia signed a memorandum of understanding (MoU) on cyber capacity building, the ‘first of its kind internet security agreement China signed with a foreign country’ (Palma 2019; *The Star* 2021). In June 2021, another MoU was signed by BSSN with the Russia-headquartered cybersecurity vendor Kaspersky to enhance cybersecurity capacity (Spencer 2021). Bilateral cooperation with like-minded states includes an agreement between the Indonesian National Police and the US Attorney General’s Office on combating transnational cyber and financial crime (Chan 2018); cybersecurity policy dialogues with the Netherlands, the first of which took place in January 2021; and policy dialogues with Australia (Netherlands and You 2021; Australian Government 2020). Indonesia’s cybersecurity cooperation with both ‘camps’, the Free Active foreign policy, and advocacy for the NAM mean that it potentially will continue to hold a middle ground position at the UN.

#### South Africa

South Africa has the fifth largest population in Africa and a 64 percent internet penetration rate as of January 2021 (Worldometer 2020; Kemp 2020). The concentration of internet access in urban areas and the lack of affordable devices, however, have served as barriers for South Africans to come online. The high price of data has also limited the intensity of internet use (Gillwald and Mothobi 2019; van der Merwe 2020). A 2018 Pew Research Center survey on internet use across sub-Saharan Africa reported that those who have a higher income and are younger than thirty years old are more likely to go online (Silver and Johnson 2018).

The South African government has recognized the importance of ICTs in accelerating economic and societal development. In 2012, it adopted the ‘National Development Plan (NDP) 2030’, which aims to establish a ‘competitively priced and widely available broadband Internet’ by 2030 (National Planning Commission of South Africa n.d., 55). The ‘SA Connect’ project was set up in support of the NDP with the goal of delivering broadband access to 90 percent of the country’s population by 2020 (Mzekandaba 2019), but it has experienced missed targets and delays (Gillwald, Mothobi and Rademan 2018, 3). Ranked seventy-sixth in the world in the 2020 Network Readiness Index, South Africa must overcome significant challenges to create a digital economy as envisaged in the NDP (Portulans Institute 2020d).

The high frequency of cybercrime incidents and data privacy breaches have led to the creation of new legal frameworks (Business Insider 2020). The Protection of Personal Information (POPI) Act came into force in July 2020 (Moyo 2020), while the Cybercrimes Act became law in June 2021 (Bhagattjee, Govuza and Sebanz 2020). The latter, and the 2020 POPI Act, which came into full effect on 30 June 2021, are key instruments in South Africa’s anti-cybercrime toolbox (Allen 2021). The rise in hate crimes and the legacy of apartheid resulted in South Africa introducing the ‘Prevention and Combating of Hate Crimes and Hate Speech Bill’ in April 2018. Proponents of the bill maintain that hate speech has become more widespread in the age of social media, but opponents argue that existing legal remedies are adequate to address the issue (Mahr 2019). The legislation was revived again in October 2019, but is yet to be passed. Unlike India and Indonesia, South Africa is an observer country to the Convention on Cybercrime (Tamarkin 2015, 5), although sovereignty concerns have delayed ratification (India and Indonesia are similarly concerned) (Fidler 2016). South Africa is a member of BRICS, an informal group of states that also consists of Brazil, Russia, India and China. Recently, all BRICS countries have ramped up their cybersecurity policies and regulations, including by instituting data localization requirements (Belli 2019).

South Africa has been a key player in UN arms control and disarmament discussions, as it remains the only country which has built nuclear weapons and dismantled them in the early 1990s. As a ‘denuclearized’ state, South Africa carries a significant moral influence in promoting nonproliferation and disarmament (Schoeman 2000). Additionally, under Nelson Mandela’s presidency, South Africa mediated (or attempted to mediate) many high-profile international conflicts, such as the Palestinian conflict, the Lockerbie negotiations, and the Northern Ireland conflict, among others. Meanwhile, the Mbeki presidency conducted several mediation talks regarding such conflicts as in the DRC and Palestine (Jordaan 2003, 175). As a result, South Africa has developed a reputation as a peaceful ‘bridge-builder’ between the global North and South (Schoeman 2000).

Like India and Indonesia, South Africa is balancing important trade and defence relations with China and the United States. A 2019 survey by the Pew Research Center found that nearly as many South Africans name China as their most dependable ally as those who name the United States (Silver 2019). As of 2020, South Africa is China’s largest trading partner in Africa, and Chinese foreign direct investment has increased dramatically in the past five years (China Africa Research Initiative n.d.; Mlambo 2019). Chinese tech companies are also expanding rapidly in the country, including those that manufacture surveillance technologies (Sherman 2019), thereby creating fears of ‘digital authoritarianism’.^([8](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.008)) Yet, South Africa is also the United States’ largest trading partner in Africa with over 600 American firms operating in the country (US Department of State 2020). The imperative to maintain stable relationships with the United States and China likely impacts South Africa’s middle ground attitude in UN deliberations that involve the major powers.

### India, Indonesia and South Africa’s positions at the UN OEWG

In this section, we examine the verbal interventions and written submissions made by India, Indonesia and South Africa over the course of the first year of the UN OEWG. In addition, we spoke to three in-country experts (one in each country) to obtain a more nuanced understanding of their governments’ positions in UN cyber norms debates. Our analysis focuses on issue areas that remain divisive, including binding versus nonbinding commitments, state sovereignty, and uneven cybersecurity capacity. We also highlighted concerns related to cybercrime and the militarization of cyberspace.

#### Support for Legally Binding International Agreements

Although India, Indonesia and South Africa have been vocal and firm in their support for GGE outcomes (UN Web TV 2020b, 2:22:21), support was also expressed for legally binding instruments to regulate state behaviour in cyberspace. India stated that legally binding commitments are preferred because states are more likely to take them seriously and that the time is now to begin discussions on the matter, as ‘it is better to be late than never’ (UN Web TV 2020e, 19:20). India called on member states to consider the possibility of additional norms regarding tampering with the supply chain and for protecting ‘critical information infrastructure’, and made a written submission in this regard (‘India’s Comments on the Initial Pre-Draft’ 2020, 2).^([9](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.009)) India also pointed to an insufficient understanding among states on how exactly international law is applicable to cyberspace, and argued that further study, as well as new laws, may be required, particularly to address new forms of ICT-enabled conflict (UN Web TV 2020e, 19:20).

Indonesia considered nonbinding instruments to be a ‘valuable framework,’ but also maintained that ‘an established rules-based international regime’ is necessary (UN Web TV 2020e, 19:20). Its delegation argued that binding legal commitments ‘will represent a major contribution towards increasing stability and security in cyberspace’ (UN Web TV 2019, 25:40). Indonesia also spoke against the ‘automatic application’ of the existing body of international law without examining the ‘context and unique nature of activities in cyberspace’ (‘Indonesia’s Response on the Pre-Draft’ 2020, 2). Indonesia argued that the ‘new realities’ presented by the digital age have brought about challenges (e.g., attribution)^([10](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.010)) which cannot be adequately addressed by the existing body of international law. As the development of international law took place in a different historical setting, ‘practical adjustment and possible new interpretations are needed, while the gap of the ungoverned issues in cyberspace also needs to be addressed’ (‘Indonesia’s Response on the Pre-Draft’ 2020, 2). Despite these concerns, Indonesia stated that international law, including the UN Charter, is applicable in its entirety to cyberspace (UN Web TV 2020c, 1:54:17). Indonesia argued that the universalization and implementation of existing GGE norms should be a priority, but it was concerned that these norms are not commonly understood and are open for interpretation (UN Web TV 2020a, 2:17:12). Simultaneously, Indonesia acknowledged the potential for additional norms, and suggested that the final OEWG report could propose them or expand on existing ones for future deliberation (UN Web TV 2020a, 2:17:12). Indonesia did not explain, however, how the addition of new norms would contribute to better understanding or more consistent interpretation of cyber norms.

Similar to Indonesia, South Africa stated that the existing body of international law ‘may not be adequately responsive’ to challenges in the digital age UN Web TV 2019, 1:56:56). Therefore, South Africa called for ‘further study’ of international law and stated that ‘new \[international\] laws may be required, particularly regarding new forms of ICT-enabled conflict’ (UN Web TV 2020e, 19:20). South Africa also encouraged member states to ‘have a long-term view of the cybersecurity architecture that will include a legally binding framework’ to hold member states accountable. Yet, it did not reiterate this point in subsequent OEWG meetings or reports. South Africa only later acknowledged that ‘some gaps’ in international law exist and clarification is needed on existing norms and international law (UN Web TV 2020c, 1:10:15). Unlike India and Indonesia, South Africa does not believe that additional cyber norms are necessary, though it will nevertheless be ‘useful to have them collated’ for future review (UN Web TV 2020c, 1:10:15). More importantly, its representative issued a ‘challenge’ to UN member states to mention specific articles of international law or norms that have been violated when making attributions of malicious cyber operations, as direct references would demonstrate genuine commitments.

#### Sovereignty Concerns

A significant consensus exists among states that international law is applicable to cyberspace. Yet, several concerns remain. For example, it is unclear whether there is a threshold for violations of sovereignty in cyberspace, and if so, what that threshold consists of (Moynihan 2019). India highlighted the need to develop a global understanding of the kinds of cyberattacks that can be considered as violations of state sovereignty (UN Web TV 2020b). Additional challenges noted include an unclear threshold for invoking self-defence, difficulties in distinguishing tools used against military versus civilian targets, and the attribution problem, among others (UN Web TV 2020b). Indonesia stated on the NAM’s behalf that state behaviour in cyberspace must follow the principle of sovereign equality among states, whether large or small, and South Africa aligned itself with this statement (UN Web TV 2019, 25:40; 2020a, 11:19). But it also expressed concerns that states still hold divergent views on important legal concepts in international law, such as what constitutes defending sovereignty in the face of cyberattacks (UN Web TV 2020c, 1:54:17). Additionally, South Africa noted that harm caused by cyberattacks may be so severe as to impinge on a government’s sovereign ability to function, and therefore, such attacks must be mitigated (UN Web TV 2019, 1:56:56).

Sovereignty concerns also extend to data governance. For example, India recommended ‘a new form of sovereignty’ based on the ownership of data (or ‘data-oriented sovereignty’), which means that the territorial jurisdiction of a country would apply to the data which is owned by its citizens, ‘irrespective of the place where the data physically is located’ (‘India’s comments on the initial pre-draft’ 2020, 4). As the internet grows in importance, governments around the world (particularly those in developing economies with large populations) are likely to rely on data governance measures as a means of exercising power (Komaitis 2017).

#### Uneven Capacity and Dissimilar Understandings

India, Indonesia and South Africa were similarly concerned regarding the lack of consensus on frequently used terms, such as ‘cyber weapons’, ‘cyber-attacks’ and ‘cyber deterrence’ (UN Web TV 2020b, 22:25). In-country experts we spoke to further highlighted the need to build domestic capacity and understanding, which would pave the way for the practical implementation of norms and capacity- and confidence-building measures.^([11](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.011)) South Africa stressed the need to ensure that existing norms are implemented within and across government departments, and called on developed countries to fund capacity building (UN Web TV 2020e, 1:04:30), including skills and knowledge transfer and training for efficient responses to cybersecurity incidents. An expert we spoke to in India maintained, however, that cyber norms are not yet a priority for the country because the government is still largely focused on using ICTs to develop its economy. Similarly, an expert in Indonesia argued that, despite ASEAN’s endorsement in principle of GGE norms (Noor 2018), Indonesia has limited capacity and resources, and still needs significant assistance.^([12](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.012)) Given the lack of capacity and common understandings, combined with the devastating impact of the novel coronavirus (COVID-19) pandemic, the hope that the practical implementation of cyber norms can be achieved presently in the three countries has been dashed.^([13](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09.ftn.013))

#### Cybercrime and the Militarization of Cyberspace

Cybercrime and the militarization of cyberspace are highlighted here as these were frequently mentioned at the UN OEWG. Cybercrime, an issue under the purview of the UN’s Third Committee on Social, Humanitarian, and Cultural Matters, was repeatedly brought up by emerging economies at the OEWG, which is located under the First Committee on Disarmament and International Security. This may indicate that cybercrime is a more pressing concern for this group of states, compared to the ‘rules of the road’ for cyberspace that dominate the agenda of highly-digitalized countries. It also signals that discussions on cybersecurity issues must be holistic, instead of siloed off from one another. Indeed, the representative from South Africa argued that the distinction between cybercrime and cybersecurity is shrinking for the country (UN Web TV 2019, 1:56:56), as its weak defence and lack of security culture makes it an attractive target for both (UN Web TV 2020d, 1:40:05). India, in contrast, raised the issue of cybercrime together with cyber terrorism (UN Web TV 2020d), while Indonesia did not reference cybercrime at all in its statements.

Some states brought up the issue of the militarization of cyberspace at the OEWG, which was met with scepticism by others (i.e., that the topic was brought up to stymie progress), because the cyber operations that concern the states the most are those that might be termed as ‘malicious use of ICTs’ (UN Office for Disarmament Affairs n.d.), of which a majority remains in the ‘grey zone’ (or below the threshold of what would constitute an armed attack) (Corn 2020; Boeke and Broeders 2018). The most vocal states have been China, Iran, and Russia (Meyer 2019), which are widely understood to possess offensive cyber capabilities of their own. But their stance on this issue—particularly against the like-minded states, many of which have openly acknowledged their offensive military cyber capabilities (e.g., Australia, the Netherlands, and the UK)—results in difficult negotiations (Meyer 2019). Indonesia and India were among the countries that also spoke against militarization, whereas South Africa was not.

### Contextualising a Middle Ground Cyber Strategy

In UN cyber norms processes, discord among member states can be summed up as a tension between two ‘camps’. China, Russia, and SCO member states have proposed a legally binding instrument to govern state behaviour. In contrast, like-minded countries like the United States, Canada, and EU member states support the practical implementation of the existing framework of international law, cyber norms, and capacity- and confidence-building measures. Alongside the two ‘camps’ of states, however, is a third cluster of countries that continues to hold the middle ground (swing states or digital deciders), which include India, Indonesia and South Africa. Nonetheless, this third cluster is not monolithic and has lacked a single coherent stance on cybersecurity issues.

India, Indonesia and South Africa have also been labelled as emerging middle powers. Categorising states may be a useful first step towards making sense of their place in global governance. Yet, the lack of consensus on the definition and methods to define middle powers, as well as what exactly they should do, limits the usefulness of the term. Muddled views on ‘middlepowermanship’ notwithstanding (Clarkson 1966), developing shared understandings on the ‘rules of the road’ for cyberspace requires moving beyond simple classifications.

By examining countries’ statements at the UN OEWG and situating them against country-level and geopolitical challenges in the development and use of ICTs, this chapter uses the empirical cases of India, Indonesia and South Africa to contextualize their middle ground preferences at the UN. It is a timely analysis given that the influence of this diverse ‘camp’ of states is only growing. Representatives of the three countries have delivered statements at every session convened by the 2019–2021 UN OEWG, and Indonesia also spoke on behalf of the NAM. Additionally, NAM members like Egypt and Colombia are among the countries that have proposed the Programme of Action (PoA) to create a permanent UN forum for cyber norms discussions, while Singapore will chair the 2021–2025 UN OEWG.

Non-Western countries account for a sizable proportion of the world’s population. A better understanding of their viewpoints in cyber norms deliberations would be helpful for the like-minded states to identify and work with those that have indicated a ‘middle of the road’ position, and thus have some willingness to compromise—while also continuing negotiations with others that have stymied progress. Our analysis indicates that India, Indonesia and South Africa constitute the former.

India remains predominantly focused on using ICTs to improve its economic development, and has been embroiled in disputes recently with China and the United States. Indonesia has a long-standing ‘Free Active’ and non-aligned foreign policy and is dedicated to the NAM while courting investments for its digital economy from both SCO and the like-minded states. South Africa is both the United States and China’s largest trading partner in Africa, and still needs significant assistance in terms of training, capacity-building, and investment support to bolster its cybersecurity capabilities. Given these trends, the three countries are likely to continue to hold a ‘middle of the road’ position in UN cyber norms processes.

We argue that India, Indonesia and South Africa maintain a purposeful middle ground position in global negotiations. A cautious engagement with the major powers on cybersecurity issues is projected to continue, as these countries are motivated by their own strategic interests and have been ardent advocates for developing countries’ principles and causes (e.g., non-alignment and disarmament) (Karim 2018). Furthermore, these countries continue to face challenges related to the development and use of ICTs, and have complex geopolitical relations with the United States and China. Further research is needed to ascertain the viewpoints of similarly situated countries and assess their impact on global cyber governance deliberations. The study of countries that hold a middle-ground position and its implications remains underrepresented in the literature, yet it is essential to address the challenges posed by the fragmentation of efforts to establish rules of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace.

[2](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.002). Formally known as the Open Ended Working Group on Developments in the Field of Information and Telecommunications in the Context of International Security.

[3](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.003). NAM is an alliance established by a group of newly independent states in Asia and Africa following the 1955 Asia–Africa Bandung Conference in Indonesia. These states did not side with either major power during the Cold War, but were instead ‘non-aligned’ in international affairs. See Waters (2001, 153).

[4](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.004). For a list of all written statements, see [https://un.org/disarmament/open-ended-working-group/](https://un.org/disarmament/open-ended-working-group/). For the recordings of OEWG meetings, see [https://webtv.un.org/](https://webtv.un.org/).

[5](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.005). The Shanghai Cooperation Organization (SCO) is an intergovernmental organization founded in Shanghai on June 15, 2001, consisting of China, India, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Russia, Pakistan, Tajikistan and Uzbekistan. The SCO has mainly focused on regional security issues, its fight against regional terrorism, ethnic separatism, and religious extremism.

[6](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.006). The New Agenda Coalition is comprised of Brazil, Egypt, Ireland, Mexico, New Zealand and South Africa. See Nuclear Threat Initiative (2020).

[7](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.007). ‘4.0’ is a term that refers to the ‘fourth industrial revolution’ in manufacturing and industry. See Indonesia Investments (2018).

[8](#Chapter09.xhtml#ch09r.ftn.008). The term ‘digital authoritarianism’ refers to the government’s use of digital technologies to surveil, censor, and suppress their people; see Shahbaz (2018).
