[**11**](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp11)

## [The Hybrid Place *Civil Society in the Open-Ended Working Group*](../Text/nav.xhtml#chp11)

*André Barrinha and Louise Marie Hurel*

**F**ROM ITS ORIGINS, developments in cyberspace owe much to the active role of non-state actors: academia, companies, and certainly civil society. While many states have publicly recognised the importance of non-state actors in developing cyber resilience, international discussions concerning cybersecurity have concentrated on inter-state fora, particularly within the United Nations Group of Governmental Experts (GGE) and the recently established Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) on cybersecurity. Regardless of the merits and motivations behind its creation, in practice the OEWG broadened the space for inter-state discussions within the UN, while also providing the possibility for other stakeholders, including civil society, to be involved in the process. As in other cases, the OEWG reintroduces key questions about participation, representation, and legitimacy of action from civil society groups in shaping and making global cybersecurity. However, rather than restricting the discussion about participation to ‘having a seat at the table’, this chapter asks *how is civil society constructed in international cybersecurity?* To answer this question, it looks at the participatory mechanisms, engagement, perception and representation of civil society in the OEWG. The chapter explores the role and concept of civil society in the context of the first OEWG (2019–2021), unpacking its complexities and multiple agencies. It approaches the concept of ‘civil society’ as a hybrid construct—enacted and consolidated in its diverse expressions. In doing so, it speaks both to a topical discussion on the politics of participation in international cybersecurity, as well as to a deeper understanding of the role of civil society in international affairs. Focusing on the complex dynamics of representation, this chapter argues that the OEWG contributed to the reification of an idea of civil society that is both diverse and unified, and questions and legitimises states’ decisions in this area.

Internet and cybersecurity governance owe much to the active role of non-state actors. Civil society organisations (CSOs), companies, technical experts and academia have all helped shape the norms, policies, standards, architectures, services and business models that currently configure a shifting landscape of power and politics of information and communications technologies (ICTs). However, scholarly literature on each of these actors’ roles in cybersecurity has been significantly uneven—with considerable focus directed towards the changing role of the state vis-à-vis emerging threats in cyberspace (Nye 2010; Kramer, Starr and Wentz 2009; Clark and Knake 2019; Buchanan 2020) and the influence of private companies and Big Tech in providing expertise, technology and services that have paved new avenues for shaping cybersecurity (Hurel and Lobato 2018, 2020; Smith and Browne 2019). Far less explored, however, is the role of technical experts (Levinson and Marzouki 2015; Hinojosa, Aiken and Hurel 2019), academia (think tanks and scholars), and civil society groups (Deibert 2013; Kumar 2021) in making and shaping cybersecurity. Even less so when it comes to international cyber norms discussions.

While many states have publicly recognised the importance of non-state actors in developing cyber resilience, international discussions concerning cybersecurity have progressively concentrated on inter-state fora, particularly within the context of the UN. The GGE (full title: Group of Governmental Experts on Developments in the Field of Information and Telecommunications in the Context of International Security) had its first session in 2004, with the participation of experts from fifteen states, including from the five permanent members of the Security Council. Its creation signalled the institutionalisation of discussions surrounding the importance of ICTs in peace and international security within the context of the UN, albeit to a limited number of states. In 2018, the UN General Assembly approved the establishment of two quasi-simultaneous processes on the role of ICTs in international security. Russia, the proponent of the first GGE, decided—after a diplomatic reversal—to sponsor instead the creation of an Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) on developments in the field of information and telecommunications in the context of international security (UN General Assembly 2018b), whereas the United States led the process towards the creation of the new GGE (UN General Assembly 2018a), which would correspond to its sixth iteration. Regardless of the merits and motivations of each proposal, in practice the OEWG resolution broadened the space for inter-state discussions within the UN by including all member states and provided the possibility for other stakeholders to be involved in the process, including civil society.

These developments revived fundamental questions of participation, representation, and legitimacy of action from civil society groups in shaping and making global cybersecurity, such as: How can civil society organisations participate? What kinds of civil society groups are able to effectively engage in these processes? Where are these organisations based? Do they have convergent views?

While these are important questions, and not particularly exclusive to cybersecurity discussions, they point to a deeper discussion about how ‘civil society’ operates not only as a signifier to understandings of ‘participation’, for example, but also as in inherent part of the tensions present in a specific debate. The term can serve as a reference to an actor, a group, a stakeholder category and even be the object of a norm. In multilateral organisations such as the UN, these civil society groups have often been included under the wider umbrella concept of ‘multistakeholder’^([1](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.001)) governance and assigned specific mechanisms to validate their ‘status’ and their organisation in this particular ecosystem. However, these mechanisms reinforce a *stakeholderisation* of participation rendering invisible the multiple agencies, the positionalities of civil society groups as well as the perceptions of ‘civil society’ that compose a landscape of contentious relationships.

Scholarly literature and practitioners have referred to *stakeholderisation* and *multistakeholderisation*—at times used interchangeably—as concepts associated with the corporatisation of policy environments (Utting 2000; Prato 2017) and as labels that have been increasingly used by international organisations to transform a policy arena into a stakeholderised environment through multistakeholder initiatives (Richter 2017). In internet governance, scholars such as Brown and Marsden (2013, 1) argue that this multistakeholderisation is connected to the process through which ‘non-governmental organizations (NGOs) and other prosumer groups from civil society argue for representation in the closed business-government dialogue, seeking to bring in both rights-based and technologically expert perspectives’. In cyber norms, this stakeholderisation has also taken place through the establishment of multiple multistakeholder initiatives such as the Paris Call for Trust and Security in Cyberspace, or in cybersecurity discussions at the Internet Governance Forums’ Best Practice Forum on Cybersecurity, for example.

Civil society groups have also been key in building standards for technologies (Cath and Floridi 2017), advocating for a more fair, accountable, and transparent use of automated decision-making technologies (Milan and van der Velden 2016). They have also developed new technologies and methods for evaluating the impacts of these technologies, such as in the exercise of human rights (i.e., Human Rights Impact Assessments), and they have actively engaged in shaping internet governance (Malcom 2014). However, when referring to stakeholderisation, we are specifically addressing how a complex myriad of actors becomes associated with a particular sector. While this enables representation and articulation of CSOs through ‘stakeholder groups’, it overlooks the topography of differences that make up the multiple ‘civil societies’ and is mute in terms of processes and means of participation: who is entitled to participate, and how, are second-order questions whose answer is unrelated to the essence of the multistakeholder label.

With that in mind, this chapter asks: How is civil society constructed in international cybersecurity? In order to answer this question, it looks at the engagement and representation of civil society in the initial stages of the OEWG—including the December 2019 Intersessional—when it was made clear the terms of their participation. It does so by problematising the idea of civil society as a hybrid construct; for some it is a symbol of democratic governance and values, while for others it is a signifier for participatory mechanisms in multilateral fora. These multiple understandings of *who* civil society is, *what* it represents and *how* it should participate in cybersecurity discussions, all make up the notion of civil society as a hybrid construct. As the chapter shows, this approach allows us to further visualise and position contested categories of civil society (and the state)—as well as their respective horizons of participation—in a more nuanced understanding of the political dynamics in the field of global cybersecurity. Within the OEWG context, civil society’s hybridity is expressed in terms of agency. However, that is contested by (some) states and other actors via (1) institutional mechanisms, (2) member states’ official statements and even by the (3) performativity of the room where the OEWG and its intersessional meeting took place in December 2019. As a result, we not only hope to contribute to a deeper and more complex understanding of civil society, but also of the role it plays and the constraints it faces amidst the contingent struggles with and among states.

In terms of structure, we first unpack the concept of civil society, how it has taken shape in cybersecurity. We then provide the context in which civil society emerged as a ‘stakeholder’ to be included in international peace and security discussions related to cybersecurity—in particular, in the OEWG. Third, in light of the state-driven environment of the OEWG, we explore how states have referred to civil society groups within this forum. After doing so, the section presents some of the institutional and political struggles that have slowly drawn the contours of *how hybridity has been expressed through the political disputes around participation in the OEWG*, as well as the state-to-state diverging opinions over that. Fourth, we analyse civil society’s engagement in the OEWG. In this section we present some of the key challenges and contributions highlighted by civil society groups participating in the process and we tap into the relationality of hybridity, exploring the role of states vis-à-vis civil society. We then conclude on how hybridity as a concept allows us to understand the legitimacy dispute that is central to the governance of international cybersecurity; to leave these relationships uncontested means leaving legitimacy-making uncontested—who can *make norms* and/or *engage in norms-making* (Hurel and Lobato 2018, 2020).

### Unpacking Civil Society

Civil society is an historically contested term (Kaldor 2003), ‘dusted off and put to active use within the social sciences . . . in order to describe the conditions of economic and democratic development prevailing in Eastern Europe after the end of the Cold War’ (Bartelson 2006, 379). This was a period of global democratisation that went hand in hand with the affirmation of a global market economy and the more intense circulation of communication around the world. Domestically, CSOs became active in democratic governance; internationally, they started to assume a more central role in a whole plethora of issues—from women’s rights to the rights of indigenous people (Selian 2004). Global governance became a common term, particularly after the Commission on Global Governance report that signalled a shift in international relations away from the pure state-centric decision-making process to a more inclusive scenario, composed of formal and informal arrangements ‘that people and institutions have agreed to or perceive to be in their interest’ (Commission on Global Governance 1995, 2).

This opened the room for new categories, agencies, and actors—including civil society—to participate in international processes as well as in transnational governance arrangements. The participation of civil society was seen to enhance democratic governance through advocacy, accountability and an overall ‘new way’ of doing things. This period also saw the consolidation of civil society as a nonprofit, voluntary ‘third sector’ that not only restrains state power but also actually provides a substitute for many of the functions performed by the state (Kaldor 2003, 9), particularly in the sphere of development aid where NGOs were called to replace direct donor presence in developing countries. However, at the same time civil society was being recognised as a key category in transnational governance, giving a ‘seat at the table’ to these groups of private citizens also raised important questions about representation and participation: ‘How does an incredibly diverse, heterogeneously rich, fractious, and resource-challenged civil society organize itself to participate effectively in these processes, to take advantage of that opening, that opportunity?’ (Cogburn 2017, 4).

Overall, the idea of civil society can be ‘politically opportune’ as it helps ‘various subordinated circles in today’s more global society to gain recognition, voice, resources and influence’ (Scholte 2011b, 36). They do so through a variety of methods that include advocacy, direct action and knowledge production. They tend to thrive in informal settings (Selian 2004), as it is not uncommon for the ‘formal’ to be the predominant domain of the state. Their participation in those settings can sometimes be limited to an outsider status, often literally *outside*, as is the case when CSOs have material tables outside the main rooms because they are not allowed to place them on the desk of delegates (Kumar and Pytlak 2020, 4).

There is not one but multiple civil societies, signified in their social contexts and in their everyday practices (advocacy, policy recommendations, community support, platform for dialogue). International discussions and multilateral environments are some of the places/spaces where these multiplicities come together. Positioned, as they are, in social relations, these civil society groups are mobilised under one cohesive vocabulary and, most importantly, under the concept of ‘civil society’ as a collective agency and/or a self-explanatory category in international dialogues. On the one hand, this enables a collective representation of an *agency* that lies within the broader setting of non-state actors or non-governmental stakeholders. On the other hand, it can obfuscate the uneven, contested and diverse agencies that are mobilised within this umbrella concept, often glossing over geographical, economic, gender and organisational particularities.

In general, we can speak of civil society ‘in relation to a governance apparatus’ (Scholte 2011b, 33), which can include the state but also go beyond it; ‘a political space where associations of citizens, seek, from outside political parties, to shape societal rules’ (Scholte 2011b, 34). Congregating an otherwise diverse set of actors—from local associations to multinational NGOs—is a sort of unity of purpose, a bounded space (Amoore and Langley 2004, 95); one that intends to be ‘a potent vehicle of change away from the present system of nation states’ (Bartelson 2006, 372). It exists and performs as an entity, both unified and fragmented, collective and individualised, diverse yet maintained as representative of a *civil society*. For Amoore and Langley, the answer to this is in understanding (global) civil society as ‘unbounded, fluid and mobile political sites’ (2004, 103). We argue that this duality provides a glimpse of the multidimensional hybridity of both *civil society as a concept* and *as a category that stands* (and is only understood) *in relation to other contingent categories such as the ‘state’*, for ‘opening up the box’ of the multiple understandings of civil society requires an equal destabilisation of the concept of ‘the state’ as a cohesive, unitary actor; recognising the tensions and contingencies also present *within* and *among* states themselves. While institutional mechanisms and participatory governance processes constantly seek to draw clear lines of who/what civil society is, we argue that its hybridity resides in unpacking *what it does—*that is, the practices, strategies and discourses—more than questioning *what it is—*as this would also bring us back to a static understanding of agency, subject and, perhaps, politics.

For the purpose of this chapter, we are interested in hybridity as deriving from civil society practices and relations; from what they do and with whom they interact in international cybersecurity spaces and agendas. In that regard, civil society, and its *multiple and complex* agencies, is not only signified in its diversity but also in relation to other actors and, of utmost interest for this chapter, in relation to the state. The *multiple and complex* agencies of civil society cannot be understood in a vacuum. As the following sections highlight, CSOs can, and often are, embroiled in geopolitical disputes.

### Civil Society and International Cybersecurity

As mentioned earlier, non-state actors play a central role in governing cyberspace. Scholars drawing from different disciplines have explored how these actors have contributed to the making of cybersecurity through the development of technical solutions and services, provision of expertise, advocacy and, most recently, in contributing to the development of global cyber norms (Hurel and Lobato 2018, 2020; Eggenschwiler and Kulesza 2020). Although civil society has been playing a central role in the field of ICTs, their relation to the specific area of cybersecurity remains far less explored. While concepts such as ‘shared responsibility’ or ‘multistakeholder’ have sought to partly capture this shared interest in the peace and stability of cyberspace across stakeholder groups, they fall short in representing the role and agency of civil society groups in cybersecurity.

Civil society groups have been key in building cyber capacities with local communities, developing inputs to international and regional processes, signalling the converging relationship between human rights and cybersecurity (Kumar and Pytlak 2020). But, in general, civil society has had a difficult relation with security as a policy field. As Deibert notes, ‘security is traditionally seen as an anathema to civil society: for some the language itself \[is\] evocative of the very constituencies that need to be resisted’ (2013, 40). Part of the explanation may also have to do with this being an area of strong state assertiveness. The association of security as the object of the state has been at the centre of the justification of the existence of the state as a cohesive and legitimate unit in the international system. While the stabilisation of the link between states and security has been extensively covered by literature in international relations, sociology and other disciplines—that includes but is not restricted to concepts such as the monopoly of violence (Weber 1994) or the development of institutions of discipline, governmentality and control (Foucault 1979, 2009) and the emergence of sovereignty itself—its equal stabilisation in cybersecurity through securitisation, development of national commands and strategies, has also contributed to the obfuscation of the role of the multiple actors involved in shaping and making cybersecurity, especially CSOs. Particularly when it comes to the international dimension of security, inter-state relations, with its rules, power configurations and priorities that are not necessarily in line with the world beyond states significantly condition the activities of these CSOs.

Civil society groups have engaged in different transnational cybersecurity processes. Established in 2011, the Freedom Online Coalition (FoC). for example, was founded and composed by governments and rapidly became a key space for multistakeholder discussions on maintaining a free and secure internet. What is more, its working group on security produced recommendations together with CSOs and the private sector on the impact of cyber-security laws, practices and policies on human rights. Other initiatives such as the Global Commission on the Stability of Cyberspace (GCSC) included representatives from civil society as commissioners in the process of developing norms proposals aimed at influencing and enhancing ‘international security and stability and guide responsible state and non-state behaviour in cyberspace’.^([2](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.002)) The Paris Call for Trust and Security in Cyberspace, for example, provided a list of norms that was open for CSOs to sign and endorse. Although important, these developments provide snapshots of how *participation* has been framed in cybersecurity discussions. In the cases above, participation is framed as (1) belonging and engaging in a group where CSOs and governments work together to produce a final output, (2) appointing individual CSO representatives to commissions so as to ensure stakeholder balance in approaching international cybersecurity challenges and (3) inviting CSOs to endorse a list of norms.

In multilateral environments such as that of the UN, a representational *cohesiveness of civil society* has been expressed in different ways. According to the UN, civil society is the ‘third sector’ of society that comprises CSOs and NGOs. Although it is possible for NGOs to receive accreditation through the UN Department of Public Information, the main way of participating in the work of the UN is by registering for consultative status with the Economic and Social Council (ECOSOC). This status provides these organisations the possibility to access discussions not only at ECOSOC but also processes in ‘its many subsidiary bodies . . . various human rights mechanisms of the United Nations, ad hoc processes on small arms, as well as special events organized by the President of the General Assembly.’ While the UN innovated in the 1990s when it institutionalised the accreditation of NGOs into specific processes, it has been criticised for falling short of moving beyond ‘generic and often episodic participation to meaningful incorporation of these actors into global political processes’ (McKeon 2009, 2).

Civil society has been continuously portrayed as a ‘stakeholder’ within the broader contours of multistakeholder governance processes—and this is particularly evident in the context of ICTs. In 2002, the UN General Assembly resolution on the ‘Creation of a global culture of cybersecurity’ stressed that ‘in a manner appropriate to their roles, government businesses, other organizations and individual owners and users of information technologies’ should be aware of cybersecurity risks, apply preventive measures and assume responsibility to enhance security of ICTs. While not explicitly mentioning civil society, the resolution did point to a shared responsibility across sectors as well as reinforced their respective roles in maintaining security of ICTs. However, the definition of the concept of internet governance by the Working Group on Internet Governance (WGIG) in 2005 was an important step in publicly recognising civil society as an integral part of the development and application of the rules, principles, norms and decision-making processes that shape the ‘evolution and use of the Internet’ (WGIG 2005, 4). At the same time, this recognition came hand in hand with the creation of expectations of inclusivity and bottom-up policy-making, operating under imaginaries of global representation, democratisation of transnational spheres and the possibility of improved outcomes (Hofmann 2016). The establishment of the Internet Governance Forum (IGF), for example, is one case of success in involving civil society and private citizens in the work of the UN (Cogburn 2017)—even leading to the consolidation of spaces such as the Best Practice Forum on Cybersecurity, a multistakeholder effort to map best practices in the field. However, in spite of the optimistic note on spaces such as the IGF, it also has its shortcomings because the consolidation of cybersecurity in multilateral bodies has offered little to no space for institutionalised participation of civil society in norms-making processes therein. The 2004 GGE set out what was to become (unusually for a GGE) a long process of discussions on ICTs within the context of international security. CSOs, traditionally relevant players in internet governance discussions, were not invited to the table.

The decision in 2018 to set up a parallel OEWG was, in that sense, a welcome change when compared to the GGE on security of ICTs. In a forum that exists under the shadow of international security, CSOs were given the opportunity to participate in the proceedings in what was the ‘UN’s first open and accessible forum to discuss ICTs in this specific context’ (Kumar and Pytlak 2020, 1). But why was that the case and what resulted from this ‘opening’? More importantly, how was hybridity at play in this setting?

### The Open-Ended Working Group

To the surprise of many, the autumn of 2018 did not bring another Russian resolution seeking a new iteration of the GGE on ICT, but rather the creation of a new, more open and inclusive group. The resolution portrayed the OEWG as a response to ‘making the United Nations negotiation process on security in the use of information and communications technologies more democratic, inclusive and transparent’ (UN General Assembly 2018b). At first, the simultaneous approval of two UN resolutions—one renewing the GGE, sponsored by the United States, and another establishing the OEWG, sponsored by Russia—led to fears of having two separate talk shops, with opposing views of how to achieve peace and security in cyberspace. Those fears were quickly dismissed by the time both processes began. But the uncertainty of the mandate and the complementarity of the two processes remained a considerable challenge for states.

The OEWG introduced two key changes to the discussions on responsible state behaviour and stability in cyberspace: first, in contrast to the 25 select members of the GGE, the OEWG was open to all 193 UN member states; second and most importantly, the resolution stressed that, if necessary, this working group should ‘study the possibility of establishing regular institutional dialogue with broad participation under the auspices of the United Nations’ and ‘provide the possibility of holding, from within voluntary contributions, intersessional consultative meetings with the interested parties, namely business, non-governmental organizations and academia, to share views on the issues within the group’s mandate’ (UN General Assembly 2018b). Framed as a *possibility*, the resolution did not formally confirm the meeting, nor did it specify the model or the format that those meetings might take. However, it did introduce an important element of ‘regular institutional dialogue’ that has been a key aspect of enhancing participation in cybersecurity discussions at the UN—and that will be further explored in the next section of the chapter. What is more, by taking such actions the resolution represented an unprecedented step in cybersecurity discussions in multilateral bodies; more open, but still a fundamental state domain.

#### Letting the ‘Stakeholders’ In

When the OEWG met for the first time in New York (September 2019), the OEWG Chair, Swiss Ambassador Jürg Lauber, had already proposed to the members that the ‘broad participation’ referred to in the resolution should include creating an intersessional meeting with multiple stakeholders, allowing CSOs and other actors to contribute to the discussion through public statements. As made public by Ambassador Lauber, he would have liked to have seen an even greater role for civil society, but those intentions were curtailed by the opposition of some states. The OEWG was to remain an inter-governmental process. Prior to the first substantive session of the OEWG, the GGE chair, Ambassador Guilherme Patriota, had organised regional consultative sessions with member states; these sessions also included OEWG chair Ambassador Lauber (although he was just observing). At the Organization of American States (OAS) GGE Regional Consultation session, the EU Cyber Direct think-tank organised a pre-event in Washington, DC on 14 August 2019 that also included a discussion on civil society’s role in international cyber norms debates, with a focus on the OEWG. The side event counted with the presence of GGE representatives, diplomats from OAS member states, the private sector and other CSOs. A similar event had taken place in Brussels in June 2019, ahead of the EU GGE Regional Consultation. However, as much as these events are important spaces of interaction and discussion between state and non-state actors, it remains challenging to determine how much of these informal discussions could be incorporated as a best practice in enhancing stakeholder dialogue in the formal OEWG process.

In the initial declarations of the first OEWG substantial session in September 2019, one state after another referred to their aims and priorities and most welcomed the chair, in a clear indication that the decision to open the debate had not tainted his position. Not many, however, acknowledged or welcomed the role of civil society. Out of the twenty-five states that published their initial statements, eleven mentioned civil society. Most of those that did were states traditionally in favour of multistakeholderism as a form of cyberspace governance.^([3](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.003)) Many of those that did not were keen on highlighting the importance of sovereignty in ICT issues. Iran, China, and Russia were among those. Sovereignty was mentioned in relation to the principle of non-interference on domestic affairs, but also in terms of sovereign equality among states. This was an idea particularly highlighted by the Indonesian delegation statement. Such differences were neither surprising nor new. Broadly speaking, some states have been very careful about the involvement of CSOs and other actors in UN activities. Cuba and China, for instance, sit on the NGO Committee to ensure that CSOs with a track record of raising issues contrary to their interests and policies are not granted ECOSOC status (Martens 2011, 48). In some cases such opposition goes right to the core of inter-state rivalry, with CSOs getting dragged in disputes that are not necessarily linked to their activity, as was the case of the Chinese objection to the participation of Taiwanese organisations at the 2003 Asia-Pacific World Summit on the Information Society Regional Conference in Tokyo (Selian 2004, 207).

Some of those barriers were all too visible even before the proceedings began, with only ECOSOC accredited organisations being allowed to participate in the OEWG sessions. Although this is considered the main format through which NGOs and CSOs can participate in UN processes, as we have previously highlighted, it is not the only one. In the case of the OEWG, the decision to restrict participation to the organisations with that particular status was imposed by *some* states—as others were open to extend the participation to organisations beyond those that were ECOSOC accredited. Criteria such as those set forth by the ECOSOC accreditation mechanism both enables and constrains CSO participation. While it provides an institutionalised mechanism through which CSO representation is possible, the OEWG experience shows that it can also become a tool for governments to restrict participation. In so doing, the articulation of these mechanisms as a political tool for shaping representation and participation raises the question of what kinds of CSOs are resourced to and institutionally equipped to engage in these processes—and from which regions they are coming from. It also sheds light on how institutional mechanisms can enhance the unevenness and asymmetries among CSOs and, therefore, on *who* can have access to the room.

Contributions from CSOs to the OEWG were to take at least three forms: (1) participating in the meetings, (2) providing written input and (3) convening with government representatives on the sidelines of consultative processes. Having started with the expectation of inclusivity and some form of multistakeholder participation, the OEWG was doubly tasked with developing its own agenda in consonance with the already existing work of the GGE, and also with providing a format through which other sectors would be able to contribute. A major challenge was that of *participation*. At the start of the OEWG, both ECOSOC- and non-ECOSOC-accredited NGOs were allowed to register for the meetings; however, with the objection of some countries, the registration process was later restricted to ECOSOC-accredited NGOs, therefore limiting by default the kinds of civil societies *qualified* to be ‘in the room’ for the substantive sessions. This partly explains the different levels of participation between the substantive sessions^([4](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.004)) and the intersessional.^([5](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.005)) In addition, formal participation in the meetings was restricted to ‘multistake-holder’ segments in the substantive session agenda. However, the physical presence of some of these civil society groups enabled ‘participation through other means’; in other words, the coffee breaks at the Vienna Café (inside the UN headquarters), side meetings and informal chats between sessions.

Well-organised, well-funded institutions, mostly located in the West, have a clear advantage over other groups constituted outside the Global North (Baillie Smith and Jenkins 2011, 168). Organisations involved in global governance practices need to have staff that are fluent in English, and well acquainted with the bureaucratic minutiae of the organisations involved in a specific issue area, be it the environment, human rights, or technology. These organisations also must possess the funding to continuously engage and advocate in these spaces. The constant search for funding makes civil society ‘vulnerable to external political and corporate influences’ (Selian 2004, 207), which raises important issues in terms of transparency and accountability (Scholte 2011a) but also in terms of effectiveness, as some of their initiatives are more about promoting an appealing image (for funders) than to deliver results (Selian 2004, 210). This was no different in the context of the OEWG. Most of the NGOs attending the substantive sessions were either international in character or based in the Global North. One CSO stressed that ‘meaningful engagement’ should not only include non-ECOSOC-accredited NGOs, but that financial resources were essential ‘for civil society groups and stakeholders from the Global South’ (Global Partners Digital 2020).

As previously mentioned, hybridity also refers to the cohesiveness of civil society as a collective agency and a self-explanatory category in international dialogues. The former can usually be identified in advocacy strategies among different civil society groups to develop and identify common positions around a particular subject. This collective agency was supported by intra-civil society activities, such as coordination calls and face-to-face meetings prior to the substantive and intersessional meetings of the OEWG. The articulation of civil society as a self-explanatory category refers to a homogenising effect that results from a narrow understanding of participation through institutional mechanisms such as ECOSOC accreditation. These mechanisms highlight at least two dynamics: one of placing the understanding of civil society, business and academia under one broader umbrella concept of NGOs; and another of glossing over the contextual differences among CSOs themselves, leaving the criteria for participation agnostic to geographical and economic challenges that are intrinsic to the uneven dynamics within civil society.

#### Letting the ‘Stakeholders’ Speak

In addition to written statements and meetings on the sidelines of the plenary sessions, civil society (together with the other stakeholders) were given the opportunity to sit at the table and express their views in a one-off intersessional meeting. The Informal intersessional consultative meeting of the OEWG with industry, non-governmental organizations and academia took place across three days, between 2–4 December 2019, in New York City. The meeting was announced as ‘informal’ and ‘consultative’, in a clear indication that what was being discussed there would not necessarily feed into the OEWG final report.

The intersessional was the first multistakeholder meeting on cybersecurity in the context of international peace and security of ICTs discussions at the UN. Altogether, the meeting included 100 states and 114 organisations from civil society, the private sector and academia. Each day was divided into morning and an afternoon sessions of three hours each. The first session was dedicated to mapping the cyber threat landscape, the second and third to rules, laws and norms, the fourth and fifth to confidence-building measures (the first one on state-to-state and state-to-private-sector confidence-building measures and the second one on capacity-building), and the final session was dedicated to looking for ‘ways forward on a multistakeholder approach’.

When it comes to the documents submitted to the intersessional, only two states did so—Japan and Iran—together with twenty-six non-state organisations, seventeen of which directly highlighted the importance of civil society in these discussions. From the two states, only Iran referred directly to civil society. The Iranian delegate’s intervention revealed his disappointment with the lack of consultation with states in terms of defining the questions discussed in the session. Unsurprisingly, it highlighted that ‘states have the exclusive responsibility of providing security in all aspect areas’, further indicating the responsibility of the other stakeholders in ensuring ‘responsible behaviour’. The most interesting aspect of the statement, however, was the criticism directed at ‘some of the participants’ for aiming to ‘present the status quo as an ideal situation’, a result of the lack of regional diversity in the meeting: ‘\[w\]e can imagine that if NGOs and business stakeholders from developing countries could participate easily in this meeting, a real picture could be seen in terms of divergent views on different aspects of the issue under discussion.’ Japan’s intervention, on the other hand, did not add much to the discussion. It started by congratulating the host of the session, David Koh, Chief Executive of Singapore’s Cyber Security Agency, for holding a highly ‘inclusive and global multistakeholder meeting’, and welcomed the ‘efforts of multistakeholders to propose and commit themselves to rules, norms and principles, such as Paris Call, the GCSC report \[Advancing Cyberstability\] and other initiatives’^([6](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.006)) in what was a less state-centric view of cyberspace.

The meeting itself started with a mix of excitement and caution, visible in the layout of the room: on one side of the room were the member states, on the other, everyone else—or almost. As has happened in other meetings of this nature, states sponsor non-state actors to participate from their own seat.^([7](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.007)) This was the case with the Cybersecurity Experts Association of Nigeria, which participated in the first session from the Nigerian seat rather than from the stakeholder side of the room—a good example of hybridity. The initial interventions by the UN Under-Secretary-General of Disarmament Affairs (Izumi Nakamitsu), the OEWG Chair (Ambassador Jürg Lauber) and the intersessional Chair (David Koh) contrasted this optimism with the need for a certain distancing. Nakamitsu acknowledged that although ‘we shall share the same ultimate goal of a more stable and secure cyberspace’, it is the case that ‘our approaches differ slightly’. Ambassador Lauber was firmer in his words—also due to the nature of his position as a chair—clearly separating the meeting from the OEWG’s usual business: ‘I said this before the Open-Ended Working Group is an intergovernmental process. I’m very well aware of that and ultimately the member states will decide the outcome of the process the product of the Open-Ended Working Group.’ He explained the need for the intersessional meeting to have a separate chair, and symbolically left the chair seat to give it to David Koh, but not before adding: ‘We chose the solution of having a special chair for the intersessionals to clearly also mark that this is part of the whole process but it’s a bit separate. So just to visualize this . . . I’m going to switch chairs to make it even more clear for everybody’. Beyond the symbolism of this act, the first session—dedicated to mapping the threat landscape—was dominated by the participation of non-state actors, with very few states participating in the debate, something that was acknowledged by the chair himself: ‘States by and large have been very well behaved and not queuing up for the microphone’. States would be far more active in subsequent sessions, but the general picture was one of diverse views but limited consensus on what to do regarding the issues under discussion. Most called for a human-centric approach to cyberspace, and for greater transparency and inclusivity.

The final session was closed by OEWG Chair Jürg Lauber, who reiterated the inter-state nature of the OEWG discussions and the narrow scope of its agenda—cybersecurity—while acknowledging the need for openness and inclusivity of the discussions (Gavrilović 2019). As written in the subsequent Chair’s Summary, ‘Stakeholders expressed the hope that “Multistakeholderism” would not be a ticking of a participation box, but that all stakeholders would have a role in actively shaping policy and decision-making’ (Koh 2019).

### Conclusion

The OEWG that started with resolution 73/27 has now concluded, with the successful approval of the its final report in March 2021. Civil society was invited to contribute to the multiple drafts of the preliminary report and it participated in an informal multistakeholder dialogue organised by a number of state and non-state partners^([8](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.008)) between December 2020 and February 2021—‘Let’s Talk Cyber’. All their contributions at every stage of the process were made available on the UN web page dedicated to the process^([9](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.009)) and the final report makes multiple references to the participation of ‘other relevant stakeholders’.^([10](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11.ftn.010))

More prominently, a new resolution (A/RES/75/240) submitted by Russia and a group of other countries in December 2020 has led to the renewal of the OEWG, which already had its first meeting in June 2021. Incidentally, the resolution argues that ‘effective international cooperation would benefit from identifying mechanisms for the participation, as appropriate, of the private sector, academia and civil society organizations’ (UN General Assembly 2020, 10). Once again, and despite the experience of the first OEWG, the details of that participation were not identified.

Although these are crucial issues for the success of the working group and the potential influential role played by civil society in shaping the debate, the focus of the chapter was on the meaning of bringing civil society to the table in these discussions. Our argument is that such invitation can only be understood within a context of hybridity. Civil society hybridity *encompasses* as well as sheds light on the uneven, contested, and diverse agencies within and among CSOs: who can be in the room, what are the possibilities of action, and *who is represented*.

The division of the room between states and ‘the rest’ symbolises the dual process of separation between the two, in which the latter are removed from any ambition of participating in the decision-making process. This is particularly curious for ICTs, since civil society actors often play a leading role in internet governance. From a purely practical point of view, it is hard to see how decisions taken by states can work independently of the active involvement of these other organisations. That is the main message. The second message is that multistakeholderism is interesting for its hybridity. State representatives can talk about diversity and the excitement of having many different voices around the table, but they sit as a single group on one of the sides. For a world that is constructed as state-led, it is more important to keep Russia and Vanuatu on the same side, than it is to separate a multinational giant such as Microsoft from a small CSO. This social construction is ultimately about reinforcing state versus non-state narratives, for which civil society is often what companies, academia and states are not. It is a concept that fills a gap and enhances the legitimacy of the process (Amoore and Langley 2004, 97), for civil society is about representativeness and plurality, even if it is not. It is about a collective that represents an undefined group that is important to interact with and give legitimacy to the process; but not sufficiently legitimate to sit at the main table or on the same side of the room, or to make any decisions.

As the chapter sought to highlight, the possibility of action, advocacy, agency and influence of civil society groups is shaped and signified by their interactions with states, especially in the context of the processes within the First Committee of the UN (as it is with the case of the OEWG). What is more, the OEWG showed how civil society is also caught in the crossfire of geopolitical tension—itself being an object of dispute among states.

However, as shown by the civil society delegate speaking from the Nigerian seat, there is a hybridity that comes from inside the state, that destabilizes the fixed arrangement that the divided room and the swapping of chairs wanted to portray. In that regard, hybridity not only helps us destabilize the meaning of civil society, but also that of the state, putting into question the artificial construction of barriers between states and the rest; between the more legitimate and the less legitimate. In short, it opens the door for a re-evaluation of who, how and what should be deciding the future of international cybersecurity.

[1](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11r.ftn.001). We follow Raymond and DeNardis (2015, 573) in defining multistakeholderism as ‘two or more classes of actors engaged in a common governance enterprise concerning issues they regard as public in nature, and characterized by polyarchic authority relations constituted by procedural rules’.

[2](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11r.ftn.002). Global Commission on the Stability of Cyberspace: [https://cyberstability.org/](https://cyberstability.org/).

[3](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11r.ftn.003). It is worth mentioning that in the analysis of those initial declarations, only Sweden and Poland mentioned civil society more than once, and only Sweden had a stand-alone reference to civil society (where it highlights the need for governments to recognize the civil society expertise). In all other cases, civil society is always referenced in conjunction with other non-state actors, with the reference to academia and the private sector a common staple across all statements.

[4](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11r.ftn.004). For the first substantive session, only seven ECOSOC-accredited NGOs registered for the meeting. The second substantive session, held in February 2020, included fourteen registered ECOSOC-accredited NGOs (with greater presence of private sector and think tanks, if compared to the first meeting).

[5](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11r.ftn.005). In total, 111 accredited and non-accredited ECOSOC NGOs were registered to the meeting with wide regional and stakeholder representation.

[6](#Chapter11.xhtml#ch11r.ftn.006). Unless stated otherwise, all the information gathered from the intersessionals was taken from videos available at UN Web TV: [http://webtv.un.org/](http://webtv.un.org/).

# [About the Editors and Contributors](../Text/nav.xhtml#link2)

**Cedric Amon** is a research associate for Trade and Digital Affairs at the Multilateral Dialogue Geneva office of the Konrad Adenauer Foundation. He holds a master’s degree in international affairs from the Graduate Institute of International and Development Studies (IHEID) in Geneva, and a bachelor’s degree in international relations and international law from the University of Quebec in Montreal (UQAM). His main research interests include the effects of digital sovereignty on the decentralized internet governance model, internet fragmentation and the geopolitics of digital standards.

**André Barrinha** is a senior lecturer in international relations at the University of Bath. His work has been published in journals such as *International Affairs*, *Mediterranean Politics*, *Third World Quarterly*, *Journal of Common Market Studies*, *European Security*, and *Journal of European Integration*. He is also one the authors of *International Relations Now and Then* (Routledge, 2nd ed.). Dr Barrinha is currently working on cyber-diplomacy as an emerging field in international relations. In 2019, he was awarded the Best Article in Global Affairs Award for a co-authored piece with Thomas Renard on cyber diplomacy and the English School. He is the current chair of the International Studies Association’s Science, Technology and Art in International Relations (STAIR) section.

**Arindrajit Basu** is a PhD candidate at Leiden University’s Faculty of Governance and Global Affairs and non-resident fellow at the Centre for Internet&Society India.

**Bibi van den Berg** is full professor of cybersecurity governance at Leiden University, and the head of the Cybersecurity Governance Research Group at the Institute of Security and Global Affairs of this university. She holds an MA and PhD in philosophy, both from Erasmus University in Rotterdam. Her research and teaching focus on several themes: cybersecurity governance, governance of security and safety, and regulating human behaviour through the use of technologies (techno-regulation and nudging).

**Fabio Cristiano** is assistant professor in conflict studies at Utrecht University, where he also coordinates the Minor in Conflict Studies. He is an associate fellow of The Hague Program on International Cyber Security and holds a PhD in Political Science from Lund University. He has recently co-edited the volume *Artificial Intelligence and International Conflict in Cyberspace* (2023, Routledge).

**Giovanni De Gregorio** is the PLMJ chair in law and technology at Católica Global School of Law, Lisbon and academic fellow at Bocconi University. Prior to joining Católica, Giovanni was a postdoctoral researcher working with the Programme in Comparative Media Law and Policy (PCMLP) at the Centre for Socio-Legal Studies, University of Oxford. His research interest deals with constitutional law, human rights, freedom of expression, privacy and data protection law. Giovanni is the author of the monograph *Digital Constitutionalism in Europe. Reframing Rights and Powers in the Algorithmic Society* (Cambridge University Press 2022).

**François Delerue** is an assistant professor of law at IE University and a member of the Jean Monnet Centre of Excellence for Law and Automation (Lawtomation). He is also an associate fellow of The Hague Program on International Cyber Security and the GEODE Center (Paris 8 University). His book *Cyber Operations and International Law* was published in 2020 and was awarded the 2021 Book Prize of the European Society for International Law.

**Paul Ducheine** is professor for cyber operations and cyber security at the Netherlands Defence Academy, and a legal advisor (Netherlands Army Legal Service). At the University of Amsterdam, he was named professor by special appointment of Law of Military Cyber Operations. His research revolves around the legitimacy of military operations in cyberspace.

**Amy Ertan** is a cyber and hybrid policy officer at NATO Headquarters where she supports the development of cyber policies and initiatives across the Alliance. Amy holds a PhD in Information Security from Royal Holloway University of London, where her doctoral research focused on the security implications of artificial intelligence in military contexts. Previously, Amy was a cyber strategy researcher at the NATO Cooperative Cyber Defence Centre of Excellence and a cybersecurity fellow at Harvard’s Belfer Center for Science and International Affairs. Her broader research interests in cyber strategy and emerging security challenges have led to publications on topics including military exercising, offensive cyber operations, and human aspects of cyber security.

**Josh Gold** is a security and intelligence analyst in the Government of Canada. He contributed to this chapter prior to joining the Government of Canada, as a research assistant at the Citizen Lab at the University of Toronto.

**Taylor Grossman** is a senior researcher in the Cyberdefence Project with the Risk and Resilience Team at the Center for Security Studies (CSS) at ETH Zurich. Her research interests include cyber norm development, ethics of war, and bureaucratic politics in national security decision-making. She holds an MPhil in international relations from the University of Oxford and a BA in political science from Stanford University. She is also a nonresident scholar with the Technology and International Affairs Program at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace.

**Louise Marie Hurel** is a PhD researcher at the London School of Economics’ Department of Media and Communications. Her work focuses on security expertise, social imaginaries, incident response, cyber norms, and non-state actors’ involvement in cybersecurity. Louise Marie is also a cyber research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute (RUSI). Her papers have been published in the *Journal of Cyber Policy* and *International Journal of Communication*, among others. Her latest publications include ‘Cyber-Norms entrepreneurship? Understanding Microsoft’s advocacy on cybersecurity’ and ‘Interrogating the cybersecurity development agenda: A critical reflection of cyber capacities and its consequences’.

**Justin Lau** is a researcher at the Citizen Lab, based at the University of Toronto’s Munk School of Global Affairs and Public Policy, and a graduate of the Munk School’s Master of Global Affairs Program.

**Jan Martin Lemnitzer** is assistant professor at the Department of Digitalization, Copenhagen Business School. He completed his PhD at the London School of Economics and Political Science and was previously director of studies at Oxford University’s Changing Character of War programme. Coming from a background in international politics and history, his research explores cyber norms and cybersecurity in international politics. More recently he has been focusing on business cybersecurity, cyber insurance and cybersecurity regulation.

**Karthik Nachiappan** is a research fellow at the National University of Singapore and author of *Does India Negotiate?*.

**Peter Pijpers** is an Army Colonel (GS). He is associate professor of cyber operations at the Netherlands Defence Academy and researcher at the Amsterdam Centre for International Law (ACIL), University of Amsterdam. The main focus of his research is related to the legal and cognitive dimension of influence operations in cyberspace, below the threshold of the use of force.

**Irene Poetranto** is a senior researcher at the Citizen Lab, based at the University of Toronto’s Munk School of Global Affairs and Public Policy, and a PhD candidate in the Department of Political Science, University of Toronto, where she studies the politics of internet regulation in Southeast Asia.

**Roxana Radu** is associate professor of digital technologies and public policy at the Blavatnik School of Government, University of Oxford. Her research focuses on the governance of technology and internet-related policymaking. She is the author of the monograph *Negotiating Internet Governance* (Oxford University Press, 2019), inspired by her work with the diplomatic community in Geneva, Switzerland. She often advises governments and international organisations on digital governance issues and serves on the Advisory Group of the EU Cybersecurity Agency. Since January 2023, she is the elected Chair of the Global Internet Governance Academic Network (GigaNet).
